# Spiritual Ethos

## Introduction to the Book

This is a work of scholarship, and at its close a work of translation. Reza Shah-Kazemi published it in 2006, reading the Shii and Sufi traditions of Islam together and drawing on sources that reach back to the seventh century. Its subject is the spirituality of Ali ibn Abi Talib — cousin and son-in-law of the Prophet Muhammad, fourth caliph, and first of the Shii Imams — and its argument is that in this one figure the inward substance of the Islamic tradition finds an integral expression.

The book moves inward. It opens with Ali's nearness to the Prophet and the household around him, then turns to what he understood by intellect as distinct from mere reason, and to the certainty that intellect yields. From there it follows his conception of justice — sacred rather than merely legal — through the ordering of society, the claims of the poor, and the sincerity that must underlie every act of rule. Its final movement is devotional: prayer, the remembrance of God, and the extinction of the self in the divine Name.

The closing chapters set commentary aside and let Ali speak for himself. The first sermon of the Nahj al-Balagha and his letter to the governor Malik al-Ashtar are given in full translation, so that the reader may weigh the man against his own words.

## 1. In the Prophet's Shadow

In relation to the Prophet, Ali (عليه السلام) was both a living presence and a promise. The epigraph names it: "I am the city of knowledge and Ali (ع) is its gate; so whoever desires knowledge, let him enter the gate" (Ana madinatul-ilm wa 'Ali babuha; Fa-man aradal-ilm fal-yatil-bab).

To speak of Ali ibn Abi Talib (ع) — cousin and son-in-law of the Prophet Muhammad, fourth caliph of Islam, and first in the line of Shii Imams — is to speak of the very heart of Islamic spirituality. This founding figure of early Islam gives full expression to the two fundamental sources of that spirituality: the revelation of the Quran and the inspired Sunna of the Prophet. Sunna here means more than the outward copying of the Prophet's actions, a narrowing all too common in these times; it means the spiritual substance of prophetic perfection, to which the Quran itself points:

> وَإِنَّكَ لَعَلَىٰ خُلُقٍ عَظِيمٍۢ

"Verily, thou art of a tremendous nature" (Al-Qalam: 4).

The Prophet's words and deeds express that spiritual substance, yet do not exhaust it. To take this substance inwardly into oneself, rather than merely copy its outward forms, is the goal of every Muslim drawn to the spiritual life. Between the believer's soul and the prophetic nature there is a deep kinship, a mysterious nearness that reaches across time and space, as the verse expresses:

> ٱلنَّبِىُّ أَوْلَىٰ بِٱلْمُؤْمِنِينَ مِنْ أَنفُسِهِمْ ۖ وَأَزْوَجُهُۥٓ أُمَّهَتُهُمْ ۗ وَأُو۟لُوا۟ ٱلْأَرْحَامِ بَعْضُهُمْ أَوْلَىٰ بِبَعْضٍۢ فِى كِتَبِ ٱللَّهِ مِنَ ٱلْمُؤْمِنِينَ وَٱلْمُهَجِرِينَ إِلَّآ أَن تَفْعَلُوٓا۟ إِلَىٰٓ أَوْلِيَآئِكُم مَّعْرُوفًۭا ۚ كَانَ ذَلِكَ فِى ٱلْكِتَبِ مَسْطُورًۭا

"The Prophet is closer to the believers than they are to themselves, and his wives are their mothers; and by the ordinance of God, blood relatives are more entitled to one another than other believers and emigrants, unless you would show kindness to your close associates. This is decreed in the Record" (Al-Ahzab: 6).

The Prophet thus shows outwardly the perfection that the spiritually sensitive believer senses, in some measure, as the very essence of his or her own soul.

It is no surprise, then, that the Prophet said of Ali (ع) that he "is as my own soul (ka-nafsi)":

> عليٌّ مِنِّي بِمَنْزِلَةِ نَفْسِي

This affirms not only the extraordinarily close bond between Ali (ع) and the Prophet, but also the kinship — and in the end the oneness — between every sanctified soul and the prophetic nature. More mysteriously still, the Prophet said to Ali (ع), "You are from me, and I am from you (anta minni wa ana minka)":

> أنْتَ مِنِّی وَ أَنَا مِنْکَ

As for how Ali (ع) took into himself the substance (مَوَادّ) of the Quran, there is this saying of the Prophet:

> إِنَّ عَلِيًّا مَعَ الْقُرْآنِ وَالْقُرْآنُ مَعَ عَلِيٍّ، لَنْ يَفْتَرِقَا حَتَّى يَرِدَا عَلَيَّ الْحَوْضَ
>
> "Ali is with the Quran and the Quran is with Ali (ع). They will not separate from each other until they return to me at the [paradisal] pool (al-hawd)."

The spirit of the Quran and the soul of the Prophet were thus made fully Ali (ع)'s own, and it is precisely this — the drawing of the twin sources of the Islamic revelation wholly into oneself — that forms the spiritual path of Islam.

The chief way to study and reflect upon the spirituality of Ali (ع) is to work through the body of teachings handed down under his name. Not every one of the thousands of sayings that bear his name can be assigned to him with confidence; yet the sheer size of the collection is itself evidence that he must have given voice to a very large number of profound teachings. No other companion of the Prophet left anything close to this body of work. So too with the sciences: his founding role in their development may be open to debate — sciences such as jurisprudence (fiqh), theology (kalam), Quranic exegesis (tafsir), rhetoric (balagha), grammar (nahw), and calligraphy (khatt), the mystical knowledge bound up with Sufism, and such hidden sciences as numerology (jafr) and alchemy (al-kimiya) — but that later masters in every one of these fields look back to him as the first impulse behind their discipline shows how far and how deep the influence of both his formal teachings and his personal radiance reached.

Because the Islamic intellectual sciences as a whole are so closely tied to Ali (ع), the Prophet's description of him as the "gate" to prophetic wisdom, quoted in the epigraph, reads two ways at once: as an account of what Ali (ع) was to the Prophet in their own lifetime, and as a prophecy of the part he would play as the tradition's sciences later unfolded. All these sciences are understood as so many outward, formal expressions of the inward, essential spirit of the Islamic revelation — a spirit that Ali (ع) gathered up whole and faithfully passed on. Before going further, the main outlines of his life call for a sketch.

Ali (ع) was a "follower" of the Prophet in the deepest sense of the word. He lived, in body, in the Prophet's shadow, and took in, in spirit, everything that shone from him. The closeness of their bond is caught in the words of one of the Imam's own sermons:

> لَمَّا كُنْتُ وَلِيدًا ضَمَّنِي إِلَى صَدْرِهِ، فَكُنْتُ أتَّبِعُهُ اتّبَاعَ الفَصِيلِ أُمَّهُ
>
> "When I was but a child he took me under his wing. I would follow him as a baby camel follows its mother."

> يَرْفَعُ لِي فِي كُلِّ يَوْمٍ عَلَمًا مِنْ أَخْلاقِهِ الْكَرِيمَةِ وَيَأمُرُنِي بِالاِقْتِدَاءِ بِهِ
>
> "Every day he would hold up for me a sign of his noble character and command me to follow it."

> وَكَانَ يَتَعَاهَدُ غَارَ حِرَاءٍ فِي كُلِّ سَنَةٍ، فَأَرَاهُ وَلَا يَرَاهُ غَيْرِي
>
> "Each year he would go into seclusion at the mountain of Hira. I saw him there, and no one else did."

> وَلَمْ يَجْمَعْ بَيْتٌ وَاحِدٌ يَوْمَئِذٍ فِي الإِسْلامِ غَيْرَ رَسُولِ اللهِ صَلَّى اللهُ عَلَيْهِ وَسَلَّمَ وَخَدِيجَةَ وَأَنَا ثَالِثُهُمَا
>
> "At that time no single household was gathered together in the religion of Islam, save that of the Messenger of God, Khadija, and myself as the third among them."

> أَرَى نُورَ الْوَحْيِ وَالرِّسَالَةِ، وَأَشُمُّ رِيحَ النُّبُوَّةِ
>
> "I saw the light of the revelation and the message, and I smelt the fragrance of prophecy..."

It is Ali (ع) as the faithful follower and transmitter of the prophetic pattern that this short life seeks to address. The focus here falls on the early years, from his birth (c. 599 CE) to the death of the Prophet (11/632). The second period — from the Prophet's death to the time Ali (ع) himself became caliph (35/656) — and the third — his brief caliphate, overtaken by tragic civil wars and ending in his assassination in 40/661 — lie outside this account. The questions those periods raise are tangled with historical and scholarly difficulty, and to enter such controversies would pull away from the chief aim of this book: to introduce the spiritual ethos of this extraordinary figure in a way that shows its relevance to everyone, both within Islam and beyond it. For this ethos rises above formal religious boundaries just as it rises above the political disputes on which the outward Shii-Sunni divide rests — disputes that serve only to draw attention away from the essential spiritual message of Ali (ع).

What this book brings forward instead is the uniting power of his spiritual teachings — teachings whose great importance and depth all Muslims, whatever their school of thought, affirm. What follows is a brief survey of the earlier period of Ali (ع)'s life, the years spent with the Prophet, together with the ways the Prophet described him in well-attested sayings; this is the indispensable background against which his role as gate to prophetic wisdom must be understood. The second part of this opening chapter then takes up some key principles of his spiritual ethos, with particular weight given to the place of the intellect within it.

According to all Shii authorities and several Sunni sources, Ali (ع) had the singular distinction of being born inside the Kaba in Mecca. His mother was Fatima bt. Asad; his father, Abu Talib, son of 'Abd al-Muttalib, was a leading member of the Hashimite clan. Abu Talib had raised the young orphan Muhammad, son of his brother Abd Allah, and would later become his chief protector when Islam was preached openly in Mecca. Some years before the mission began, in a time of drought, Muhammad lightened Abu Talib's burden by taking his young son Ali (ع), then about five years old, into his own household. From that time on, Ali (ع) was like a son to him, and the two were seldom apart.

Ali (ع) is regarded as the first male to embrace Islam, though he was only nine or ten years old at the time. Abu Bakr was the first grown man to enter the new religion, and Khadija, the Prophet's wife, was the first woman. That the young boy answered the prophetic message at once, and held to it without wavering ever after, is of great significance. About three years later, when the Prophet was commanded by revelation to "warn thy nearest kin":

> وَأَنذِرْ عَشِيرَتَكَ ٱلْأَقْرَبِينَ

(Ash-Shu'ara: 214), he invited the leading members of his clan to a feast and then addressed them: "O sons of 'Abd al-Muttalib, I know of no Arab who hath come to his people with a nobler message than mine. I bring you the best of this world and the next. God hath commanded me to call you unto Him. Which of you, then, will help me in this, and be my brother, mine executor and my successor among you?" All remained silent except the youthful Ali (ع), who spoke up: "O Prophet of God, I will be thy helper in this." The Prophet then laid his hand on Ali (ع)s neck and said, "This is my brother, mine executor and my successor among you. Hearken unto him and obey him." The clan's leaders met this with mockery at the time, yet the declaration was taken far more seriously as the years passed and the extraordinary character, nobility, and intelligence of the Prophet's young cousin grew ever more dazzlingly plain.

Also important in this early period is the part Ali (ع) played in the migration (hijra) from Mecca to Medina (622 CE), the event that marks the start of the Muslim calendar. The Prophet's enemies had plotted to kill him; knowing this, Ali (ع) risked his own life by taking the Prophet's place and sleeping in his bed on the night of his departure. Once in Medina, the Prophet took Ali (ع) as his "brother" in the pact of brotherhood he made between the "emigrants" (al-muhajirun), the Muslims who had come with him on the hijra from Mecca, and the "helpers" (al-ansar), the Medinan tribes who had embraced the new faith.

During the Medinan period, Ali (ع) distinguished himself on the battlefield, as a scribe of the Quranic revelation as it came down, and as a leading companion of the Prophet. His courage and heroism in battles against the overwhelmingly larger forces of the Meccan Quraysh and their allies were second to none. He stood at the front of nearly all the major engagements fought under the Prophet's banner and was often appointed standard-bearer. He was never beaten in any of the single-combat duels with which battles customarily opened; indeed, he was never beaten in any battle he fought at all. Both his unbreakable courage and his unmatched skill as a warrior became legendary. It is said that during the battle of Uhud a heavenly voice was heard proclaiming, "There is no chivalric knight but Ali (ع), no sword but Dhul-Faqar (la fata illa 'Ali, la sayf illa dhul-faqar)," this last being the name of the sword the Prophet had given him. The most celebrated of all the battles that fixed Ali (ع)s reputation as the foremost — indeed invincible — warrior of Islam was Khaybar, in 7/629. The Muslims had been unable to make any headway against the heavily fortified, seemingly impregnable defenses of their opponents. The Prophet then declared that he would give the army's banner to one who "loves God and His Messenger and is loved by God and His Messenger," and through whom victory would be granted. He sent for Ali (ع), who was absent with an ailment of the eyes. The Prophet healed him by placing saliva upon his eyes, and Ali (ع) went on to lead the Muslims to victory. In the thick of the fighting, having lost his own shield, he is said to have torn a gate from its hinges and used it as a shield — a gate that, after the battle, eight men could barely lift.

<!-- editorial:begin -->
> **Editorial note (tradition-grounded).** 
> When the Prophet calls Ali "as my own soul," the word rendered "soul" is *nafs*, and it helps to
> know its range. A clarification preserved in the wider tradition traces it to a root meaning "to
> breathe": from breath comes *nafs*, the breathing self — the living person one simply is — while
> in later spiritual writing the same word names the lower self or ego whose cravings the seeker
> must master. Hearing both senses keeps the saying from shrinking into a claim about blood
> kinship: it reaches from the very self a person is toward the perfected self this chapter
> describes. A related teaching in this tradition also reads the word *Quran* as carrying the
> sense of "connection" — a fitting background to the report that Ali and the Quran "will not
> separate," each bound to the other until they meet the Prophet at the pool.
<!-- editorial:end -->

## 2. The People of the House and the Prophet's Witness

The People of the House and the Prophet's Witness

Ali (ع)'s role as a warrior is only one of many things that account for his unique place in the Islamic tradition. Far more significant is his closeness to the Prophet in every respect, and the wisdom and sanctity that flowed from that nearness. Ali (ع) received the immense honor of marrying the Prophet's daughter Fatima, who — alongside her mother Khadija, the Prophet's first wife — is held to be the most saintly woman in Islam. One epithet the tradition gives Fatima is Majma' al-Nurayn, 'Confluence of the Two Lights,' the two lights being nubuwwa (prophecy) and walaya (sanctity). After the Prophet himself, then, Ali (ع) stood at the head of what came to be known as the Ahl al-Bayt, 'the People of the House' — that is, the Prophet's family.

The Ahl al-Bayt are addressed in an important Quranic verse as those purified of all defilement:

> وَقَرْنَ فِى بُيُوتِكُنَّ وَلَا تَبَرَّجْنَ تَبَرُّجَ ٱلْجَهِلِيَّةِ ٱلْأُولَىٰۖ وَأَقِمْنَ ٱلصَّلَوٰةَ وَآتِينَ ٱلزَّكَوٰةَ وَأَطِعْنَ ٱللَّهَ وَرَسُولَهُۚۥٓ إِنَّمَا يُرِيدُ ٱللَّهُ لِيُذْهِبَ عَنكُمُ ٱلرِّجْسَ أَهْلَ ٱلْبَيْتِ وَيُطَهِّرَكُمْ تَطْهِيرًۭا

'God only wisheth to remove from you all impurity, O People of the House, and to purify you with a complete purification' (Al-Ahzab: 33).

According to several of the most authoritative early sources, the Prophet called for Fatima, Ali (ع), and their two sons Hasan and Husayn, and declared, 'This is my Ahl al-Bayt.' On one well-attested occasion he gathered these members of his family beneath his cloak and recited the words of this verse. It happened while a Christian delegation from Najran was in religious debate with him, when a revelation came calling for a challenge of mutual imprecation (mubahila) against the Christians. The Prophet brought forward the four named above for this mubahila, but the Christians declined the challenge.

The importance of the Ahl al-Bayt is expressed further in a verse in which the Prophet is instructed:

> ذَلِكَ ٱلَّذِى يُبَشِّرُ ٱللَّهُ عِبَادَهُ ٱلَّذِينَ آمَنُوا۟ وَعَمِلُوا۟ ٱلصَّلِحَتِ ۗ قُل لَّآ أَسْـَٔلُكُمْ عَلَيْهِ أَجْرًا إِلَّا ٱلْمَوَدَّةَ فِى ٱلْقُرْبَىٰ ۗ وَمَن يَقْتَرِفْ حَسَنَةًۭ نَّزِدْ لَهُۥ فِيهَا حُسْنًا ۚ إِنَّ ٱللَّهَ غَفُورٌۭ شَكُورٌ

'Say: I ask you for no reward, save love of the near of kin' (Ash-Shura: 23).

Several of the most significant Quranic commentators identify 'the near of kin' in this verse as the Prophet's Ahl al-Bayt. Love of the Prophet's family is thus explicitly demanded by the revelation — though this does not exclude the verse's wider sense, which takes in love of all those bound to one by close kinship. How deeply the Prophet's family is to be revered by all Muslims is shown by a further practice: blessings are to be invoked upon his al, or progeny, as part of the blessings invoked upon him. The Quran instructs the believers:

> إِنَّ ٱللَّهَ وَمَلَٓئِكَتَهُۥ يُصَلُّونَ عَلَى ٱلنَّبِىِّۚ يَٓأَيُّهَا ٱلَّذِينَ آمَنُوا۟ صَلُّوا۟ عَلَيْهِ وَسَلِّمُوا۟ تَسْلِيمًا

'Truly, God and His angels bless the Prophet. O ye who believe, bless him and greet him with peace' (Al-Ahzab: 56).

When this verse was revealed, the Prophet was asked how the blessing should be performed. He answered that it was to be invoked upon him and his al in these words: 'O God, bless Muhammad and the progeny of Muhammad, as Thou hast blessed Abraham and the progeny of Abraham. Truly, Thou art the Praised, the Glorious ...' This blessing, to be recited during the canonical prayer, together with the obligation to love the Prophet's family, is beautifully expressed in the following lines by Imam al-Shafii, founder of one of the four schools of Sunni jurisprudence:

Loving you, O family of the household of the Prophet of God, is an obligation (fard) from God in the Quran which He revealed; Sufficient to show the grandeur of your dignity is that one who blesses you not, no prayer has he.

There is also a prophetic saying that underscores the spiritual significance of the Ahl al-Bayt:

Truly, I am leaving behind among you the two weighty things (al-thaqalayn): the Book of God and my Ahl al-Bayt. They will not be parted from each other until they return to me at the [paradisal pool] al-hawd.

Beyond these sayings that concern Ali (ع) as the most important member of the Prophet's Ahl al-Bayt, there are sayings of the Prophet about Ali (ع) specifically that deserve to be cited in full; only on the basis of these sayings can one begin to appreciate the immensely important role Ali (ع) plays in the spiritual dimension of the Islamic faith. These traditions (ahadith, sing. hadith) are presented here as a list, with minimal commentary, in the manner they appear in most standard hadith collections. Anyone who takes prophetic guidance seriously cannot but take Ali (ع) seriously, and it is largely for this reason that countless Muslims across the centuries have meditated on these sayings, drawing from them diverse interpretations and incalculable inspiration. In a certain sense, these sayings form the prism through which the tradition views Ali (ع), and they deepen one's spiritual receptivity to his teachings. The crucial importance of Ali (ع) in the spiritual firmament of nascent Islam emerges more clearly in the light of these prophetic descriptions; they should therefore be regarded as a significant aspect of the 'prophetic background' to Ali (ع)'s seminal contribution to Islamic spiritual and intellectual life, whether in a Shii, Sunni, or Sufi context.

The traditions given below can be found in any standard Shii hadith collection. But to show that they — together with their implications and the devotion they have inspired — are not confined to Shii Islam, they are cited here from two of the most authoritative Sunni traditionists. The first is Ahmad al-Nasai, author of the Sunan, one of the six collections deemed 'canonical' in the Sunni corpus (al-kutub al-sitta). The second is al-Hakim al-Nisaburi, author of al-Mustadrak 'alal-sahihayn (Supplement to the Two Sound Collections), which gathers those prophetic traditions that meet the exacting standards of authenticity set by Bukhari and Muslim, authors of the sahihayn, yet were left out of their own collections. The sayings concern Ali (ع)s distinctions, virtues, and merits — all conveyed by the Arabic word fadail (sing. fadila) — and they explain why Ahmad b. Hanbal, another leading Sunni traditionist and founder of one of the four Sunni schools of law, made the following statement: No companion of the Prophet has had such fadail ascribed to him as those which have been ascribed to Ali ibn Abi Talib (ع).'

The sayings are these:

'Truly, Ali (ع) is from me and I am from him (inna Ali minni wa ana minhu), and he is the wali (patron/spiritual master) of every believer after me.'

'Ali (ع) is with the Quran and the Quran is with Ali (ع), إِنَّ عَلِيًّا مَعَ الْقُرْآنِ وَالْقُرْآنُ مَعَ عَلِيٍّ، لَنْ يَفْتَرِقَا حَتَّى يَرِدَا عَلَيَّ الْحَوْضَ. They will not separate from each other until they return to me at the [paradisal pool] (al-hawd).'

'Three things were revealed to me regarding Ali (ع): he is the leader of the Muslims, the guide of the pious, and chief of the radiantly devout (sayyidul-muslimin, imamul-muttaqin, wa qaidul-ghurral-muhajjalin).'

'Gazing upon Ali (ع) is an act of worship (al-nazar ila Ali ibada).'

'May God have mercy on Ali (ع). O God, make the truth revolve around Ali (ع) wherever he turns (adiril-haqq maahu haythu dara).'

'I am the city of knowledge and Ali (ع) is its gate; so whoever desires knowledge, let him enter the gate.'

'O Ali (ع), you are a leader (sayyid) in this world and the Hereafter. Your beloved is my beloved, and my beloved is the beloved of God; your enemy is my enemy, and my enemy is the enemy of God. Woe to those who hate you after me [after I have passed away].'

'Whoever desires to live my life and to die my death and to take his rest in the eternal Garden my Lord has promised me, let him orient himself toward Ali ibn Abi Talib (ع), for truly he will never lead you away from right guidance, nor lead you into error.'

The Prophet said that Ali (ع) was 'as my own soul' (ka-nafsi).

He said to Ali (ع), 'You are from me and I am from you (anta minni wa ana minka), أنْتَ مِنِّی وَ أِنَا مِنْکَ.'

... whoever obeys Ali (ع) obeys me, and whoever disobeys him disobeys me.

'You will clarify for my community that over which they will differ after me (anta tubayyinu li-ummati makhtalafu fihi badi).'

'There is one among you who will fight for the تَأْوِيل [spiritual interpretation] of the Quran as I have fought for its tanzil [literal revelation], عَلِیٌّ یُقَاتِلُ عَلیٰ تَاوِیلِہِ کَما قَاتَلْتُ عَلیٰ تَنزِیلِہِ.' Abu Bakr asked, 'Is it I?' The Prophet said, 'No.' 'Umar asked, Is it I?' The Prophet said, 'No, it is the one who is mending the sandal.' The Prophet had given Ali (ع) his sandal to mend.

'O Ali (ع), there is in you something akin to Jesus, on whom be peace and blessings. The Jews hated him so intensely that they slandered his mother; and the Christians loved him so intensely that they ascribed to him a rank he did not possess.' 'O Ali (ع), whoever separates himself from me separates himself from God, and whoever separates himself from you, O Ali (ع), separates himself from me.'

'Whoever curses Ali (ع) curses me, and whoever curses me curses God.'

The Prophet said to his wife 'Aisha, Call unto me the leader (sayyid) of the Arabs.' She asked, 'O Prophet of God, are you not the leader of the Arabs?' He said, 'I am the leader of the children of Adam, and Ali (ع) is the leader of the Arabs.' When some women asked Fatima why the Prophet had given her in marriage to a man as poor as Ali (ع), she brought the question to the Prophet. He said: 'Are you not pleased that God, exalted and glorified be He, surveyed the people of the world and chose two men from among them: one is your father, and the other your husband?'

'The first of you to reach the [paradisal] pool (al-hawd) is the first of you who entered Islam, Ali ibn Abi Talib (ع).'

'Ali (ع) is from me and I am from him (Ali minni wa ana minhu), and nobody can fulfill my duty but myself and Ali (ع).'

Ali (ع) himself relates that the Prophet told him that none but a believer will love him, and none but a hypocrite will hate him.

When the Prophet was about to set out on the expedition to Tabuk, he left Ali (ع) as his deputy in Medina. Ali (ع) was saddened not to be going with him. The Prophet said, 'Are you not content to stand in relation to me as Aaron stood in relation to Moses, except that there is no prophet after me?'

The Prophet prayed to God to bring 'the most beloved of Thy creatures' to share a meal of fowl with him. Only when Ali (ع) arrived did the Prophet invite him to join him.

Among the several Quranic verses on which the Prophet commented with reference to Ali (ع) is 13:7: 'Verily thou art a warner, and for every people there is a guide.' The Prophet said, 'I am the warner ... you are the guide, O Ali (ع). After me, the rightly-guided shall be guided by you.'

On the revelation of verse 55 of Sura 5 (al-Maida), 'Verily your wali is only God and His Messenger and those who believe, establish the prayer and give alms while bowing in prayer,' the commentators relate this to the occasion when Ali (ع), while bowing in prayer, extended his ring to a beggar who had asked for alms. On being told of the incident, the Prophet recited this verse, then added what are perhaps the most celebrated words ever spoken about Ali (ع): 'For whomever I am the mawla [guardian, master, close friend], Ali (ع) is his mawla (man kuntu mawlahu fa-Ali mawlahu).'

This last saying was uttered on a number of occasions, the most famous being after the Prophet's final pilgrimage to Mecca, in the year 10/632, at a pool midway between Mecca and Medina known as Ghadir Khumm. The Prophet assembled all the pilgrims, had a pulpit erected, and addressed the thousands gathered there. The address culminated in the declaration: 'For whomever I am the mawla, Ali (ع) is his mawla.' For Shiis, this is regarded as an unambiguous designation (nass) of Ali (ع) as successor to the Prophet; for Sunnis it indicates the special closeness of Ali (ع) to the Prophet, but not his nomination as successor in the political domain. That the reference to Ali (ع) as mawla — in some versions as wali — carries the highest spiritual significance is, however, not seriously disputed. 'Umar is reported to have approached Ali (ع) and said, Bakh bakh' — an expression of congratulation — 'you have become my mawla and the mawla of every Muslim.' The polemical debate that has exercised countless minds throughout history, and that continues to this day, turns on the political implications of Ali (ع)'s spiritual authority, his walaya.

Although the place of Ali (ع) within Shii Islam is not the subject here, his charisma was such that, even during the Prophet's own lifetime, a group came to be known as the shia, or 'partisans,' of Ali (ع). Al-Tabari, author of the first great commentary on the Quran, reports that the Prophet interpreted the verse:

> إِنَّ ٱلَّذِينَ آمَنُوا۟ وَعَمِلُوا۟ ٱلصَّلِحَتِ أُو۟لَٓئِكَ هُمْ خَيْرُ ٱلْبَرِيَّةِ

'Those who have faith and do righteous deeds, they are the best of created beings' (Al-Bayyinah: 7), as referring to Ali (ع) and his 'shia.'

Four individuals in particular were renowned for their devotion to Ali (ع) during the Prophet's lifetime and are regarded as the prototypes of early Shiism: Salman al-Farisi, Abu Dharr al-Ghifari, 'Ammar b. Yasir, and Miqdad b. Amr. This early love for Ali (ع) should not be identified with the theological doctrines later elaborated in the Shii branch of Islam in subsequent generations. Yet at the core of Shiism, in all its different expressions, lies fidelity to Ali (ع). This fidelity, loyalty, or love is one aspect of walaya; the other, complementary aspect is the spiritual authority or sanctity of Ali (ع). And neither devotion to Ali (ع) nor the spiritual orientation toward the walaya he embodies belongs to Shiism alone. It extends across the whole of the Islamic faith, for devotion to Ali (ع) as head of the Prophet's Ahl al-Bayt is found throughout the Muslim world, and is especially deep wherever Sufism flourishes. Ali (ع) is regarded as the first spiritual pole' (qutb) of Sufism after the Prophet himself, and he stands at the summit of all the spiritual chains (salasil, sing. silsila) through which the Sufi orders trace their lineage back to the Prophet. To speak of Ali (ع) is to speak not of exclusivist sectarianism but of universal spirituality.

<!-- editorial:begin -->
> **Editorial note (tradition-grounded).** 
> The term walaya, which the chapter pairs with prophecy as one of the "two lights," rewards a
> closer look at its root. In the wider tradition the word draws on a cluster of meanings —
> nearness, friendship, and being in charge — so that it can carry the senses of guardianship,
> authority, and devoted allegiance all at once. That range helps explain why sanctity should be
> named through this particular word alongside the Prophet's family: the same root that speaks of
> spiritual nearness also speaks of the loyalty owed to those held close. Read this way, the
> chapter's stress on Ali's "closeness to the Prophet in every respect" and the "love of the near
> of kin" that the revelation demands are not separate themes but facets of a single idea the word
> itself holds together.
<!-- editorial:end -->

## 3. Intellect, Not Reason

The spiritual ethos of Ali (ع) has an intellectual heart. The intellect (al-aql) plays a central role in shaping and absorbing his spiritual teachings. To sketch the key features of that ethos, and above all its intellectual side, is to give firmer ground to the chapters that follow. One aim here is to bring out the 'spirit of the intellect', اَلرُّوحُ الْعَقْلُ — the term coined for the bond between the intellect and the spirit as it appears in the Imam's worldview. This is a spirit that both contains the work of the rational mind and reaches beyond it, extending into areas no longer linked with the intellect today, such as moral conduct and a feeling for beauty. This spiritual understanding of the intellect is tied directly to the Quranic idea of the spirit (al-ruh), and much of what follows explores what comes of engaging deeply with this unifying spirit that belongs to the intellect.

It is worth explaining at the outset why the English word 'intellect' is preferred over 'reason' as a translation of 'aql. The word is meant to carry the original meaning of intellectus in Latin Christendom, which was almost identical to that of nous in the Greek of the Church Fathers. Intellectus means understanding', and within it lies the sense of standing under a thing. To understand something is to stand beneath a perception of reality and to let that perception overshadow and govern how one acts, since a person always acts in the shadow of how things are perceived. Intellectus, or nous, is therefore the faculty that can see, directly and contemplatively, the higher realities of which the Quran speaks but which lie hidden from the senses. Reason, by contrast — the Latin ratio and the Greek dianoia — is indirect and step-by-step. It works through logic and reaches only mental concepts of those realities. The root of ratio means a relation or comparison between two numbers, and that captures how reason works: by comparison, by a logic of yes and no, weighing one thing against another until it has pinned down what it is examining. Reason, so understood, is the instrument the intellect uses, but it can reach only the outward form of a thing — only the mental concept or image of a reality. With the intellect one can contemplate, or 'see', the Absolute; with reason one can only think about it. To know about a reality is one thing; to contemplate and experience it directly is another. It is the intellect, not reason, that is capable of that experience — even though it is reason that brings the intellect to it.

These opening reflections on the spirit of the intellect in the Imam's ethos turn mainly on a short discourse of the greatest importance in the Nahj al-balagha: the one the Imam gave to one of his closest disciples, Kumayl b. Ziyad. The approach taken is that of an interpretive commentary on the Imam's words. It draws on his other sayings on the theme, on Quranic verses that agree with them, and now and then on traditions of the Prophet that either help clarify the saying at hand or are themselves clarified by it. Before turning to that dialogue, three factors belonging to this intellectual outlook must be stressed: the central place of revelation; the harmony between revelation and the intellect; and the distinction between the intellect, understood as the principle from which consciousness unfolds, and reason, understood as one expression of that principle.

Beginning with the first: everything in the Imam's life and teaching is centered on the Quranic revelation and on its embodiment in the Prophet's conduct. The Quran is the center around which the intellect must turn, for the intellect needs a center — principles and definitions it can hold fast to if it is to be guided rightly. Both the thought and the life of the Imam can be read as a kind of spiritual commentary, a تَأْوِيل, on the sacred text. In matters of doctrine, it can be said with confidence that Ali (ع) had an inborn gift for the work of تَأْوِيل. In his life too — from his birth in the most holy shrine of the Kaba to his martyrdom in the mosque of Kufa — the inner meaning of the revealed text unfolds like a drama. Ali (ع) refused to stop at the surface of things, whether religious, ethical, or political, and in this he showed an unflinching faithfulness to the sacred substance of the religion — a faithfulness that called for inward insight and outward armed resistance alike. The saying of the Prophet cited earlier bears closely on this: Ali (ع) would fight for the تَأْوِيل of the Quran just as he, the Prophet, had fought for its tanzil, عَلِیُّ یُقَاتِلُ عَلیٰ تَاوِیلِہ کَما قَتَلُ عَلیَ تَنزِیلِہِ. Ali (ع)'s short caliphate was dominated by bloody civil wars, and a strong case can be made that all three — the wars of the Camel (al-Jamal), of Nahrawan, and of Siffin — were fought for the sake of interpreting and applying the Quran rightly, for making plain its central message of تَوْحِيد, and for realizing that oneness at every level, spiritual and social alike. These were not political battles or mere power struggles. They were fought to make clear and to uphold the oneness of God according to the principles of the Quran — in thought, in speech, and in action. The Imam's response to a bedouin bears this out vividly. Just as the battle of the Camel (al-Jamal) was about to begin, the man approached him and said, O Commander of the Faithful (یا أمیر المومنین)! Do you say that God is one?' Those present rebuked the bedouin, telling him this was neither the time nor the place for such questions. The Imam, however, intervened: 'Leave him, for surely what the bedouin wishes is what we wish for the people.'

This response bears directly on the principle that right intention comes before outward action, whatever that action may be. Had the Imam been guided by reason alone, he would have sided with those who rebuked the bedouin and sent him off to a fitter time and place. But he saw with the intellect, which stands above reason. For the Imam, the deepest intention behind every action — whether religious in the ordinary sense or military, as here — is knowledge of the true nature of ultimate reality. What the Imam 'wishes for the people', then, is precisely this knowledge, and it must lie at the very heart of every concern, motive, and act, whether on the battlefield or on the prayer-rug. There is therefore no time or place where the question 'What do you mean by saying God is one?' is irrelevant or out of place. It was for the sake of right knowledge of the divine, and all that follows from it in society, that the Imam was fighting, in fulfillment of the prophetic saying just mentioned. On the very field of battle, then, the Imam gives a discourse on the meaning of divine oneness. The opening of his reply holds a key to his whole spiritual ethos. He rejects any notion that God's oneness is a matter of number or counting. He tells the bedouin that one may not say 'one' while holding any idea of number in mind, for 'that which has no second does not enter into the category of numbers'. The moment a thing can be counted as 'one', it at once allows 'two', 'three', 'four'; a oneness taken in this counting sense is undone by the very number meant to affirm it.

'That which has no second' is not merely a God to whom no partner can be assigned, as a theologian might put it. It is that reality which has no 'otherness' at all: the Real, al-Haqq, which is at once beyond the world and present within it, past all 'things' yet shown forth by all 'things', at once out of reach and never to be taken away. To say that God has no otherness is not to say that there exist things which might have been His partners but which believers merely choose not to associate with Him. It is to affirm that nothing whatever in existence bears His likeness — nothing that could ever be set beside Him. In these few words the Imam brings about, for those who have 'ears to hear', a shift of awareness: from the doctrine of one God against many gods to the mystical grasp of the one and only Reality, before which everything else is mere appearance. All things then come to be seen as outward appearances asking to be read, as signs (ayat) pointing past themselves to That of which they are the outward showing — for an aya is exactly a meaningful token that points beyond itself to another. Tracing these appearances back to their source and origin is the literal meaning of تَأْوِيل: a return to the awwal, 'the First' (عَودَۃ الی الاول). On one side, the Real is described as the beginning and end of all things, the apparent and the hidden; there is nothing in being but this one Reality: 'He is the First and the Last, the Outward and the Inward' (Al-Hadid: 3).

> هُوَ ٱلْأَوَّلُ وَٱلْآخِرُ وَٱلظَّهِرُ وَٱلْبَاطِنُ ۖ وَهُوَ بِكُلِّ شَىْءٍ عَلِيمٌ

On the other side, the apparent many is to be 'brought back' to the real one: 'We shall show them Our signs (sa-nurihim ayatina) on the horizons and in their own souls, so that it be clear to them that He is the Real' (Fussilat: 53).

> سَنُرِيهِمْ آيَتِنَا فِى ٱلأٓفَاقِ وَفِىٓ أَنفُسِهِمْ حَتَّىٰ يَتَبَيَّنَ لَهُمْ أَنَّهُ ٱلْحَقُّ

In one saying attributed to Ali (ع), the Prophet is described as the first whom God taught the science of تَأْوِيل, and who in turn taught it to Ali (ع). According to the Prophet, 'Truly, Ali (ع) is with the Quran, and the Quran is with Ali (ع), أنَّ علیٌ مع القرآن، و قرآن مع العلی. In another famous saying, often quoted by Sufi commentators, Ali (ع) claimed that he could load seventy camels with the pages of commentary he could offer on the Fatiha alone, the opening chapter of the Quran. It is worth noting too that Ali (ع)s cousin Ibn Abbas, often called the first commentator', claimed to have learned the science of interpretation from Ali (ع).

On this point, the following saying of the Imam is of fundamental importance, and it helps explain why he is held in the highest regard within the discipline of Quranic exegesis, tafsir: 'The Quran is a book inscribed, set between two covers; it does not speak with a tongue, and it cannot do without an interpreter (la budda lahu min tarjuman).' The mutual dependence of revelation and the intellect is underlined again in the saying, 'The prophet of a man is the interpreter of his intellect (rasul al-rajul tarjuman aqlihi).' Elsewhere there is an explicit correspondence between the intellect and the 'inner' messenger: the intellect is 'the messenger of the Real'.

The Imam's teachings, which give voice to his spiritual ethos, should therefore be read above all as creative interpretations of the Quranic revelation. They set in motion a movement from the letter of the revelation to its spirit, and from the spirit of the revelation to the spirit of the Revealer — a 'retracing', a return to the source, the origin, the beginning, al-awwal, carried out to the degree that the spirit of the intellect is brought to life within the interpreter. Yet the Revealer stays of necessity hidden even as He is disclosed through the revelation. He is hidden because the essence of what is revealed — the divine Reality in itself — cannot be spoken or put into words; only the forms of its manifestations can be revealed. The interplay between form and the formless, the evident and the hidden, the revealed and the concealed — such opposites lie at the root of the paradoxical manner of expression for which the Imam is renowned, and which recurs throughout this book. The relationship that most concerns the present discussion is the second of the three factors named above: that between revelation and 'intellection', by which is meant the creative use of the intellect. This relationship matters all the more in a setting shaped so largely by faithfulness to revelation. Where strict rationalists hold intellect and revelation to be mutually exclusive, this outlook sees them as perfectly in accord, standing in a relation of creative exchange, each needing the other for completeness, for wholeness, for the 'making one' that is the very essence of تَوْحِيد.

Indeed, one of the most striking things about the Imam's outlook is the way it exposes the hollowness of many false or overdrawn oppositions that later came to mark, or were imposed upon, Islamic thought. This follows from the strongly synthesizing quality of the Imam's discourses — seen as 'pre-philosophical' by some scholars, and as 'supra-philosophical', inspired and evocative, by those within the Islamic spiritual tradition. The seemingly unbridgeable split between intellect and revelation appears in several sharpened forms: reason against faith, convention (sunna) against nature (tabia), imitation (taqlid) against verification (tahqiq), piety against inquiry, and so on. In Ali (ع)'s ethos, however, intellect and revelation are not mutually exclusive but complementary, and at the highest level inseparable, since truth is one. While the human intellect plainly cannot be equated with divine revelation, the spirit that animates the intellect is not, in the end, bound by human limits. It is this spirit that perceives and grasps the divine quality of revelation.

The divine spirit that shapes the form and fills the content of revelation must be fathomable, to some degree, by human consciousness. Without this, revelation would be no more than a set of rules and regulations for the will, rather than a source of creative inspiration for the intelligence. Far from curbing the workings of reason and imposing some unintelligible command, divine revelation on the contrary calls for a creative engagement with all the resources of the intelligence — above all those acts of reflection and meditation for which the Imam himself is renowned, and which the Quran repeatedly asks for. It is the very opposite of a decree imposed from without: the Quran constantly demands fikr, deep and sustained reflection, and such reflection does not shut the meaning of the text inside its literal surface but opens it, unfolding the inner horizons of meaning folded within the letter.

For the revealed text is itself silent; it speaks only through its interpreter, the intellect. This understanding of the bond between intellect and revelation strictly rules out all literalism and shallowness, every attempt to pen meaning inside a flat, one-sided reading. How deeply the text's meaning is reached depends on the depth and keenness of the interpreter's own intellect, for the text carries many levels of meaning at once. At its deepest, this 'prophetic' intellect rejoins the very source of revelation itself. For it is this 'inner prophet', the innermost degree of consciousness, that is the final ground on which a person can confirm the verses of the outer revelation as true — proving them through lived experience, and not merely affirming them by dogma. It is in this sense that the Imam says, in the first sermon of the Nahj, that one purpose of God's sending prophets to humankind was 'to unearth for them the buried treasures of the intellects' (dafain al-uqul).

This saying is one of the keys to the spiritual ethos under study, and it returns again and again throughout these essays. Revelation does not stifle the intellect; it brings the intellect to life. It is a clarion call to the intellect's spiritual depths, cultivating the seeds of consciousness — the seeds of توحید — that God has planted there and that lie buried within us, and bringing them to fruition. The spirit of a person's intellect already carries the first imprint of its Creator. In the world of the spirit it beheld its Lord and testified to His lordship, and revelation — the ذکر — does no more than remind it of that primordial testimony. A reminder is never a new piece of information. It is knowledge a person already holds but has forgotten, for one can only be reminded of what one already knows. We were created in this world in a kind of amnesia. Just as a physician tells the family of a man who has lost his memory to surround him with familiar things — with signs (آیات) out of his past — to stir his memory, so God has set His reminders in the horizons and the souls (آفَاق وَ أَنْفُس). The spirit of the intellect, then, cannot be a stranger to what descends as revelation from on high. On the contrary, it must be fundamentally akin to it, and for just this reason a person can be wholly certain that the revelation truly comes from the Absolute. The point is not to set intellect against revelation, but to see the intellect as revelation, or at least as one mode of it: the God-given means by which a person comes to discern, affirm, and plumb the divine 'revelations' outside himself — both those of scripture and those made up of the 'signs' (ayat) of the natural world. If the human intellect can perceive and recognize revelation, it is because both are rooted in one and the same ultimate source. We came from a spiritual realm, and so did revelation, sent to remind us of what we once beheld and have since forgotten. Prophetic revelation awakens and quickens the depths of the human intellect; it does not replace or displace it. The messengers do for these buried seeds what the farmer does when he waters what lies hidden beneath the soil, what the miner does when he digs into the mountain for its gold and silver, and what the diver does when he goes down to the sea-bed to bring up its buried treasures. Seen this way, the revealed text is not some outside source set against the intelligence. It is the outward, objective expression of the very principles that the intellect itself yields up to the 'inner prophet' — the intellect serving as the inner messenger whom the outer messenger awakens and nourishes. And by 'intellect' is meant not simply the faculty of reasoning, but the source of consciousness that gives the human spirit its articulate form.

The intellect holds unsuspected depths and buried treasures; it is far from exhausted by its surface operations, the cognitions and reasonings that belong to its purely mental dimension. The third matter concerns the relationship between intellect and reason. Just as there can be no contradiction whatever between revelation and intellection, so too a false split between reason and intellect must be avoided. Reason should rather be seen as one mode of the intellect; reason no more contradicts intellect than intellect contradicts revelation. Reason is a mode of the intellect, but it must not be equated with it wholesale — just as the universal objectivity of revelation cannot be reduced to the subjective particulars of any one person's intellection, to his or her own 'private' revelation. To equate reason with intellect would amount to denying the very existence of the intellect's 'buried treasures' — those supra-rational dimensions that lie, at first, beneath the rational surface of the intellect, and so hidden from it. The revelation that the intellect constitutes is embedded within the creation of the human being, the intellect being what most decisively defines a human being as such. Far from pitting intellect against revelation, each is to be seen in the light of the other. On one side, the substance of revelation can be engaged only through the intellect; on the other, the intellect's 'buried treasures' can be disclosed only through revelation. The two genuinely depend on each other.

This understanding of the intellect stands close to what the Imam calls the 'heart' (قَلْب), that innermost mode of perception able to 'see' God. It is the 'heart' that can 'see' God — not with eyesight, not with بصارۃ, but with insight, with بصیرۃ, the spiritual insight generated by the 'verities of faith' (haqaiq al-iman / حقائق الایمان). As the Imam put it, when a questioner asked whether he had 'seen' his Lord: 'Eyes see Him not through sight's observation, but hearts see Him through the verities of faith.'

Between the spiritual depths of the intellect and its rational surface there is both continuity and discontinuity. The continuity shows itself in a plain fact: all thinking begins with concepts that carry intelligible meaning. Thought sets out from ideas that reason can make out, and these must bear some relation to the realities they express, or they collapse into ideas that lead nowhere. But there is discontinuity too between the spiritual and the rational aspects of the intellect, in that reason stops short at the ideas it has 'de-fined'. The Arabic word for definition, hadd (حَدّ), carries — like its English counterpart — both senses at once: a conceptual definition and a limitation, a boundary. It is the very boundary that reason draws around an idea, weighing its pros and cons until the idea is fixed and 'defined'. To pass beyond the limits set by this rational act of definition, something more than reason is needed; and it is this 'something more' that makes the ethos under study a spiritual one rather than a rationalistic one. The ethos is certainly 'rational', in that its form can indeed be examined by reason; but it is not 'rationalistic', since its essence does not rest on reason alone.

Ali (ع) often hints, in highly allusive terms, at the mysteries and marvels — the unearthed treasures of the intellect — that he has directly witnessed with the eye of his purified heart. He alludes to this supra-rational mode of awareness indirectly too, through a bewildering use of apophatic discourse and paradoxical utterance, with which the corpus attributed to him is filled. In this ethos it seems that the higher the truth, the deeper the paradox. The sheer gulf between reality as it is in itself and the human conceptions formed of it is powerfully evoked by this creative use of paradox. In modern terms, each human conception of reality needs to be 'deconstructed'. Yet unlike a modern deconstructionism so thoroughly destructive that it has nothing left to 'reconstruct', the Imam's apophasis feeds directly into modes of spiritual intuition, contemplation, and realization of what reason, on its own, cannot reach. It is this intuition and contemplation that 're-members' — or 'reconstructs', if one likes — the spiritual realities that lie beyond the categories of reason. The value of the paradoxes, the antinomies, the apophasis, and the negation rests entirely on the eminently positive realities that the intellect can plumb, and so come to know its own hidden depths. The consciousness that results does not so much negate reason as place it, revealing the limits of reason's proper sphere. It shows that reason alone cannot 'comprehend' the higher realities, nor express them fully — however indispensable reason may be in setting the movement of knowledge toward those realities in motion.

Thus when the Imam denies, as he so often does, that human conceptions can encompass the divine Reality, what he denies is the limitation that inescapably comes with all such conceptions — not the positive content, the mana or spiritual meaning, of the conception itself. For this conception is a necessary starting point for a process of assimilation that draws on a deeper level of awareness than the one the mind has 'de-fined', and so reaches far beyond the realm of speculative thought. Seen in this light, the negation of the limits of human conception is the prelude to an absolute affirmation: the affirmation of the Absolute, which has no limit. The spiritual negation of conceptual limitation is of an upward, 'constructive' order, not a nihilistic, deconstructive one. Nowhere is this style of discourse more apparent than in the famous opening lines of the first sermon of the Nahj al-balagha:

Praise be to God, whose laudation those who speak cannot deliver, whose graces those who count cannot number, whose rightful due those who strive cannot render; He who cannot be grasped by far-reaching aspirations, nor fathomed by profound intuitions; He whose attribute has no binding limitation, no existing description, no time appointed, no term extended.

The divine attribute (sifa) has no limit by which it can be defined — laysa li-sifatih hadd mahdud — and yet it is affirmed as real. It has no 'existing description', and yet in some sense it is described (mawsuf) — by the very words of the Quranic revelation; for in the Quran God is described as having such attributes as mercy, compassion, and power. He is the Merciful, the Compassionate, the Almighty, and yet the Imam affirms that these very attributes cannot be defined, bounded, or described. The paradox sharpens further when the Imam speaks of the perfection of ikhlas, the 'rendering sincere' — the purification of one's devotion to God and of one's conception of Him. This perfection, he says, is to divest (nafy) God of all attributes. On the one hand the attributes are affirmed, and on the other they are to be negated. The rational mind can think of God only through the attributes put into words in the Quran; and yet here the Imam appears to negate those same attributes, stripping the mind of any convenient conceptual handle by which to grasp the nature of the divine Reality. Affirming and negating the attributes at once raises a seemingly irresolvable contradiction, and in doing so opens the mind to a deeper mode of knowing. This style of discourse appears designed to awaken in the listener some latent awareness inscribed in the heart or the depths of the intellect, and to draw the seeker to dive into those depths, into the knowledge 'buried' in the intellect. The very shock of the apparent contradiction in this apophasis seems meant to precipitate a leap of consciousness, from the formal activity of reason to the supra-rational contemplation of the spirit.

The words spoken by the Imam immediately after affirming that his heart had indeed witnessed God, not by means of outward vision but through 'the verities of faith', run:

My Lord is not described in terms of dimension (bu'd), nor motion (haraka), nor rest (sukun) ... nor through coming or going: He is subtle in subtlety but not described by subtlety, magnificent in magnificence but not described by magnificence, great in greatness but not described by greatness ...

Again the attributes are affirmed and denied. God possesses these attributes but cannot be reduced to them. The mind can begin to think of God through these categories, but He cannot be reduced to such conceptions. The attributes give the mind something by which the divine nature can be understood, but this understanding is fragmentary, never complete, and it must be negated as the prelude to a fuller grasp. The mind's first conceptual orientation to the divine Reality must be both negated and sublimated: negated as a mental conception, while the positive content of that conception is sublimated through spiritual intuition. Only then can the two-dimensional 'portrait' that reason draws be given a third dimension — that of depth. But this third dimension emerges only once the insufficiency of the rational depiction is understood — hence the need for the cognitive shock that paradox produces, a shock meant to expose the pretension of reason as it strains to disclose the nature of the Absolute, the transcendent source of reason's own activity.

The Imam's discourses show a repeated combination of affirmation and denial, mirroring the negation and affirmation found in the first testimony of Islam: la ilaha, 'no god', being the negation (nafy); then illaLlah, 'except God', coming as the affirmation (ithbat). This helps to show that the negation of the attributes — which the Imam names as the perfection of ikhlas — is in fact an affirmation of the infinitude of the divine Reality. Each attribute is to be 'negated', but only insofar as the mind conceives it. Such negation is necessary because, at the level of the mind, each attribute excludes the other attributes, and excludes as well the object of attribution itself — that is, the divine Essence. This exclusion follows inescapably from the mere possibility of drawing a conceptual distinction between the attribute and the object of attribution, between the divine quality and the divine Essence. For any conception to take place in the mind, defined ideas are necessary; and where there are definitions, there are limits, and so exclusions. The reality of the attribute, however, is unlimited, by virtue of its absolute identity with the Essence. The attribute that has been 'negated' is paradoxically affirmed — not in itself, but inasmuch as it is nothing but God: illaLlah. Thus one can place each of the negated attributes in the position of the nafy of the first testimony and arrive at such formulations as la rahmana illaLlah, 'There is no All-Compassionate except God'; la rahima illaLlah, 'No All-Merciful except God', and so on. The apparent negation of the attributes becomes, in fact, an affirmation of their true identity, freed of any restrictive tie to the poverty of mental conceptions. The attributes are not added on to the Essence but are absolutely one with it, distinguishable from it only in concept, not in being. The Essence does indeed comprise all the attributes named — greatness, subtlety, and the rest — but it is also infinitely more than these attributes; and the attributes, for their part, are infinite only within the Essence and as the Essence. The Imam's apophasis here can be seen as a commentary on the Quranic refrain, 'Glorified be God above what they describe' (Al-Mu'minun: 91). The infinitude of God strictly requires, on the human plane, the negation of all that the mind can conceive through reason — but not all that the heart can 'see' through spiritual vision. This very negation on the plane of reason calls out to something deeper, and that in turn can be understood only in the light of spiritual practice, which is always assumed as the reflective, devotional, or contemplative setting of those who heard the Imam's sermons and discourses. The heart, the symbolic seat of the contemplative intelligence, calls out to be 'polished', and this is exactly what the 'remembrance of God' does — this dhikr Allah being, according to the Imam, the 'most excellent form of worship'. Even at this level of practice there is a further paradox: the 'reality' of the remembrance is not attained until 'you forget yourself in your remembrance'.

It is not only the rational mind and the determinate conceptions matched to it that are to be negated or 'deconstructed', but the individual soul itself, as the seat of all cognition, rational or supra-rational. This final denouement — fana — is the ultimate form of apophasis.

These considerations show how far the spirit of the intellect, in Ali (ع)'s view, surpasses the rational faculty. The intellect must be seen, first and foremost, as a spiritual faculty; and the spirit, in turn, must be recognized as the source of the intellect, as well as the source of life. Without the intellect the spirit is reduced to unconscious existence; conversely, without the spirit, the intellect tends toward lifeless thought. These aspects of the spirit are clearly expressed in the first sermon of the Nahj, which can be read as a commentary on the Quranic verses that describe the creation of man in terms of God's breathing' of His spirit into man: 'He began the creation of man with clay ... He fashioned him and breathed into him of His spirit' (ثُمَّ سَوَّىٰهُ وَنَفَخَ فِيهِ مِن رُّوحِهِ).

The following lines, from the first sermon, form one of the many passages in which Ali (ع) comments on this infusion of the divine spirit into man:

> ثُمَّ جَمَعَ سُبْحَانَهُ مِنْ حَزْنِ الاْرْضِ وَسَهْلِهَا، وَعَذْبِهَا وَسَبَخِهَا۔ تُرْبَةً سَنَّهَا بالمَاءِ حَتَّى خَلَصَتْ۔ وَلاَطَهَا بِالبَلَّةِ حَتَّى لَزَبَتْ۔ فَجَبَلَ مِنْها صُورَةً ذَاتَ أَحْنَاءَ وَوُصُولَ، وَأَعْضَاءَ وَفُصُول۔ أَجْمَدَهَا حَتَّى اسْتَمْسَكَتْ، وَأَصْلَدَهَا حَتَّى صَلْصَلَتْ۔ لِوَقْت مَعْدُود، وَأجَل مَعْلُوم۔ ثُمَّ نَفَخَ فِيها مِنْ رُوحِهِ فَمَثُلَتْ إِنْساناً ذَا أَذْهَانُ يُجيلُهَا، وَفِكَر يَتَصَرَّفُ بِهَا، وَجَوَارِحَ يَخْتَدِمُهَا، وَأَدَوَات يُقَلِّبُهَا، وَمَعْرِفَة يَفْرُقُ بِهَا بَيْنَ الحَقِّ وَالبَاطِلِ۔

Then He, glorified be He, gathered up a quantity of dust, from some hard earth and some soft, some sweet earth and some salty, and poured water onto it until it was pure, then kneaded into it some moisture until it stuck together; then He fashioned from this a form with curves and joints, limbs and parts. He compacted it until it held tightly together, solidified it until it became as sounding clay, assigning to it a measured duration and a known consummation. Then He breathed into it of His spirit, and it stood up as a human being, endowed with intellectual powers with which to reflect, thoughts by which to conduct himself, limbs to put to service, instruments at his disposal, and knowledge with which to discern between the true and the false ...

The body had been fashioned whole — its eyes, its ears, the organ of thought all in place — and yet it could neither hear nor see nor reflect until God breathed His spirit into it; only then did it stand up as a human being. It is this 'in-spiration' — literally the in-breathing, the placing of the spirit, of God's breath — that produces consciousness and life. Death, by contrast, is the 'ex-piration', the departure of the spirit. Life without 'aspiration' — the intention to be true to one's spirit — is a kind of death. It is through the spirit that inanimate, unconscious matter is brought to life and to awareness of itself: it generates not only knowledge but self-knowledge as well. This intimate intertwining of the gift of life with the capacity to know — existence that is conscious, and knowledge that is existential — is one of the foundations of any truly spiritual perspective. The question is not one of setting spirit against body, but of recognizing that the infusion of the spirit into the body is precisely what brings the body to life; and for this reason knowledge vivifies, in the very measure that it is spiritual: 'O ye who believe, respond to God and His Messenger when they call you to that which vivifies you' (Al-Anfal: 24). According to the Imam, one of the most important aspects of this call from God to human beings is to 'remind them of the buried treasures of the intellect.'

Existentiation is the bestowal of the spirit, which is consciousness. The 'aql, or intellect, is the principle by which the spirit is given articulate form, and so it is not merely a mental faculty but is itself constitutive of our true life. The intellect is that of which the mental faculty is one formal expression, one mode of its working, but by no means the only one. The aql is many-sided, taking in aspects that in our times are rarely associated with intelligence. To be true to one's intellect — to the treasures buried deep within it, and not merely to the rational functions at work on its surface — amounts to being spiritual.

For Ali (ع), the true intellectual, al-aqil (الْعَاقِل), is one who not only thinks correctly but also acts ethically and, at the deepest level, seeks to realize the ultimate Reality. The intellectual is defined as one who 'puts all things in their proper place' (العاقل من وضع شئی حق محلہ), one who brings his acts into line with the intentions of his Creator. This same alignment is the very definition of justice (عَدْل); and so only the true intellectual can be fully just (عَادِل), for only one who sees things as they truly are can put them in their right place.

Thought, action, and realization are all the concern of the true intellectual, the 'aqil. Correct thinking, impeccable virtue, and authentic being are inseparably bound up with the intellect in the spiritual ethos considered here. These aspects of the ethos come into sharper focus in the light of the Imam's discourse to Kumayl.

<!-- editorial:begin -->
> **Editorial note (tradition-grounded).** 
> A related teaching preserved in this tradition clarifies the very word this chapter turns on.
> The Arabic 'aql, here rendered "intellect," derives from a root whose first sense is to tie down
> — the same word names the rope that tethers a camel. What follows from that image is a mind that
> binds and holds meaning fast, a restraining, unifying faculty far deeper than ordinary
> step-by-step reasoning. That etymology quietly supports the distinction the chapter draws:
> reason weighs and compares, but the intellect gathers and secures, tying the knower to the
> reality perceived rather than merely thinking about it. Read this way, Ali's "spirit of the
> intellect" is not a sharper logic but a faculty that fastens the whole person — conduct and
> contemplation alike — to what it beholds.
<!-- editorial:end -->

## 4. The Discourse to Kumayl

The discourse the Imam delivered to Kumayl brings these aspects of the ethos into sharper focus. Its importance lies above all in its deeply esoteric character. Taking his disciple Kumayl ibn Ziyad by the hand, the Imam led him out of Kufa to a graveyard. Once the city lay behind them, he drew a deep breath and gave the following private counsel.

> يَا كُمَيْل بْن زِيَادٍ، إِنَّ هذهِ الْقُلُوبَ أَوْعِيَةٌ فَخَيْرُهَا أَوْعَاهَا فَاحْفَظْ عَنِّي مَا أَقُولُ لَكَ۔ النَّاسُ ثَلاَثَةٌ- فَعَالِمٌ رَبَّانِيٌّ وَمُتَعَلِّمٌ عَلَى سَبِيلِ نَجَاةٍ، وَهَمَجٌ رَعَاعٌ أَتْبَاعُ كُلِّ نَاعِقٍ يَمِيلُونَ مَعَ كُلِّ رِيحٍ، لَمْ يَسْتَضِيئُوا بِنُورِ الْعِلْمِ، وَلَمْ يَلْجَؤُوا إِلَى رُكْنٍ وَثِيقٍ

O Kumayl ibn Ziyad, truly these hearts are vessels, containers for knowledge and wisdom, and the best of them are those which hold the most. So retain from me that which I say to you. People are divided into three types: a lordly knower ('alim rabbani); one who seeks knowledge (mutaallim) for the sake of deliverance; and the common folk (hamaj raa), the word naming the moth that hurls itself at any flame, following just anyone, swaying with every current, neither desiring to be illumined by the light of knowledge nor seeking refuge in any strong support.

O Kumayl, knowledge is better than wealth, for knowledge guards you, while you must guard your wealth; and wealth diminishes as it is spent, while knowledge only increases as it is disbursed, replenishing itself the more it is given away; the fruits of wealth vanish when the wealth itself is gone, the friends bought with money and the comforts it purchased all falling away with it, while no one can take a man's knowledge from him. O Kumayl ibn Ziyad, the wise apprehension of knowledge (marifat al-ilm) is a religion by which God is worshipped, for din, religion, is kin to dayn, a debt, and it is by acquiring knowledge that a man repays the debt his own intellect has incurred to its Maker. Through it man acquires obedience in his life and a good name after his death. And knowledge is a judge, while wealth is judged: knowledge is active and wealth passive, for the one who knows takes command of what he owns, his knowledge directing him how to handle it, whereas wealth is forever something ruled upon by its possessor.

O Kumayl ibn Ziyad, those who hoard wealth perish even while they live, but the knowers endure for as long as time subsists; their material forms are absent, but their images in the hearts of men are present. Ah, what abundant knowledge is here (and he pointed to his breast), would that I could find one to bear it. Yes, I found one who was quick to understand, but he could not be trusted with it, exploiting the tools of religion for the sake of worldly gain, empowering himself with the graces of God against His slaves and with His proofs against His friends; for when the vessel of the heart is corrupt, this knowledge, pure and precious as it is, becomes a weapon turned against the very friends of God. Then there was another, obedient to the bearers of truth but lacking heartfelt insight, in whom, at the first appearance of obscurity, doubt was kindled in the heart. So alas, neither this one nor that! Nor one who is greedy for pleasure and submissive to passion, nor one obsessed with acquisition and accumulation, neither of them a guardian of religion in any respect. They resemble nothing so much as grazing cattle. Thus does knowledge die with the death of its bearers, when its trust falls to the worthless and the self-serving.

But indeed, O my God, the earth will never be empty of one who establishes the proof of God, whether overtly and in the open or fearfully and in obscurity, lest God's proofs and elucidations come to naught. Yet how many are such as these, and where are they? By God, they may be the smallest in number, but with God they are the greatest in rank, for here authority is not the possession of the many, and truth is not settled by the count of those who hold it; the guardians of the proof are few, and it is they who stand highest in the sight of God. Through them God preserves His proofs and elucidations, so that they may entrust them to their compeers and sow them in the hearts of those who resemble them. Through them, knowledge penetrates the reality of insight; they take hold of the very spirit of certainty; they make easy what the extravagant find harsh, and they befriend that by which the ignorant are estranged; with their bodies they keep company with the world, while their spirits are bound to the transcendent realm. They are the vicegerents of God upon His earth, the summoners to His religion. Ah, how I long to see them! Go now, Kumayl, as you will.

The four sentences of the discourse's second-to-last paragraph describe those who bear the title of lordly knower ('alim rabbani): those who are fewest in number yet greatest in the sight of God, and through whom His religion is sustained. By His religion is meant the divine reality of religion, the pure and unchanging essence of the faith, perfect in itself, as distinct from religion in the ordinary sense, whose outward institutions are subject to the shifts of human history. From Adam to the Seal of the prophets those outward institutions have changed with the conditions of men, but the essence, the message, and the purpose have never altered: the salvation of the soul through willing submission to God, as the Quran declares: إِنَّ ٱلدِّينَ عِندَ ٱللَّهِ ٱلْإِسْلَمُ, "Truly, religion in the sight of God is submission" (Ali 'Imran: 19). It is by means of the knowledge of the true knowers that religion is true to itself, and only through such sages that religion truly lives in the world.

"Through them, knowledge penetrates the reality of insight." He does not say that their knowledge penetrates it, but that through them knowledge penetrates the reality of insight. Knowledge can remain merely abstract, far removed from the reality of insight. There is the outward, surface knowledge of a thing, its zahir, and there is the inner, hidden knowledge of the same thing, its batin. Yet knowledge holds within it a potential that calls out to be realized; it needs to be brought to life, so that it may reach past the words and concepts that make up its own outward form, the meaning that lies ready on the surface, to something deeper. A man may have a surface knowledge of a verse of the Quran, of a saying, of why he prays and fasts and how many cycles each prayer requires. But when he presses past that surface and asks what the bowing and the prostration truly represent, a world of knowledge opens that is wholly unlike the bare information he began with. Degrees of knowledge are plainly implied here, and with them the need to avoid all literalism and shallowness, and the easy mistake of equating factual information with real knowledge, let alone with wisdom. The shift of consciousness that this penetration requires is shown vividly by the Imam's gesture toward his breast: for it is not the chest but the heart hidden behind it that holds knowledge. The chest is the outward form, the zahir that conceals the heart as its batin, and to reach the heart one must first pass through the chest.

The word chosen to translate hajama is "penetrate," but the primary sense of the verb is to assault, to take by storm, to charge. In the warfare of that age the battle lines were drawn as a right wing, a left wing, and a center, the heart of the army (qalb al-askar), where the king or commander stood. The aim of the assault was to break through the wings and reach that heart, for once the king was taken the battle was won, and the victor took command of all the resources of the defeated: the kingdom, its treasury, its army, its people. The chessboard preserves the same rule. So the word helps to show that there is in conceptual knowledge a latent force, one might almost call it an explosive power, deriving from the reality of knowledge, its haqiqa or spiritual essence. This is what calls out to be made real through tahaqquq, realization in depth rather than mere verification on the surface; and the striving by which one storms the outer wall of words to reach the heart of their meaning is tahqiq, investigation. This haqiqa can be indicated, but never captured, by the words and concepts that make up its formal, communicable aspect, just as the chest can only be opened to reach the heart. By means of this inner force, which its outer conceptual form at once hides and calls forth, knowledge is able to bring about a shift of consciousness from one degree of knowledge to another: a man begins to see the very realities of this world differently from those who rest in the surface play of cause and effect, so that the same act which is a burden to one is a delight to another. An indirect, mental conception of reality thus opens the way to a penetrating, lived perception of reality. This is the difference between mere sight and true insight. Sight reaches only the outward, surface reality, whereas the reality of insight opens the intelligence to reality as such, while ordinary insight, like abstract knowledge, remains only at the surface level of understanding. The same idea is expressed in a saying of Ali (ع) concerning the true understanding that plumbs, and so truly knows, the buried treasures of knowledge: "He who understands knows the depth of knowledge (man fahima 'alima ghawr al-ilm)." And the effort that true knowledge demands, as against the mere accumulation of facts, is likewise indicated: "Through learning and assimilating knowledge, until it becomes deeply rooted in one's consciousness, is knowledge attained (bil-taallum yunalul-ilm)."

The urgency of realizing this indwelling power within knowledge, of moving from the abstract to the concrete, the potential to the actual, the static to the dynamic, the formal to the essential, is powerfully evoked by hajama, a word bound up with battle. It warns against taking the first understanding of an idea for its full comprehension, and its full intellectual comprehension for its dynamic interiorization in the spirit. For when knowledge at last storms the surface of religion and takes the heart, a transformation begins from within: the heart, now illumined by the realities it has seized, takes command of the limbs and the actions, which are the soul's own resources, exactly as the victor takes command of the kingdom he has won. This interiorization is a process of infinite unfolding within, not the mere registering of some fact from without. It requires the effective integration of knowledge within being; knowledge from without must be transformed into being from within, woven into the deepest texture of one's existence. Knowledge thus demands not merely the attention of the mind but the total orientation of the soul.

In this ethos, then, the effective integration (تَوْحِيد) of knowledge within being cannot be reached without the participation of the whole soul; and this in turn requires the elimination of all the vices that flow from self-centeredness. Thus the 'aqil, the true intellectual, is described by the Imam as one constantly engaged in struggle against his own soul, against the lower inclinations, the passionate whims, and the distracting caprices of the ego. All these vices, far from being confined to the plane of morality, reach into the domain of knowledge, and this is grasped in the very measure that knowledge itself is understood as a concrete vision of things rather than a merely notional or conceptual apprehension. The vices are then seen as so many clouds obscuring the vision proper to the reality of insight. According to the Quran, evil actions become a rust that covers over the heart: "Nay, their hearts have become rusted by that which they have done" (Al-Mutaffifin: 14). The aqil, then, is engaged in a constant inner struggle against the tendencies that not only sully the soul but blind the heart. As a virtuous man he opposes vice on the moral plane, because it is contrary to virtue; but vice is opposed for a further reason, on the higher plane of spiritual reality: it contradicts the nature of the Real, it obscures the reality of insight, and it blinds the eye of the heart, which alone can see God.

This is not to deny the validity of the moral perspective; the stress falls rather on the deeper motive that drives the 'aqil toward virtue, a motive that rises above the moral plane while including it. Morality emerges here as the fruit or effect, and spirituality as the seed or cause. By drawing attention to the intellectual side of the aqil's motive for shedding the vices, the Imam shows more clearly how immorality is rooted in ignorance; and the uprooting of that ignorance, for the true intellectual, amounts to dissolving the very substance of the vices.
