This uprooting demands great effort, a relentless battle within, indeed the most formidable of all battles, al-jihad al-akbar, the greatest struggle, as the Prophet defined it.

The following sayings of Ali (ع) bear on the importance of this inner struggle:

"Struggling against the soul through knowledge, such is the mark of the intellect."

"The strongest of people are those who are strongest against their own souls."

"Truly, one who fights his own soul in obedience to God, and does not sin against Him, has the rank of the righteous martyr in the sight of God."

"The ultimate battle is that of a man against his own soul."

"He who knows his soul fights it."

"No jihad is more excellent than the jihad of the soul."

In another saying the Imam clarifies what the Prophet meant by defining the greatest jihad as the jihad of the soul (al-nafs). It is not so much a matter of fighting against the soul as of the battle for the soul itself. This emerges from the metaphor by which the Imam defines the struggle: al-aql, the intellect, is the leader of the forces of al-Rahman, the Compassionate; al-hawa, whim, caprice, desire, commands the forces of al-shaytan, the devil; and al-nafs, the soul, wavers between them, drawn by both (mutajadhiba baynahuma), falling under the sway of whichever of the two prevails.

These sayings make clear that for the true intellectual the soul in its entirety must be "conquered" and enlisted on the side of the intellect. The aim is not to destroy the lower soul but to free it from the destructive influence of its caprice, its egoistic whims, its subjectivism, its individualism, all of which the word hawa carries. The Prophet, the Quran tells us, does not speak from hawa; rather: "It is naught but a revelation revealed" (An-Najm: 3-4). The divine objectivity of revelation is here contrasted with the human subjectivity of passionate whim. In the context of the holy war within, the Imam's sayings bring a similar opposition into focus: the objectivity of the intellect must prevail over the subjectivity of individual caprice and egotistic desire. It is the intellect that represents, in the sense of "makes present," the divine power of al-Rahman, and that combats the forces of al-shaytan as embodied in al-hawa.

The word hawa is used further in the Quran to denote desires that spring from the ego, by which the ego becomes a kind of false god: "Hast thou seen him who maketh his desire his god?" (25: 43; almost identical at 45: 23). This is the subtle or hidden idolatry (al-shirk al-khafi) that the Prophet described as being more hidden than a black ant crawling on a dark stone in a moonless night. To eliminate this idolatry of the ego, a force higher than the ego itself must be brought to bear, for the ego cannot be controlled by anything that belongs to it; and that higher force is the divine Reality, reached through the power of the intellect and the graces of faith. This partnership between the effort to overcome the egoism of hawa and the inflowing of divine grace is well expressed in the following Quranic verse: "As for he who fears the station of his Lord, and restraineth his soul from whim (hawa), verily, Paradise will be his abode" (An-Nazi'at: 40-41).

The soul's fundamental energy is not to be destroyed but converted and redirected, away from the transient objects of individualistic desire and the impulses of al-shaytan, and toward the one true object expressed by al-Rahman. This "conquest" of the soul involves far more than arriving at some mental conception. It is not only the mind, the will, and the character that need to be transformed; the soul's very deepest disposition must be integrated into the truth the intellect perceives, if one is to be capable of perceiving that truth in all its dimensions and consequences across the different planes of being.

For the Imam the need to win the soul over in its totality follows naturally from his understanding of تَوْحِيد, the most fundamental principle of Islam. تَوْحِيد is a verbal noun, and to translate it as mere oneness or unity fails to capture its full force. It means literally "affirming one," the affirmation of and belief in God's oneness, which is the outward, theological meaning; or "realizing one," the realization that there is but one reality, which is the inner, spiritual meaning. In the Imam's perspective, therefore, the absolutely Real is perceived not only as being one but also as making one, as integrating. The Real is not only unique; it is also unifying. تَوْحِيد thus comes to mean not merely affirming the oneness of God, but being one oneself so as to reflect the One perfectly: to know God is to be "integrated." Here a link appears with the root meaning of 'aql, which is "to bind." Yet whereas the later mystical tradition tends to stress the limiting aspect of this word, the intellect as that which defines and encloses meaning, in contrast to the heart, which intuits and discloses spiritual truth, here the intellect is that which "binds together" the scattered elements of the soul into a living unity. This principle is expressed in the first of the following series of the Imam's sayings, each of which merits deep reflection:

"He who knows God integrates himself (man 'arafaLlah tawahhad)."

"He who knows his soul disengages himself (man 'arafa nafsahu tajarrad)."

"He who knows people isolates himself (man 'arafal-nas tafarrad)."

"He who knows the world withholds himself (man 'arafal-dunya tazahhad)."

Elsewhere this total adherence to the Real is described by the Imam as "sincerity," sidq or ikhlas, terms that denote far more than simple truthfulness, as is already evident from the root of ikhlas. The verb khalusa means to be utterly pure, true to one's essence, unmixed with anything foreign to it. This dimension of the meaning comes through clearly in the following saying of the Imam: "Make purely for God (akhlis liLlah) your action and your knowledge, your love and your hatred, your taking and your leaving, your speech and your silence."

To be totally sincere, then, means to make oneself "purely" for God, to carry no "stain" of impurity, understood here as whatever is other than God, in one's devotion, action, intention, or even feeling. One must love and hate only in relation to one's bond with God. Of all the tendencies that creep into one's motives and intentions, the most destructive is egotism. Ikhlas, pure sincerity, calls for the active effort to be, think, and act purely for God, that is, with selflessness, which is why the Imam places such stress on the holy war within the soul as the "ultimate battle." One who wins this battle attains the rank of the "righteous martyr," for the victor here is a martyr in the truest sense: he has sacrificed his lower life for the sake of God, with whom is the real life, the life witnessed by the intellect, which is the only element within human consciousness able to look upon the soul as "the other" with genuine objectivity.

As the saying holds, "struggling against the soul through knowledge, such is the mark of the intellect." The intellectual engaged in this battle is also one who bears "witness" (al-shahid, meaning both martyr and witness, as in the original Greek sense of the word). He bears witness to God through the intellect's sacrifice of whatever opposes the truth of God, and the chief opponent is his own egotism, his own "false life." To possess perfect ikhlas, then, is to be rid of all hypocritical self-preoccupation and to replace it with a sincere theocentricity, a mode of being centered upon God rather than oneself. Ikhlas, therefore, means being for God, or belonging to God, in the sense indicated by the Quranic verse: "Say: Truly my worship and my sacrifice, and my living and my dying, are for God, Lord of the universes" (Al-An'am: 162).

That worship is the first thing mentioned in this verse is of particular significance for understanding Ali (ع)'s spiritual ethos, and the indispensable role of worship and devotion must be stressed in any account of it. The second chapter takes up this role in the context of the quest for the fundamental virtue of justice. The ultimate fruits of worship and devotion, whose very essence is the remembrance of God, are the subject of the third chapter. One of the defining features of the Imam's spiritual ethos is indeed the emphasis he places on prayer, taking that word in its widest sense to include every means by which a person is oriented and attuned to the Real, al-Haqq: formal worship and supplication, meditation and reflection, remembrance and contemplation.

Worship is therefore an integral aspect of ikhlas, precisely because it relates to and sustains a fundamental dimension of the human soul. That within the soul which yearns to worship, and is nourished only by worship, must be given its due. In a spiritual context, if worship is pushed aside, one's sincerity is compromised: one is no longer being sincere with God, no longer "just" to God, no longer rendering God His due. And the spiritual logic of the Imam holds that if one is not just with God, one cannot be just to His creatures. "Whoso establishes well-being between himself and God," he says, "God establishes well-being between him and mankind."

One might also cite here the Quranic verse: "Worship thy Lord until certainty cometh to thee" (Al-Hijr: 99). The quest for knowledge demands total dedication if it is to yield its inner riches, and that dedication includes the quasi-alchemical transformation of consciousness brought about by total concentration on the divine Reality, which is prayer in one of its deepest modes. In this form of prayer, the intensity of dedication is such that nothing else can occupy one's consciousness alongside its object: the "remembrance" (dhikr) of God strictly implies the forgetting of everything else, including, above all, oneself. The spiritual reality of the dhikr can only be attained when "you forget your soul in your remembrance." Concentration upon the divine Reality as the supreme object of knowledge, made present through the Name or Names of God, is thus a mode of prayer, one which, to be effective, requires both presence of mind and a genuine engagement of the will. It is far from sufficient to pray absent-mindedly or mechanically. In this connection the Imam says, "He who prays without exertion is like one who shoots [arrows] without a bow." Equally, there is nothing passive or easy about acquiring knowledge: "Knowledge calls out for action; if it is answered [it is of avail], otherwise it departs."

The same idea is expressed more forcefully in this saying of the Imam: "When you hear knowledge [being imparted] cleave to it, do not mix it up with jest, lest hearts spew it forth." Knowledge that is not taken seriously will be of no avail; it remains "undigested" and cannot be assimilated by the "heart," that innermost faculty which has access to the "reality of insight" and which can thus "see" God. For it is the heart that sees Allah, not by eyesight, not by بصارۃ, but through the spiritual insight, the بصیرۃ, generated by the true principles of faith, the حقائق الایمان. Indeed, it is not the individual who "assimilates" knowledge to himself; it is knowledge, or rather the reality of being expressed through true knowledge, that assimilates the individual to itself: "Acquire knowledge, and [true] life will acquire you." The relationship between life and consciousness, so central to the intrinsic function of the spirit, is again at the heart of this maxim. At first, the individual seeks knowledge and cultivates consciousness; then, life "seeks" the individual as being makes itself more fully real within him. Put differently, the individual first makes an active effort to integrate knowledge within his being; but at a certain point, this activity is transformed into passive receptivity: being, "true life," integrates the individual within itself. To return to the Imam's guidance to Kumayl: "Through them knowledge penetrates the reality of insight." It is not the knowers who penetrate; it is knowledge that penetrates to the reality of insight, and it does so through them, in the very measure of their effacement within knowledge, an effacement that is the shadow cast by their effective integration within the life that "acquires" them.

<!-- editorial:begin -->
> **Editorial note (tradition-grounded).** 
> When the Imam speaks of "marifat al-ilm," he draws on a word the wider tradition marks off
> sharply from ordinary information. The root ع-ر-ف means to know by recognition — to meet
> something and know it firsthand, not merely to know about it; marifa is thus the experiential
> knowledge of God, the gnosis of one who has come to recognize Him rather than only reason toward
> Him. This helps explain why the discourse sets the lordly knower so far above the one who merely
> accumulates learning, and why it can describe a knowledge that "penetrates the reality of
> insight": such knowing is a firsthand acquaintance held in the heart — an inner (batin)
> recognition beneath the outward wording — rather than a store of facts that could be turned into
> a tool for worldly gain.
<!-- editorial:end -->

## 5. The Fruits of Certainty

The discourse to Kumayl continues: "They rejoice in their intimacy with the spirit of certainty." This sentence bears directly on the sheer happiness that flows from the life of the spirit. In the Arabic phrase *wa basharu ruh al-yaqin*, the primary sense of the word *bashara* is to have intimate experience of something, to come into direct physical contact with it. The verb in this form also carries the connotation of joy, so that "rejoice in intimate contact" better conveys what the single word holds. To touch the spirit of certainty is not to arrive at a cold logical fact, the conclusion of a purely mental process. This contact means entering deeply into the very object of one's certainty, which is ultimately nothing less than the divine itself. That reality is infinitely more beatific than any conceivable paradise — the Quran affirms that the divine *ridwan*, beatitude and contentment, is "greater" than Paradise — and so it cannot but bestow joy upon the soul that opens itself to it in earnest:

> وَعَدَ ٱللَّهُ ٱلْمُؤْمِنِينَ وَٱلْمُؤْمِنَتِ جَنَّتٍۢ تَجْرِى مِن تَحْتِهَا ٱلْأَنْهَرُ خَلِدِينَ فِيهَا وَمَسَكِنَ طَيِّبَةًۭ فِى جَنَّتِ عَدْنٍۢ ۚ وَرِضْوَنٌۭ مِّنَ ٱللَّهِ أَكْبَرُ ۚ ذَلِكَ هُوَ ٱلْفَوْزُ ٱلْعَظِيمُ

"God has promised the believers, men and women, gardens beneath which rivers flow, wherein they abide, and goodly dwellings in gardens of Eden. And beatitude from God is greater. That is the supreme triumph" (At-Tawbah: 72). In the words of Ali (ع), "Learning and reflecting upon knowledge is the delight of the knowers."

This is what one might call an epistemology of joy, for "the most joyous of mankind is the intellectual (*al-aqil*)." Such a joyous epistemology stands in sharp contrast to modern scientistic conceptions of knowledge as dry, cold, and abstract, and it merits careful attention, for it reveals a crucial dimension of the spiritual knowledge proper to the Imam's perspective, and indeed to all spiritual traditions worthy of the name. Engaging with truths ought to impart happiness, and it does so in the measure that the truths in question are fundamental to one's own being, or to Being as such. These two modes of being are ultimately identical, given the uniqueness, the "one-and-onliness," of Being. That which has no second, no other, cannot enter the category of number; being is indivisible. Hence the being of the individual cannot ultimately differ from Being as such, however great the existential disparity — or rather incommensurability — between the individual and God. True knowledge is knowledge of Being; it is therefore inevitably a form of self-discovery, even though knowledge of the Absolute is impossible without absolute self-abandonment. The highest discovery calls for the deepest effacement.

In the initial stages of this process, however, true knowledge as acquired by the intellectual is a kind of self-discovery that brings the happiness of a homecoming, a return from the exile of ignorance. This follows because "the most intellectual of people" is "the one who is closest to God," and "the one who is most alive." To be close to God is to be fully alive, and this true life gives profound joy — the *joie de vivre*, the sheer joy of living. It is a joy proper to existence as such, and independent of particular experiences that may or may not be pleasurable.

At this point the crucial relationship between life and consciousness comes into view: between the fruits of the in-spiration of God's spirit on the one hand, and this life of spiritual consciousness and joy on the other. Those who are true to the spirit of God within them genuinely possess joy. It is also by reason of their intimate and unshakeable certainty of the divine goodness and compassion that the true *faqih* — not so much "the jurist" as "one who truly understands" — is defined by the Imam as "he who does not make people despair of the mercy of God, and does not make them lose hope in the gracious spirit of God (*rawh Allah*)." So deeply is the sage immersed in the joyous certainty of God's mercy that there is no room for despair. To despair of this mercy is itself a mark of unbelief:

> قَالَ وَمَن يَقْنَطُ مِن رَّحْمَةِ رَبِّهِۦٓ إِلَّا ٱلضَّآلُّونَ

"And who despaireth of the mercy of his Lord except those who stray in error?" (Al-Hijr: 56). The divine assurances of mercy in the Quran come fully alive in the souls that possess a profound and not merely notional knowledge of the beatific nature of the divine Reality:

> وَٱكْتُبْ لَنَا فِى هَذِهِ ٱلدُّنْيَا حَسَنَةًۭ وَفِى ٱلْآخِرَةِ إِنَّا هُدْنَآ إِلَيْكَ ۚ قَالَ عَذَابِىٓ أُصِيبُ بِهِۦ مَنْ أَشَآءُ ۖ وَرَحْمَتِى وَسِعَتْ كُلَّ شَىْءٍۢ ۚ فَسَأَكْتُبُهَا لِلَّذِينَ يَتَّقُونَ وَيُؤْتُونَ ٱلزَّكَوٰةَ وَٱلَّذِينَ هُم بِـَٔايَتِنَا يُؤْمِنُونَ

"My mercy encompasseth all things" (Al-A'raf: 156), and

> وَإِذَا جَآءَكَ ٱلَّذِينَ يُؤْمِنُونَ بِـَٔايَتِنَا فَقُلْ سَلَمٌ عَلَيْكُمْ ۖ كَتَبَ رَبُّكُمْ عَلَىٰ نَفْسِهِ ٱلرَّحْمَةَ ۖ أَنَّهُۥ مَنْ عَمِلَ مِنكُمْ سُوٓءًۢا بِجَهَلَةٍۢ ثُمَّ تَابَ مِنۢ بَعْدِهِۦ وَأَصْلَحَ فَأَنَّهُۥ غَفُورٌۭ رَّحِيمٌۭ

"thy Lord has prescribed for Himself mercy" (Al-An'am: 54). These are the ones who truly understand, the true *fuqaha*.

This opening to joy through intimate knowledge of God's mercy is inseparable from beauty, the perception of which is another unexpected accompaniment of the intellect in the Imam's perspective. The appreciation of beauty is not merely a matter of subjective aesthetics; it is an objective dimension of the intellect's own function. Beauty also leads directly to the heart of ethics. The Arabic *ihsan* is often translated simply as "virtue" or "excellence," but one of its key literal meanings is "making beautiful," for *husn* means "beauty." Virtue, *ihsan*, can thus be understood as that which makes the soul beautiful. In this light the relationship among intellect, ethics, and aesthetics becomes far clearer. "The excellence of the intellect," says Ali (ع), "is in the beauty of things outward and inward (*jamal al-zawahir wal-bawatin*)." The outward beauty of forms and actions must be complemented by inward beauty — beauty of soul, or virtue in its most integral and penetrating sense. All of this follows naturally from two sayings attributed to the Prophet: "Truly God is beautiful and He loves beauty," and "Assume the character traits of God."

This cultivation of beautiful traits within oneself makes one lovable to God, since He loves beauty. The process of making oneself lovable to God is inseparable from emulating the virtues brought to perfection in the soul of the Prophet:

> قُلْ إِن كُنتُمْ تُحِبُّونَ ٱللَّهَ فَٱتَّبِعُونِى يُحْبِبْكُمُ ٱللَّهُ وَيَغْفِرْ لَكُمْ ذُنُوبَكُمْ ۗ وَٱللَّهُ غَفُورٌۭ رَّحِيمٌۭ

"Say: O ye who believe, if ye love God, follow me, the Prophet; God will love you" (Ali 'Imran: 31). This drawing of God's love through making oneself beautiful in the fullest sense is alluded to in another saying of the Imam: "The foundation of the intellect is the assimilation of love (*awwal al-aql al-tawaddud*)." Rather than referring simply to love as such (*wudd/mawadda*), the Imam draws attention to the need to make oneself lovable to God by using the form *tawaddud*. He alludes thereby to the process of integrating and realizing the principle of love within oneself, and to expressing outwardly the love that is experienced inwardly — love being appreciated at once as outward radiance and inner attraction.

To follow the Prophet, and thus make oneself receptive to the divine influx of love, means, among other things, cultivating the prophetic virtues. These virtues are prophetic not in the sense that they belong exclusively to the Prophet, but because he is held to have realized virtue in all its plenitude and is therefore the exemplar, par excellence, of virtue as such. Beauty and love are not the only qualities that articulate the intellect's receptivity to the divine Reality; kindness and contentment, courtesy, generosity and modesty, and wise forbearance (*hilm*) are equally essential. None of these moral qualities is absent from the soul of the true intellectual. There can be no wisdom or intellect in the fullest sense if the soul lacks them. It is precisely the presence of these virtues that lets the intellect develop the dimensions of depth and breadth. Without them it stays confined to the flat, horizontal plane of its own operations, to the activity of reason alone. Reason, until touched by faith and deepened by grace, remains unaware of what lies beneath the surface of its own field, oblivious of the hidden treasures of the intellect, and blind to the need to gather the whole soul within the mold of that integral, or unifying, virtue which bestows integrity upon intelligence. Such integration, this making one, is absolutely fundamental to تَوْحِيد, now grasped as a dynamic process of spiritual unification demanded by the very idea of God's oneness. Truth must clearly inspire the soul; but it is equally necessary that, conversely, virtue should aerate the intelligence, providing the leaven that allows it to expand to its full dimensions.

Sinful, immoral qualities are understood in this perspective not only as vices but as so many blind spots and intellectual dysfunctions. The heart is diseased by vice; its eye is blinded by these vices and thus rendered unable to see the Real. As the Quran says:

> فِى قُلُوبِهِم مَّرَضٌۭ فَزَادَهُمُ ٱللَّهُ مَرَضًۭا ۖ وَلَهُمْ عَذَابٌ أَلِيمٌۢ بِمَا كَانُوا۟ يَكْذِبُونَ

"In their hearts is a disease and God increased them in disease" (Al-Baqarah: 10), and

> أَفَلَمْ يَسِيرُوا۟ فِى ٱلْأَرْضِ فَتَكُونَ لَهُمْ قُلُوبٌۭ يَعْقِلُونَ بِهَآ أَوْ آذَانٌۭ يَسْمَعُونَ بِهَا ۖ فَإِنَّهَا لَا تَعْمَى ٱلْأَبْصَرُ وَلَكِن تَعْمَى ٱلْقُلُوبُ ٱلَّتِى فِى ٱلصُّدُورِ

"It is not the sights that are blind, but blind are the hearts" (Al-Hajj: 46).

The vices are all comprised within egotism, which is in turn often described simply as caprice (*hawa*). In one saying, the Imam speaks of the conquest of caprice as amounting to salvation: "He attains deliverance whose intellect dominates his caprice." Deliverance, *falah*, which carries the sense both of salvation and beatitude, is thus to some degree accessible here and now; it is not a grace granted only posthumously. This deliverance involves tasting the joy that emanates from certainty, from intimate contact with the spirit of that certainty. As for the means of cultivating receptivity to this spirit of certainty, what is required, over and above obedience to the rules and regulations of the revealed Law, is that combination of purity of intention and sincerity of devotion expressed by the word *ikhlas*. Without this sincerity or purity, compliance with formal rules is nothing but hypocrisy (*nifaq*) and pretension (*riya*), which the Imam in turn defines as the gravest of all sins, *shirk*, the attribution of partners to God: "Know that the slightest pretension (*riya*) is polytheism." This saying can be read as a commentary on one of the Quran's most important condemnations of hypocrisy. The verses in question refer to those who deny the essence of faith while outwardly conforming to it:

"Hast thou seen the one who belieth religion? He is the one who repelleth the orphan and urgeth not the feeding of the poor. So woe be to those who pray, those who are heedless of their prayer, who want only to be seen, and withhold small kindnesses" (Al-Ma'un: 1-7).

This short sura makes clear that formal acts of devotion have no value whatsoever unless they are integrated into a life of virtue, and unless they are performed with the right intention, that is, with *ikhlas*. It also helps explain why the Imam's ethos is described here not merely as religious but as spiritual: it takes the ideals of religion to their most profound application. To be spiritual in this sense is precisely to be true to one's intellect, true to the treasures buried deep within it, and not merely to the rational functions operating on its surface. Without this dimension of depth, religion is meaningless. "There is no religion for one who has no intellect," says the Imam. Likewise: "Action without knowledge is error." What counts is the intention motivating action, for "action, all action, is dust, except what is purified within it." This detachment from action as such, and the orientation instead toward an inner state of being from which good actions cannot but emerge, clearly reflects the Quranic teaching:

> لَن يَنَالَ ٱللَّهَ لُحُومُهَا وَلَا دِمَآؤُهَا وَلَكِن يَنَالُهُ ٱلتَّقْوَىٰ مِنكُمْ ۚ كَذَلِكَ سَخَّرَهَا لَكُمْ لِتُكَبِّرُوا۟ ٱللَّهَ عَلَىٰ مَا هَدَىٰكُمْ ۗ وَبَشِّرِ ٱلْمُحْسِنِينَ

"It is not their sacrificed flesh nor their blood that reacheth God, but it is piety from you that reacheth Him" (Al-Hajj: 37).

"There is no religion for one who has no intellect." No group perhaps better exemplified the principle in this saying than the Kharijites. Among those who rebelled against the Imam were numerous readers (*qurra*, sing. *qari*) of the Quran, people who enjoyed considerable religious prestige on account of their Quranic knowledge and intense worship. Their piety, however, was more apparent than real; it was, in fact, a zeal of bitterness, a caricature of true piety. When his supporters mentioned the long night vigils performed by members of this group, the Imam gave a sharp reply: "Sleeping with certainty is better than praying with doubt." Certainty imparts peace and joy; thus sleep, for one immersed in what the Imam calls the spirit of certainty, is a bodily repose that only deepens the happiness flowing from a good conscience and spiritual certitude — the latter understood as a kind of permanent wakefulness, in the sense of being awake to higher realities. Bodily sleep does not, therefore, contradict spiritual wakefulness. As the hadith of the Prophet has it: "My eyes sleep, but my heart sleeps not."

In relation to formal worship, the true intellectual, the *'aqil*, whom the Imam defines as the one who puts each thing in its proper place (العاقل من وضع شئی حق محلہ), is one who obeys the divine injunction to pray. But rather than praying in mechanical fashion and supposing that the action alone suffices, he knows that such obedience is a necessary but not sufficient condition for achieving receptivity to grace. There must be consciousness of what one is doing and why one is doing it; it is this awareness, together with virtue — inward beauty — that attracts divine acceptance. "The most worthy knowledge for you," says the Imam, "is that by virtue of whose presence action is accepted by God."

"They make easy what the extravagant find harsh; they befriend that by which the ignorant are estranged." This sentence bears, among other things, on how the remembrance of death — and the renunciation of this world that it makes necessary — marks the soul of the true sage. In the first place, what the extravagant — the avid, worldly types — find harsh is outward adversity, difficulty, and deprivation. These are all circumstances which the Quran views as the trials that inevitably accompany life in this world:

"And We shall try you with something of fear and hunger and loss of property and life and fruits; but give glad tidings to the patient, those who, when struck by affliction say: 'Verily, we belong to God and verily unto Him we are ever-returning.' These are the ones upon whom there are blessings from their Lord, and mercy; and these are the ones who are rightly guided" (Al-Baqarah: 155-157).

The wise, the rightly guided (*al-muhtadun*), see the blessings poured out upon those who bear affliction with patience — a patience that grows from their knowledge of the truth: "By the Age, truly man is in a state of loss, except those who believe and do virtuous deeds and exhort one another to truth and exhort one another to patience" (Al-'Asr: 1-3). These are the believers who stand firm under trial, rooted in their awareness of where they come from and where they are going, and in the knowledge that unto Him we are ever-returning. For this reason they can make the trials and difficulties of life easy to bear, whereas the extravagant meet them only as harshness and bitterness. The ease that lies within every difficulty is hidden from the ignorant but discerned by the wise: "Verily, with hardship cometh ease; verily with hardship cometh ease" (Ash-Sharh: 5-6). The wise do more than expect ease as the certain fruit of hardship; they taste that fruit already, as the seed hidden within the hardship itself, for they befriend the very thing that estranges the ignorant.

One layer of meaning here is lit up by this saying of Ali (ع): "Death is more intimate to me than the breast of the mother is to the suckling babe." In the verse cited above, the mention of trials and difficulties comes just after a reminder that those slain in the path of God are not truly dead, "nay, they are alive but ye perceive not." This is not merely an affirmation of the life hereafter; it also points to the esoteric meaning of being slain for God: *al-fana fiLlah*, extinction in God. The Imam's intimacy with death can be understood in the light of this death undergone while still alive, to which he points in another saying: "I am the death of the dead." Because life in the world is itself a kind of death — a limit, and so a privation, set against the infinite plenitude of the divine realms — to die to this death amounts to true life:

> وَمَا هَذِهِ ٱلْحَيَوٰةُ ٱلدُّنْيَآ إِلَّا لَهْوٌۭ وَلَعِبٌۭ ۚ وَإِنَّ ٱلدَّارَ ٱلْآخِرَةَ لَهِىَ ٱلْحَيَوَانُ ۚ لَوْ كَانُوا۟ يَعْلَمُونَ

"And the life of this world is nothing but sport and play," says the Quran, "and verily, the abode of the Hereafter, that is true life, did they but know" (Al-'Ankabut: 64).

It is not the physical world as such that is to be negated; what must be overcome is worldliness, the egotistic attachment to the world. One of the central principles of the Imam's outlook, stressed again and again throughout the *Nahj al-balagha*, bears directly on this: the principle of *zuhd*, which no single English word can render adequately. It gathers several meanings: asceticism, austerity, abstention, detachment, doing without, and making little of. The following passage from the *Nahj* conveys well the attitude of the *zahid* toward this world:

The world is an abode for which annihilation is ordained, and for its people departure from it is decreed. It is sweet and green, and it hastens to one who seeks it, and confounds the heart that gazes upon it. So set out from it with the best of the provision available to you, and ask not from it above what suffices and seek not from it more than what can be attained.

What is this best of provision, this *zad*? In many places in the *Nahj* it is named *taqwa*, another word that resists simple translation, its meaning taking in piety, fear of God, awe, and God-consciousness. "I exhort you, O slaves of God, to *taqwa*, which is the provision," says the Imam. This provision for the next world is attained only in the measure of one's detachment from the perishable aspects of the life of this world, for "whoso desireth that which hasteneth away, in that We hasten for him what We will":

> مَّن كَانَ يُرِيدُ ٱلْعَاجِلَةَ عَجَّلْنَا لَهُۥ فِيهَا مَا نَشَآءُ لِمَن نُّرِيدُ ثُمَّ جَعَلْنَا لَهُۥ جَهَنَّمَ يَصْلَىٰهَا مَذْمُومًۭا مَّدْحُورًۭا
>
> "Whoever desires the fleeting life, We hasten for him therein what We will, for whom We intend; then We appoint for him Hell, wherein he burns, disgraced and rejected." (Al-Isra: 18)

The Imam describes the despicable side of the world in vivid terms. "By God, this world of yours is more contemptible in my eyes than a chewed-up bone of a pig in the hand of a leper." "An image of the world is the snake, soft to touch, but virulent poison within." The world is "as worthless to me as that which drips from the nose of a goat." It is the grandeur and beauty of God that renders the world insignificant and distasteful; only in the light of the Absolute do the pleasures and attractions of the world appear fleeting and illusory: "The greatness of the Creator for you belittles creatures in your view." In a similar vein, the Imam speaks of a brother in God: "What made him great in my eyes was the smallness of the world in his eyes (*kana yuazzimuhu fi 'ayni sighar al-dunya fi aynihi*)."

This, according to the Quranic usage of *zahid*, is the real meaning of *zuhd*. In the sura named after Joseph (*Yusuf*), the brothers of Yusuf held him of little value, selling him for a small price, and they are described in relation to him as *zahidin*:

> وَشَرَوْهُ بِثَمَنٍۭ بَخْسٍۢ دَرَهِمَ مَعْدُودَةٍۢ وَكَانُوا۟ فِيهِ مِنَ ٱلزَّهِدِينَ
>
> "And they sold him for a paltry price, a few dirhams counted out, and they were of those who deemed him of little worth." (Yusuf: 20)

In the Imam's perspective, the world is likewise deemed of little value — not worthless altogether, but rendered all but insignificant beside the Hereafter, beside eternity, beside ultimate reality. All created things are reduced to infinitesimal, fleeting shadows destined for annihilation: "Everything perishes except His Face":

> وَلَا تَدْعُ مَعَ ٱللَّهِ إِلَهًا آخَرَ ۘ لَآ إِلَهَ إِلَّا هُوَ ۚ كُلُّ شَىْءٍ هَالِكٌ إِلَّا وَجْهَهُۥ ۚ لَهُ ٱلْحُكْمُ وَإِلَيْهِ تُرْجَعُونَ
>
> "And do not invoke with God another deity. There is no deity except Him. Everything will perish except His Face. His is the judgment, and to Him you will be returned." (Al-Qasas: 88)

Yet the world is not deprived of all value; indeed, its significance lies precisely in what it cannot help but signify: "And there is nothing that does not hymn His glory with praise":

> تُسَبِّحُ لَهُ ٱلسَّمَوَتُ ٱلسَّبْعُ وَٱلْأَرْضُ وَمَن فِيهِنَّ ۚ وَإِن مِّن شَىْءٍ إِلَّا يُسَبِّحُ بِحَمْدِهِۦ وَلَكِن لَّا تَفْقَهُونَ تَسْبِيحَهُمْ ۗ إِنَّهُۥ كَانَ حَلِيمًا غَفُورًۭا
>
> "The seven heavens and the earth and whatever is in them exalt Him. And there is not a thing except that it exalts Him by His praise, but you do not understand their way of exalting. Indeed, He is ever Forbearing and Forgiving." (Al-Isra: 44)

Everything in the world, every creature in existence, proclaims the infinite creativity of the Creator. So that the imagination might fathom the mystery of this universal praise, the Quran says: "And the birds in flight, each one knoweth its prayer and glorification":

> أَلَمْ تَرَ أَنَّ ٱللَّهَ يُسَبِّحُ لَهُۥ مَن فِى ٱلسَّمَوَتِ وَٱلْأَرْضِ وَٱلطَّيْرُ صَٓفَّتٍۢ ۖ كُلٌّۭ قَدْ عَلِمَ صَلَاتَهُۥ وَتَسْبِيحَهُۥ ۗ وَٱللَّهُ عَلِيمٌۢ بِمَا يَفْعَلُونَ
>
> "Do you not see that God is exalted by whoever is within the heavens and the earth and by the birds with wings spread in flight? Each one has known its prayer and its exalting, and God is Knowing of what they do." (An-Nur: 41)

There is therefore no contradiction in the Imam despising the world and yet waxing lyrical in his descriptions of the beauties of virgin nature, among them that unsurpassable marvel, the peacock. This comes in a sermon that also includes descriptions of birds in general, as well as ants, serpents, and elephants. The sermon closes with a galvanizing description of Paradise, setting the marvels of this world beside the delights of the next; in the light of the next, all terrestrial phenomena pale into insignificance. For once the eye of the heart is turned toward Paradise, "your soul would become averse to the marvels of this world, its passions, its pleasures and its embellished scenery."

It might seem contradictory to bring together such apparently opposed qualities as joy in certainty, discussed above, and the befriending of hardship. Yet the second is in fact the fruit of the first. Every difficulty without exception is borne through the spiritual resources that faith opens up when it is joined with intellectual assimilation — or rather, in the measure that faith is assimilated by the intellect and so becomes *marifa* or gnosis, a certainty so deep and so complete that "even if the veil be lifted" it cannot increase, as the famous saying noted earlier has it. It is possible to believe, at one level of the mind, that there is no god but God. The Imam, however, draws attention to what this apparently simple belief should imply when it is sincere or pure: "I bear witness that there is no god but God alone Who has no partner, a testimony whose sincerity has been tested and whose essence has been assimilated with conviction (*mutaqadan musaffaha*)."

The word *musaffa* refers to the choicest and purest part of any substance, its very essence. The root *massa* means "to suck," the idea being that the liquid or juice of a substance or fruit holds its essence, that which is extracted from it. Here the Imam is stressing the spiritual substance of the testimony, as distinct from mere verbal affirmation or mental comprehension of the words. He affirms a heartfelt connection with the reality to which the testimony bears witness; the words that define it are an echo of the reality they convey, and are not to be confused with that reality itself. He continues: "We cling to it always, for as long as we endure, storing it up as protection against the terrors that will befall us. For truly, it renders faith resolute, opens up spiritual virtue, pleases *al-Rahman* (the All-Compassionate), repels *al-shaytan* (the devil)."

To return to "befriending that by which the ignorant are estranged": this points not only to the trials of life, which strengthen the wise and repel the ignorant, but also to the inescapability of death. For the wise, death means release, the encounter with God, blissful eternity — in short, a mode of تَوْحِيد, in which the soul enjoys ever deeper degrees of integration within higher principles. For the ignorant, by contrast, death is perceived as a frightful disintegration, the end of their world rather than the beginning of endless life. There is also the prefiguration of death in life already noted above: *fana*, "extinction," "effacement," the "perishing" of egoistic consciousness before the Absolute. The ignorant recoil in horror at the prospect of losing what they falsely take to be their life, not realizing that this sacrifice of the egoistic and limited self is the very ground on which infinite consciousness is glimpsed or tasted. One can only taste the ocean of the Real by being drowned in it.

The means to this ultimate attainment is likewise embraced by the wise and feared by the ignorant, namely, the act of pure prayer, total concentration on the Real. This concentration strictly precludes all worldly or egotistic preoccupations; the sheer intensity of focus upon the Absolute burns away thoughts of the relative, and for those who ignorantly identify with relativities, both inward and outward, this amounts to a kind of death. For the wise, by contrast, this concentration on the Absolute, the unsullied remembrance of God, is a joyous contemplation of reality before which the ego is displaced and all illusions are dispelled. This leads directly to the next sentence of the passage: "With their bodies, they keep company with the world, while their spirits are tied to the transcendent realm."

This sentence becomes more concrete when read alongside another well-known expression of the Imam. He describes himself as belonging to a group whose spiritual degree is such that "their hearts are in the Gardens of Paradise while their bodies are at work." To be tied or attached to that realm — which encompasses the paradisal abodes and reaches up to the divine Presence itself — means that they already dwell within that transcendent realm more fully than within this earthly one. This helps explain the saying quoted earlier: God keeps man perpetually in that which his soul desires. Those whose hearts or spirits are in the transcendent domain are those whose consciousness is determined by transcendent realities in substance, not merely in concept. Those whose consciousness, by contrast, is shaped by worldly concerns are in this world in the sense of being enclosed by its formal limitations; their orientation and imagination are trapped within a worldly framework. Their actions bear the mark of this worldliness, whereas the actions of the wise are the fruits of their immersion in a higher reality. One might say that their outward actions in the world belie their inward transcendence of all action; they act, yet are not bound by the fruits of their action. As noted earlier, they know that action, all action, is dust, except what is purified, accomplished sincerely, within it.

At this point the notion of the Hereafter deserves consideration within the Imam's ethical framework. The Hereafter is repeatedly invoked in the Imam's discourses. Is this simply a matter of holding out a heavenly reward and threatening a hellish punishment in order to enjoin the good and forbid the evil? Even if the answer is partly affirmative, there is a deeper dimension to this apparently straightforward use of divine warnings and promises as levers for moral action.

The ethical orientation here is defined not merely by belief in God but by the spiritual reality of the Hereafter as well, both objectively, in itself, and subjectively, insofar as it is assimilated by the individual. It thereby becomes a dynamic dimension, a process, an orientation, a reflux, within the believer's own soul, rather than a bare belief or hope. Anticipation of the Hereafter characterizes every believer in principle; its realization characterizes the soul of the *wali Allah*, the friend of God, the saint and the sage. As noted earlier, the Imam speaks in a famous sentence that bears witness to his total immersion in the reality of which he is certain: "Were the veil to be removed, I would not increase in certitude (*law kushifal-ghita, ma azdadtu yaqinan*)." The anticipation — or better, the foretaste — of the Hereafter deepens rather than diminishes in proportion to one's virtue, piety, and sanctity; it does not shrink into a merely heuristic device whose value is exhausted once its moral impact is achieved. The following moving passage from the *Nahj* deserves reflection in this light. What appears here is the description of a blissful vision of the reality of Paradise, not merely a rhetorical flourish in the service of moral exhortation:

If you were to cast the eye of your heart toward what is described for you of the Garden, your soul would become averse to the marvels of this world, its passions, its pleasures and its embellished scenery. And your soul would be rapt in contemplation of the swaying trees whose roots lie hidden in dunes of musk on the banks of the rivers of the Garden, and the clusters of dazzling pearls hanging down from the branches of its trees …

It is, of course, true that the highest degree of spirituality transcends the desire for Paradise and the fear of Hell, along with the moral conduct proportioned to them. But this means that when such higher degrees of realization are attained, the soul's state can properly be described as paradisal, that is, as being already so utterly content in the beatific presence of God that it desires nothing more. This state of soul is called in the Quran *al-nafs al-mutmainna*, "the soul at peace in absolute certainty":

> يَٓأَيَّتُهَا ٱلنَّفْسُ ٱلْمُطْمَئِنَّةُ
>
> "O reassured soul." (Al-Fajr: 27)

The Imam frequently echoes, implicitly if not explicitly, the Quranic principle that "the Hereafter is better and more lasting":

> وَٱلْآخِرَةُ خَيْرٌۭ وَأَبْقَىٰٓ
>
> "But the Hereafter is better and more enduring." (Al-A'la: 17)

The Hereafter is not only the time at which divine warnings and promises reach their consummation; it is also the space in which all positive qualities, human, angelic, divine, are manifested in their fullness, those qualities that appear on earth as reflections of higher realities and that encompass and subtly penetrate the entire sphere of terrestrial existence. The Quran, perhaps more than any other scripture, furnishes the imagination with numerous and complex descriptions of paradisal bliss. One of the key functions of these descriptions is alluded to in the Quran itself: "Assuredly, We purify them with a pure quality, remembrance of the Abode of the Hereafter":

> إِنَّآ أَخْلَصْنَهُم بِخَالِصَةٍۢ ذِكْرَى ٱلدَّارِ
>
> "Indeed, We chose them for an exclusive quality: remembrance of the Home of the Hereafter." (Sad: 46)

The mere thought of Paradise is itself a purification of the mind and heart, a means of guarding the soul against the ever-present temptation to seek its ultimate happiness and well-being in this world alone. Conversely, one's well-being in the herebelow, in regard to action, lies in the performance of good deeds, and such well-being is proportioned to the depth of one's belief that "Whatever good ye send before for your souls, ye will find it with God, better and more tremendous as reward":

> إِنَّ رَبَّكَ يَعْلَمُ أَنَّكَ تَقُومُ أَدْنَىٰ مِن ثُلُثَىِ ٱلَّيْلِ وَنِصْفَهُۥ وَثُلُثَهُۥ وَطَآئِفَةٌۭ مِّنَ ٱلَّذِينَ مَعَكَ ۚ وَٱللَّهُ يُقَدِّرُ ٱلَّيْلَ وَٱلنَّهَارَ ۚ عَلِمَ أَن لَّن تُحْصُوهُ فَتَابَ عَلَيْكُمْ ۖ فَٱقْرَءُوا۟ مَا تَيَسَّرَ مِنَ ٱلْقُرْآنِ ۚ عَلِمَ أَن سَيَكُونُ مِنكُم مَّرْضَىٰ ۙ وَآخَرُونَ يَضْرِبُونَ فِى ٱلْأَرْضِ يَبْتَغُونَ مِن فَضْلِ ٱللَّهِ ۙ وَآخَرُونَ يُقَتِلُونَ فِى سَبِيلِ ٱللَّهِ ۖ فَٱقْرَءُوا۟ مَا تَيَسَّرَ مِنْهُ ۚ وَأَقِيمُوا۟ ٱلصَّلَوٰةَ وَآتُوا۟ ٱلزَّكَوٰةَ وَأَقْرِضُوا۟ ٱللَّهَ قَرْضًا حَسَنًۭا ۚ وَمَا تُقَدِّمُوا۟ لِأَنفُسِكُم مِّنْ خَيْرٍۢ تَجِدُوهُ عِندَ ٱللَّهِ هُوَ خَيْرًۭا وَأَعْظَمَ أَجْرًۭا ۚ وَٱسْتَغْفِرُوا۟ ٱللَّهَ ۖ إِنَّ ٱللَّهَ غَفُورٌۭ رَّحِيمٌۢ
>
> "And whatever good you put forward for yourselves, you will find it with God. It is better and greater in reward." (Al-Muzzammil: 20)

In other words, a sincere orientation toward the Hereafter is transformed into a perception of heavenly beatitude already in the herebelow, in the form of those very acts and attitudes which, being good, share the same nature as the felicity to which they lead. In this light, one who sees beyond the veil of this world grasps morally good actions and noble intentions as vivid prefigurations of heavenly realities, not merely as ethical prerequisites for salvation. Goodness, in other words, does not simply lead to a heavenly reward; it already participates in that reward: "Is the reward of goodness (*ihsan*) aught but goodness?":

> هَلْ جَزَآءُ ٱلْإِحْسَنِ إِلَّا ٱلْإِحْسَنُ
>
> "Is the reward for good anything but good?" (Ar-Rahman: 60)

The Imam's saying, "The fruit of good action is like its root," can be read as an echo of the idea that the end is already present in the means that lead to it. Fitting to close is the following verse of the Quran, which also serves as an introduction to what follows:

> وَبَشِّرِ ٱلَّذِينَ آمَنُوا۟ وَعَمِلُوا۟ ٱلصَّلِحَتِ أَنَّ لَهُمْ جَنَّتٍۢ تَجْرِى مِن تَحْتِهَا ٱلْأَنْهَرُ ۖ كُلَّمَا رُزِقُوا۟ مِنْهَا مِن ثَمَرَةٍۢ رِّزْقًۭا ۙ قَالُوا۟ هَذَا ٱلَّذِى رُزِقْنَا مِن قَبْلُ ۖ وَأُتُوا۟ بِهِۦ مُتَشَبِهًۭا ۖ وَلَهُمْ فِيهَآ أَزْوَجٌۭ مُّطَهَّرَةٌۭ ۖ وَهُمْ فِيهَا خَلِدُونَ
>
> "And give good tidings to those who believe and perform virtuous deeds, that for them are Gardens underneath which rivers flow. Every time they are given to eat from the fruits thereof, they say: This is what we were given to eat before. And they were given the like thereof." (Al-Baqarah: 25)

The fruits of the paradisal gardens are thus experienced already in the herebelow, in the form of all goodness, beauty, and truth, and the diverse modes of happiness that flow from them. All such experiences are so many foretastes of the ultimate beatitude in Paradise. On the ethical plane, the performance of good deeds — and, even more directly, the realization of intrinsic virtue, *ihsan* — is therefore not simply a prerequisite for posthumous salvation, but is already a kind of deliverance, here and now: a deliverance from the imprisonment of sin on the outward plane, and from the bondage of egocentricity on the inward plane. This theme of salvation here and now, grounded in unshakeable certitude, is fundamental to the spirit of the Imam's teachings and will be explored further throughout the rest of this book. In the following chapter, the cardinal virtue of justice will be considered within Ali (ع)'s perspective, rooted as it is in *ihsan*, a virtue defined by the Prophet as worshipping God as if one saw Him, and concretely affirmed by the Imam as real and not merely hypothetical, for God is indeed seen by the pure of heart through the verities of faith.

<!-- editorial:begin -->
> **Editorial note (tradition-grounded).** 
> The chapter's closing turn on *ihsan* rests on a resonance within the Arabic itself: in the root
> that yields both *husn* (beauty) and *ihsan*, goodness and beauty are a single word, so to do
> well is already to make something beautiful. The wider tradition preserves *ihsan*'s fullest
> sense as the highest of these forms — to worship and act as though seeing God, giving every deed
> its inward beauty — which quietly answers the chapter's own opening, since to touch the spirit
> of certainty is precisely to enter the presence of the One that certainty beholds. A related
> thread runs through the vocabulary of joy: the same root behind *bashara*, the intimate contact
> named here, also yields *bushra*, glad tidings — good news being what visibly brightens the face
> — so the fusion of touch and gladness the chapter finds in that one word is written into its
> very stem.
<!-- editorial:end -->

## 6. A Sacred Conception of Justice

The discussion of justice that follows does not speculate in the abstract about what justice means in the letter Ali (ع) wrote to Malik al-Ashtar fourteen centuries ago. Nor does it offer a dictionary definition, where justice means little more than fairness, equality, and fair play. Its first aim is to bring justice into alignment with the sacred, and with the reality of the sacred One, القدس. Justice carries a sacred and spiritual dimension that present-day discussion so often overlooks. At the same time, a careful sense of what the sacred means opens a deeper understanding of where justice is rooted, in the divine nature itself, and a clearer awareness of why justice is needed, both in the world and in the soul.

The central argument is this. In Ali (ع)'s view, a person's ethical orientation toward justice grows immeasurably deeper once it is consciously joined to the spiritual precepts of the Islamic faith. The spirit behind moral rules and ethical values is strengthened by a commitment to transcendent principles — even though, or precisely because, those principles reach beyond the realm of action in which the rules themselves apply. The شریعۃ can be pictured as having a body and a spirit, much as a person does. Its body is made up of actions — prayer, fasting, the paying of زکوۃ — while the spirit that gives those actions life rests on the nature of the divine, which is justice. To follow the شریعۃ is therefore to train in becoming like the divine through the principles of justice, so that action must be completed by contemplation of the divine. Here contemplation and action complete each other rather than clash, and this belongs to the very heart of the Islamic message of تَوْحِيد, توحید — an integrating oneness that makes theory and practice a single thing, so that a person becomes a true موحد. This unity shines with dazzling clarity in the life of the Imam, as it does in the life of the Prophet. The harmony between these two principles — contemplation and action, being and doing, theory and practice, the ideal and the real — runs all through the remarkable document these reflections consider.

These reflections stand against the background of present-day ethical debate, and may be read as a modest contribution, from an Islamic point of view, to the scholarly discussion of virtue and ethics. They should be read, in particular, in light of one of the most serious and far-reaching critiques of modern and postmodern ideas of virtue in general, and of justice in particular: that of Alasdair MacIntyre. Put simply, he holds that for several centuries ethical thought in Western society has drifted far from its moorings in absolute values. He argues, compellingly, that debates about morality and ethics, along with their philosophical foundations, are no longer grounded in any conception of 'the good life' or of 'the Good' as such. As a result, he holds, various shades of relativism, nihilism, utilitarianism, and opportunism have become, in practice, the shifting ground on which ethical values are formed — if they are formed at all. Relativism holds that knowledge, truth, and morality are not absolute but relative, existing only in relation to a culture, a society, or a historical moment. Nihilism rejects all religious and moral principles and takes life to be essentially meaningless. Utilitarianism counts an action right only if it is useful or benefits the majority, so that good and bad rest on nothing deeper than a calculation of greater benefit or loss. And opportunism seizes whatever advantage the moment offers, with no regard for plan or principle — the way of the manipulator, who flatters, performs, or deceives to get what he wants.

Whatever value these human-made theories sometimes yield, none of it is rooted in the divine. So even where they happen to mirror the values of faith, they stand on nothing; they are without spirit, dead corpses. Practicing virtue case by case is not really virtue at all. It is a series of practical adjustments to changing circumstances, made in the absence of any genuine principle of virtue. The virtue of truthfulness shows this. If a person resolves always to speak the truth but never grasps the reason underneath it — that truth is the very nature of our Creator, الحق, the absolute truth, and that our purpose in this world is to take on the virtues of our Creator — then truthfulness will in the end turn situational and self-serving, something to be given up whenever circumstances seem to call for it, spoken when it profits and abandoned when it costs. But once truth is seen as the nature of the divine, it becomes everlasting and eternal, indifferent to consequence, because a person speaks it in emulation of the immortal nature of God. The virtue itself becomes immortal, and the need to practice it case by case falls away. 'We are looking,' writes MacIntyre, 'for a conception of the good which will enable us to order other goods, for a conception of the good which will enable us to extend our understanding of the purpose and content of the virtues, for a conception of the good which will enable us to understand the place of integrity and consistency in life ...'

It is at this level, above all, that the insights of Ali (ع) and other sages of the Islamic tradition become especially relevant. MacIntyre's own answer to the crisis is a reworking of an Aristotelian ethics set within a Thomistic worldview, and it shares a great deal with the Islamic tradition of ethical thought (akhlaq). But one can argue for more than that: a careful engagement with the Imam's legacy yields far more than a merely theoretical approach to justice, and in two respects. First, the Imam was himself a model of justice, and so a living embodiment of this virtue and other fundamental ones. His life shows a perfect, indeed unflinching, adherence to this cardinal virtue — a life ruled not by his نفس but entirely by the divine nature of God. The remarkable actions recorded of him, at which one marvels, emerge as the fruit of his irresistible attraction toward the nature of الحق, for his whole existence was perfectly oriented toward God. His focus was never on the action itself but on the source of the principles behind it, so that, fixed upon the beauty of the divine nature and striving to take it on, he produced good actions naturally and inevitably. Although this work does not go into many details of his life and its lessons, it will recall certain incidents and events that help illustrate the point. Second, and more important here, the discourses, letters, and sayings attributed to Ali (ع) offer a rich source for reflecting on the living spirituality that lies at the root of a life of virtue. They afford a glimpse of a way of life wholly governed by the sacred, one in which the desire for the 'good life' emerges as the fruit of an irresistible attraction toward the Good as such — which is identical to the absolutely Real, al-Haqq.

In other words, a person is ethically inspired by 'the Good' in direct proportion to how far his very being is oriented toward the Real. If moral rules rest on ethical outlooks, and those outlooks rest on a conception of the 'good life', then that conception of the Good comes in turn from the moral agent's most basic understanding of what life means. Amyn B. Sajoo, in a recent thoughtful work on Muslim ethics, draws a careful distinction between ethics and morals. He argues that ethics is concerned more with conceptions of 'the good', while morals bear more specifically on rules about right and wrong. Ethics deals with the soul and the spirit of the good, the باطن, and it is out of ethics that morals are born. 'Certainly,' he continues, 'the concepts overlap substantially in theory and practice; but it is well to bear in mind the more encompassing sense of reality that typifies ethics.' Setting ethics inside the wider framework of spiritual virtue, in the sense of ihsan, احسان, widens the scope of both ethics and morality still further. It lends both a dimension of existential depth and grounds them in a more organic and all-embracing framework. The spiritual life has three components: اسلام, which supplies the outer aspects of morality; ایمان, which is the framework of ethics; and the union of the two, serving as the principle beneath them, which issues in احسان — the good life, the beautiful life. Abstract questions of right and wrong come to rest on the more decisive principle that emerges in answer to a single question: what is reality?

The previous chapter's discussion of the deeper meanings of divine unity bears on this. In the Imam's spiritual vision of تَوْحِيد, a person perceives, according to his capacity, an ultimate reality that not only 'has no second' but also has no moral 'opposite'. People tend to picture the world as good against evil, God against شیطان, always thinking in opposites. The Imam teaches, however, that God has no opposite at all. ابلیس does not stand against God, for nothing has the capacity to stand against God. He stands against God's creation, and his whole effort is to drive people away from their inborn divine nature by making its opposite beautiful — arrogance where God's nature is modest, lewdness where it is decent, boastfulness where it is humble. For the goodness that belongs to God is not the moral opposite of evil. It occupies an entirely different plane, that of al-Haqq, الحق, the absolutely Real, whose only 'opposite' is unreality, or nothingness. Goodness is at one with the pure positivity of the Absolute, while evil derives solely from a negative capacity to negate the Real, the Good. In other words, the essence of all goodness and beauty is identified with the Creator, while evil is a mode of the created — the corrupted state of a creation that has strayed from the divine nature. This is strongly implied in the verse of سورۃ الفلق that calls the believer to seek refuge in God from 'the evil of that which He created' (Al-Falaq: 2):

> قُلْ أَعُوذُ بِرَبِّ ٱلْفَلَقِ ۱ مِن شَرِّ مَا خَلَقَ ۲

'Say: I seek refuge with the Lord of the daybreak, from the evil of what He has created.'

This verse is often mistranslated as 'from the evil which He created', which is wrong both theologically and lexically. The expression 'min sharri ma khalaqa', مِن شَرِّ مَا خَلَقَ, clearly refers to the evil that derives from creation, not to creation as such. In theological terms, God creates morally free agents, and it is they who author evil in creation. When creation chooses, through its own free will, to negate the divine nature of its Creator, evil is born. God may be said to 'create' all actions only in the metaphysical sense that He is the source of all being — and so of every mode within being, of which action is one. He cannot, however, be said to have created evil as such, only the conditions within which evil arises. Evil is thus the negation of the Good, which is at one with the Real, and so evil has no ultimate ground in being. It is only a temporary straying from that pure and divine nature, an illusion seen by the 'eye of the heart'.

The verse, then, can be read as a call to seek refuge in the Real from the unreal, since goodness and evil share no common measure in being. Goodness can be opposed only by the appearance of evil — an appearance that the 'eye of the heart' grasps as illusion, that eye which truly 'sees' God through 'the verities of faith'. This vision of the merely relative reality of evil, far from breeding indifference toward it, greatly strengthens the spiritual resolve to overcome it, for the moral effort to be good grows in proportion to one's conviction of being accountable to that goodness. This conviction, in turn, is carried inward from mind to heart by the certainty that goodness is 'more real' than evil; and that certainty is itself nourished by the spiritual vision of the good as the Real. Seeing that good and evil cannot be measured against each other therefore leads not to indifference before evil, but to an orientation toward the good that grips one's whole being. In the Imam's view, the notions of 'right' and of 'rightful due', both held within the meaning of haqq, حق, take on an air of absoluteness. To understand what is right leads to a commitment to fulfill one's obligations; what is rightfully due is the restoration of reality through the removal of illusory evil. Both alike are charged with reality, which is justice. The moral notions of rectitude and propriety express a principle that lies beyond morality, a principle belonging to ultimate reality. One's commitment to fulfilling one's obligations, to giving every being its rightful due, likewise becomes charged with reality. The resolve to be just is made more absolute by the very absoluteness that belongs to the Real.

This duty to give each and every being its due connects to the plainest definition of justice the Imam gives, وَضَعَ الشَّئُی حَقَّ مَحَلِّہِ, 'Justice puts everything in its right place.' A person is 'just' insofar as he gives everything its proper due, rendering the haqq owed to each person, indeed to each and every thing in existence. The capacity to be 'just' in this demanding and universal sense grows in proportion to one's attunement to al-Haqq, the Real. As the previous chapter makes clear, this spiritual conception of virtue not only covers one's duty to all, but engages all that one is. One has to be good before one can do good, and the only way 'to be' is to be at one with the Real, which alone truly 'is'.

The Imam's comment on justice recalls the more famous definition of Plato: ...we have laid down, as a universal principle, that everyone ought to perform the one function in the community for which his nature best suited him... that principle, or some form of it, is justice. There are indeed many remarkable parallels between Plato's *Republic* and the Imam's letter to Malik al-Ashtar. Some of the more obvious correspondences deserve attention, along with the way the Imam's explicit spirituality illuminates what stays implicit in Plato's philosophy: how a person can actually attain the vision of the Real, which Plato calls 'gazing upon the Good'. It is this contemplation of the Good as such that serves as the source of one's capacity to act ethically at every level, for it lets one take the Good 'as a pattern for the right ordering of the state and of the individual'.

The influence of Platonic and Neoplatonic ideas of justice upon the shaping of ethical thought in Islam is well attested in the sources. Within that thought, justice is not one virtue among several. It is the perfection, or the sum, of all the other virtues — above all the three chief Platonic virtues of temperance, courage, and wisdom, which must govern, respectively, the appetitive, the spirited, and the rational dimensions of the soul. When all the dimensions of the soul, each "filled out" by its proper virtue, stand in their right place, the soul may be called "just."

What has received too little attention, however, is a spiritual correspondence: between Plato's chief means of arriving at the vision of the Good, anamnesis, and the central role of dhikr (ذِکْر) in Islam. Both words may be rendered by the English "remembrance." This same remembrance is what achieves justice and unlocks its power — the recollection of ultimate reality, and of unchanging principles at their several levels of application. It is not a matter of learning something new, but of recollecting principles already inherent in the very substance of the intellect: principles by which all things can be understood, and which are rooted, in the end, in the Sovereign Good itself. Such remembrance is the cognitive and philosophical foundation of ethical thought. That its importance has been largely ignored is a symptom of the modern severing of ethics from spirituality, which treats each as though it were sealed off from the other. Ethics is still taken seriously, as a branch of philosophy and a discipline in its own right; spirituality, all too often, is dismissed as irrelevant to serious scholarly inquiry and consigned to the realm of the imaginary, the vague, and the mythological. This pushing of spirituality to the margins of supposedly serious discourse is closely bound up with the reductive view of knowledge that has increasingly marked Western thought since the Renaissance — a reduction that goes hand in hand with the steady emptying out of the sacred dimension of life.

In the Islamic tradition, ethics and spirituality are intimately joined. Nowhere is this clearer than in the Sufi tradition, now increasingly, and most aptly, called the ihsani tradition — taking the word ihsan (احسان: excellence, virtue, goodness) in the sense the Prophet gave it in a tradition of central importance, one that concerns the three fundamental aspects of the Islamic way. It is known as "the hadith of Gabriel," for the questioner in this exchange, unknown to the Companions who were present, was the angel Gabriel (جبرئیل) in human form. He approached the Prophet as he sat among his Companions, and said:

'O Muhammad, tell me about submission (al-islam),' يَا مُحَمَّدُ، أخْبِرْنِي عَنْ الإسْلاَمِ. The Prophet replied: الإِسْلاَمُ أَنْ تَشْهَدَ أَنْ لاَ إِلهَ إِلاَ اللهُ وَأَنَّ مُحَمَّداً رَسُولُ اللهِ، وَتُقِيمَ الصَّلاَةَ، وَتُؤْتِيَ الزَّكَاةَ، وَتَصُومَ رَمَضَانَ، وَتَحُجَّ الْبَيتَ إِن اسْتَطَعْتَ إِلَيْهِ سَبِيلاً, 'Al-islam is to testify that there is no god but God and Muhammad is the messenger of God, to perform the prayers, to pay the poor-due (al-zakat), to fast in Ramadan, and to make the pilgrimage to the House if you are able to do so.' 'You have spoken truly (صَدَقْتَ),' Gabriel said. The Companions, who saw only a man and did not know he was an angel, were astonished that he should question the Prophet and then declare that he had spoken truly. 'Then tell me about faith (al-iman),' he said, فَأَخْبِرْنِي عَنِ الإِيْمَانِ. The Prophet said: أَنْ تُؤْمِنَ بِاللهِ، وَمَلاَئِكَتِهِ، وَكُتُبِهِ، وَرُسُلِهِ، وَالْيَوْمِ الآخِرِ، وَتُؤْمِنَ بِالْقَدَرِ خَيْرِهِ وَشَرِّهِ, 'It is to believe in God, His angels, His books, His messengers, and the Last Day, and to believe in divine destiny, both the good and the evil thereof.' 'You have spoken truly (صَدَقْتَ),' he said. 'Then tell me about virtue (al-ihsan),' he said, فَأَخْبِرْنِي عَنِ الإِحْسَانِ. He said: أَنْ تَعْبُدَ اللهَ كَأَنَّكَ تَرَاهُ، فَإِنْ لَمْ تَكُنْ تَرَاهُ فَإِنَّهُ يَرَاكَ, 'It is to worship God as though you see Him, and if you see Him not, yet truly He sees you.'

The word ihsan can also be translated literally as "doing what is beautiful," since its root (حَسَنَ) is bound up above all with beauty, husn. Virtue and vice, indeed, are often spoken of in Islamic ethical discourse as husn and qubh (قُبْح) — literally, beauty and ugliness. In the end it is the vision of divine beauty that inspires virtue, which is beauty of soul, just as it is the departure from the divine qualities that leaves the soul ugly (قُبْح). It is no surprise, then, that the division of labor ran as it did. The specialists in outward action have been the jurists, the fuqaha; the specialists in formal belief have been the theologians; and it fell to the mystics to be the custodians of spiritual virtue, of "making beautiful." It is no coincidence, either, that the great majority of artists in the Islamic world — calligraphers, painters, poets, musicians, and the rest — have been practicing Sufis.

From the Prophet's definition of ihsan as "worshipping God as if one could see Him," the crucial bond stands clear — between worship and virtue, between spirituality and ethics, between devotion to the Creator and goodness toward creatures. One cannot be had without the other. This bond lies at the very heart of the spiritual tradition in Islam. And it is the denial and subversion of that tradition by some modern Muslims which, more than any other single factor, lies behind the atrocities now committed by Muslims in the name of Islam.

Both the theory and the practice of ethics have always been integral to Sufism. Even some who wrote in more philosophical and less explicitly spiritual terms, such as al-Farabi, were known to be practicing Sufis as well. The most influential figure in whom spirituality and ethics meet is, of course, al-Ghazali, whose magisterial Revival of the Sciences of Religion stands as a monument to the mark left by Islam's deepest spirit upon the ethical temper of the believing soul. He has aptly been called the greatest thinker on ethics in Islam; and since his thinking is so thoroughly shaped by Sufism, this alone testifies to how close the two stand. It would be no exaggeration to say that most treatises written by Sufis are, in fact, treatises on "ethics" — provided ethics is understood not merely as akhlaq in the narrow sense, but as the fruit of ihsan, of spiritual virtue. The spiritual stations (maqamat) the Sufis describe often bear the names of particular virtues: patience (sabr), gratitude (shukr), contentment (rida'), generosity (karam), and so on.

The thread running through all these stations is a transformative awareness of God, the principle of being and acting "as if you could see God" — that is, conducting and composing oneself in the consciousness of standing constantly in the divine presence, for even if one cannot see Him, "He sees you." The imperative of ihsan thus lies at the very root of a life of virtue in the spiritual universe of Islam. Ali (ع), in his sermons in the نھج البلاغۃ, returns again and again to the "vision" of God, at whatever degree, as the true basis of a life of virtue, and warns repeatedly against its opposite, the engrossing vision of oneself, as the source of all vice. On one hand, the Imam says, "Know that your every action is dependent upon your prayer" — that is, upon one's way of "gazing upon the Good," to use Platonic terms. On the other, he warns against vanity, conceit, and the lavish praise of oneself by others, for these are Satan's (شیطان) most reliable opportunities to efface the virtue, the ihsan, of the virtuous (المُحسنین). The true, objective vision of al-Haqq (اَلْحَقّ), the Real — attained through the effacement of self in prayer, for in the prostration (سجدۃ) of prayer one effaces one's own ego, one's nafs (نَفْس) — stands here against the distorted, subjective vision of the ego that pride inflates, which is precisely what Iblis (اِبْلِیس) desires. The first leads to justice, which puts all things in their proper place, including, most fundamentally, the ego itself. The second leads to every injustice that flows from self-aggrandizement, fanaticism and bigotry among them, for Satan, the very type of pride and disobedience, is described as "the leader of the fanatics and the forerunner of the proud" (امام المتعصبین و سلف المستکبرین, imam al-mutaassibin wa salaf al-mustakbirin). Bigotry is intolerance toward those who hold different opinions; and fanatical bigotry is an especially intense form of injustice, for it carries that ruinous prejudice which subordinates the objective demands of justice to the supposed privileges of one's self — whether that self is defined as the individual, the family, the clan, the tribe, the nation, or the religion.

The Imam's letter appointing Malik al-Ashtar as his governor of Egypt appears as letter 52 in most editions of the نھج البلاغۃ. For centuries it has served as a source of inspiration, read as an ideal constitution for Islamic governance. In its fairly detailed account of the duties and rights of the ruler, of the various officers of the state, and of the main classes of society, it complements the more general framework of principles set down in the famous "Constitution of Medina" dictated by the Prophet. The counsel it contains, however, reaches well beyond the bounds usually set by political or legal texts on governance. Though addressed in the first place to the ruler or governor of the polity, much of it in fact bears on ethical principles of universal reach, and so speaks as much to the governed as to those who govern. The letter is as much a statement of ethics as of politics — not a discursive "system" or theory of ethics, to be sure, but a stirring expression of the spiritual ethos the Imam embodied and radiated. That ethos flows directly from the sources of the Islamic revelation, and so it lays bare the roots of virtue and ethics within a climate governed by the all-embracing principle of revelation. The document may accordingly be read also as a commentary on that revelation — one that in turn invites a deeper entering into the meanings enshrined in the revealed texts. Malik al-Ashtar was a long-standing and devoted follower of the Imam, spoken of as one of his "right-hand" men. Even Muawiya — the Imam's chief antagonist in the civil war that dominated his caliphate — used the phrase, notably on hearing that his own plot to have Malik poisoned before he could take up his post as governor of Egypt had succeeded. Tabari reports Muawiya as saying: "Ali ibn Abi Talib (ع) had two right hands. One of them was cut off at Siffin, and the other today." The Imam, for his part, responded to the news of Malik's assassination thus: "Malik, and what was Malik! By God, were he a mountain, he would be the solitary one soaring above all others, and were he a rock, he would have been impenetrably solid. No horse could ascend such a mountain, no bird could fly up to it."

At the center stands the Imam's definition of justice: "Justice puts everything in its right place." In everyday speech, justice means little more than fairness, equality, and fair play — a serviceable but essentially formal sense of the word. The conception at work here is of an altogether different order. It aligns justice with the sacred — with القدس, the All-Holy — and locates the very roots of justice within the divine nature itself. What binds this conception of justice to the sacred is the demand that one first put one's relationship with God in its right place; everything else follows from that. The same order of precedence is plainly set out in an important statement of the Imam's letter to Malik: "Be just with God and be just with people [giving them what is their due] from yourself." Being just with God means, among other things, conforming as far as one can to His own nature — which is not merely the source of justice but is justice. The weight falls on that word is: justice is not simply one attribute among His many, standing beside His mercy, His wrath, and His love. It is the very essence of His nature, from which all His other qualities flow. His mercy is an expression of His justice, and so are His anger, His love, His creating, and His destroying. "I bear witness," the Imam says in one of his sermons, "that He is Justice ('adl) and He acts justly." It might be objected that between the divine reality of justice and its human expression there lies a radical break. In God, justice means that everything is in its right place; in man, justice means the effort to put everything in its right place — an unchanging metaphysical quality on the divine side, a dynamic act of will on the human side. One response runs thus: man's moral effort to act justly, in a world where things are not in their right place, is rooted in an innate spiritual disposition toward justice. Just action thus expresses the principle of justice, which, in man as in God, is that unchanging principle by which everything is in its right place. Just as divine action — in creation, in judgment, and elsewhere — is an expression of absolute justice, so in man just action expresses not only a moral, volitional, and intellectual effort to realize justice, but also a spiritual affinity with the ultimate nature of reality: with things as they truly are in God, and therefore as they ought to be here on earth. Applied to state and society, one of the plainest consequences of this conception of justice is the need for piety and rectitude in those who rule. As the Imam says in another sermon: "The most momentous of the reciprocal rights that God has made obligatory is the right of the ruler over the subjects, and the right of the subjects over the ruler, a reciprocal obligation which God has ordained for them." He then makes a point indispensable to any discourse on good governance in Islam: "The subjects will not be righteous except through the righteousness of the rulers, and the rulers will not be righteous except through the uprightness of the subjects. So if the subjects render to the ruler his rightful due and he renders them theirs, then will right be honored among them, the ways of religion will be upheld, and the banners of justice will be erected." This insistence on the ruler's piety is, as one would expect, a central part of the Imam's message to his governor; but it amounts to far more than an exhortation to abide by the formal rules of Islamic law.
