The whole of the Imam's life may be seen as a quest to conform as perfectly as possible to the divine nature, and to this quality of justice above all. The dimensions of this principle as they appear in his life and teachings — social, economic, political, juridical — are far too many to treat in a brief compass. One defining moment of his caliphate shows the rigor with which he held to justice at whatever cost. The revolt of Muawiya, and the civil war into which the young Muslim empire was thereby plunged, may be seen as having begun not at Siffin but in Medina. On assuming the caliphate, Ali (ع) was advised by his cousin Ibn al-Abbas to confirm all of 'Uthman's governors in their posts for a time, and then, once he had consolidated the new order, to remove them. The Imam's reply to this proposal was reported thus: "I do not doubt that this would be best for the sake of reconciliation in this world. But there is my obligation to the Truth (alladhi yalzamani min al-haqq) and my knowledge of Uthman's governors, so, by God, I shall never appoint one of them."

So the die was cast: the Imam dismissed Muawiya along with all of his predecessor's governors. He saw clearly that Ibn al-Abbas' proposal had every chance of success; he knew that all Muawiya wanted was worldly power, and that Muawiya would have been content to govern Syria had the Imam allowed it. Commentators have cited this episode — which made the revolt of the governors all but inevitable — as evidence of Ali (ع)'s want of political acumen and his intransigence. It ought rather to be understood as an expression of his disdain for worldly ease when that ease must be bought at the price of essential principles. As he says in one of his aphorisms: "People renounce no part of their religion for the sake of rectifying their world without God opening them up to something worse than it" — worse, that is, than the very defect they sought to rectify.

Confirming in power those governors whose corruption was beyond both doubt and redemption would plainly have amounted, for the Imam, to "renouncing" a part of his religion, for religion without justice is not religion. To support unjust governors, even as a tactical maneuver, was a compromise of religious principle simply inconceivable to him. This attitude was embodied in countless episodes of his life, all of which show that the principles set down in the letter to Malik al-Ashtar were far from merely theoretical.

What follows examines one dimension of justice in particular — political on the surface, ethical in its outward expression, but spiritual in its depth: the justice of the political ruler, taken in relation to its most clearly definable opposite, which is injustice, tyranny, oppression, and corruption. A deep assimilation of the Imam's spirituality can lend an existential power to the moral effort to live justly — a power absent when justice is conceived solely in terms of individual or horizontal values, that is, in terms of a secular moral philosophy cut off from religious or spiritual principles. The crucial role of devotion and worship, both formal and supra-formal, belongs to this same inquiry. Contemplation of the divine Reality is the fountainhead from which whole and integral virtue flows, freely and without ceasing — a point sharpened by comparison with Plato's "gazing" upon the Sovereign Good as the source of all virtue.

The inquiry that follows takes its bearings from a single document above all others: the letter of Ali (ع) to Malik al-Ashtar. It is there that a sacred conception of justice is set out most fully, and it is from its key passages that the present analysis draws.

Around that letter stand the Imam's other writings. His further letters, his sermons, and his sayings are gathered here as they bear upon the same theme, extending and deepening what the letter to Malik sets down. Justice, treated in this way, is not read out of one text in isolation but heard across the whole body of his counsel.

Beyond the words of Ali (ع) himself, the analysis turns to certain traditions of the Prophet and to verses of the Quran. These are brought forward not as ornament but as illumination, for they cast light on the very themes the letter raises and lay bare the ground on which its conception of justice rests.
