> **Editorial note (tradition-grounded).** 
> Among the acts the chapter names as the "body" of the shariah is the paying of alms — zakat. A
> related teaching preserved in this tradition clarifies why that word was chosen: its root joins
> two ideas that Arabic holds as one, growth and purity, on the understanding that what is
> cleansed also thrives. The obligatory alms purifies the giver's wealth and soul while causing
> what remains to grow. Read alongside the chapter's argument, this is telling: an outward act of
> the shariah's "body" is not mere ritual compliance but a discipline that inwardly purifies the
> one who performs it — the very training in the divine nature that the chapter says completes
> action with contemplation.
<!-- editorial:end -->

## 7. Justice and the Tyrant's Pride

The letter is addressed to one of the Imam's closest and most trusted companions. This gives his warnings against injustice and oppression a very precise force, beyond the universal theme they express. The warnings apply to everyone who lives formally under Islam and its laws, and who begins with every right intention, yet remains open to the temptations of political power. They reckon fully with an unfortunate but proven truth, the one captured in the maxim that power corrupts — or, in the Imam's own words, 'he who rules, appropriates (man malaka istathara).'

The Imam's understanding of justice plainly reaches beyond its own historical setting and beyond the formal boundaries of the Islamic faith. The letter can be read as speaking not only to those who wish to govern by just principles, but to all who are not corrupt at the outset yet remain corruptible — a group that takes in the great majority of humankind.

A note of sober realism sounds from the very opening of the letter. What dominates the tone of the preamble is the capacity for evil in man when he is left to himself and deprived of divine grace; this is why the preamble stresses adherence to God's wajibat, the religious obligations binding on every Muslim. Malik is instructed to set obedience to God above all else, 'to abide by what He has commanded in His Book ... for no one prospers except through abiding by them, and no one is wretched except through repudiating and neglecting them'. The preamble is introductory, but it is in no way perfunctory. Obedience to the divine commands is meant as something not merely legally binding but spiritually liberating. Such obedience plays a crucial part in freeing a person from the tyranny of the 'soul which incites to evil' (al-nafs al-ammara bil-su), which the Imam mentions immediately after his call to obedience: 'For truly, the soul incites to evil, unless God has mercy.' This sentence is almost identical to the words of the Prophet Joseph in the Quran (Yusuf: 53), and it is of the greatest importance in setting the scene for the ethical and socio-political instructions that follow. What determines the spiritual substance of the soul — and, still more, its moral conduct — is one essential relationship: that between the human soul and divine grace. Without grace the soul leans toward evil; with God's help it is freed from its base inclinations and led back to its true, original nature, its fitra, to which the Quran refers in the following verse:

So set thy purpose for religion with unswerving devotion: the nature of God (firat Allah), according to which He hath created man. There is no altering God's creation. That is the right religion (al-din al-qayyim), but most men know not. (Ar-Rum: 30)

The actual state of the fallen soul, then, is called to give way to the ideal state of original human nature. That nature is both the origin and the end of the human condition, and at the same time the true substance of every human soul — always accessible in principle, even if for most people it stays clouded, in practice, by fallen human nature. The quest to revive this original nature returns to two complementary functions: the human intellect and divine revelation. In the first sermon of the Nahj, the Imam states that God sent to mankind 'His messengers, dispatching prophets in succession, in order to claim from them [His creatures] the fulfillment of the covenant of His creation; to remind them of His forgotten graces; to remonstrate with them through communication [of His revelation]; to unearth for them the buried treasures of the intellects (dafa in al-uqul).'

These 'buried treasures' can be identified with the original, God-given knowledge that belongs to the fitra. From this whole human nature, in full harmony with the divine nature, all the essential virtues flow freely and unhindered. 'Let your most beloved treasure be the treasure of virtuous acts', the Imam writes. Virtue is indeed the 'most beloved treasure' for the person who reverses the natural leanings of the egoistic soul and turns instead toward his own deepest nature — and, by that same act, toward the divine Reality that supplies the foundation, in being itself, of all genuine virtue. For al-Haqq, the Real, is not only the deepest foundation of virtue; it is the true substance of every positive quality and, in the end, of every existing thing. As one of the most celebrated supplications attributed to the Imam puts it:

By Thy tremendousness (bi-a matika), which has filled all things ... by Thy Names which have filled the foundations of all things, by Thy knowledge which encompasses all things, by the light of Thy face, through which all things are illumined ...

From this vantage point, every virtue can be seen as the reflection, on the human plane, of a divine attribute — what is called a divine 'Name'. This is why the central theme running through the ihsani tradition — the path of spiritual excellence (احسان) that the Prophet defined as worshipping God as though one could see Him, in the knowledge that even if one does not see Him, He sees him — finds expression in the saying: 'Assume the character traits of God (takhallaqu bi-akhlaqiLlah).'

In the Imam's view the bond between human virtue and divine reality is wholly indispensable. Seen from the human side, the practice of a virtue draws down the divine quality that answers to it; seen from the side of reality, the divine quality is the source and — in keeping with true تَوْحِيد — the very substance of all human virtue. Every positive quality, in itself and in its outward showing, belongs to God — the One who 'has no partner' in theology and 'has no second' in being. This inward grasp of the principle of تَوْحِيد is strongly implied in the Quranic verse, 'And thou threw not when thou didst throw [a handful of dust], but God it was who threw' (Al-Anfal: 17). These divine qualities are not to be treated as purely transcendent, as though they were wholly one with the divine essence and entirely out of the human soul's reach. They are also to be grasped as models, patterns of perfection that the soul is to imitate as fully as it can. The goal is to grow as 'like' God as possible, yet this can happen only within the bounds of one's servitude to Him. A person is generous for God's sake, because God is الکریم, the Generous; he is compassionate and merciful because God is الرحمن الرحیم — never out of any claim that 'I am generous' or 'I am merciful', since the aim is to imitate the qualities of God, not to claim to embody them. To be truly virtuous, then, the individual must be effaced before God, and must at the same time share in and embody the very qualities he hopes God will show toward him. He remains lowly, humble, and effaced throughout, certain that no amount of virtue or intelligence can ever lessen or match the qualities of God — for there is no proportion between the Absolute and the relative, the Creator and the created. His effort to embody these qualities gains its power in the measure that he grasps the divine attributes — grasps them by intuition and in lived experience, rather than merely by reason and in theory. It is like gazing into a mirror: what one sees is a reflection, but an inverted one. The aim is to catch, on the human plane, the inverted reflection of divine transcendence. For man is at once خلیفۃ اللہ, God's vicegerent upon the earth, and عباد اللہ, His slave. The awareness of servitude makes sure that, in representing God, man does not slide into competing with Him in grandeur; and the active commitment to representing God's attributes keeps him from the spiritual paralysis of neglecting his duties toward the world. Such is the meaning of the verse, يَٓأَيُّهَا ٱلنَّاسُ أَنتُمُ ٱلْفُقَرَآءُ إِلَى ٱللَّهِۖ وَٱللَّهُ هُوَ ٱلْغَنِىُّ ٱلْحَمِيدُ, 'O mankind, you are the destitute before God, and God is the Self-sufficient, the Praiseworthy' (Fatir: 15). There must be a synthesis of the active human virtues with the spiritual awareness of one's own nothingness before the divine Reality. The bond between intelligence and humility deserves emphasis here: to perceive the divine attributes with the intellect produces humility, and the effacement of the soul in turn draws attention away from the ego and toward the transcendent reality as the source of all virtue and truth.

The idea of the 'soul which incites to evil,' and the Quranic story of Joseph (Yusuf) in which it arises, deserves closer attention. The Imam could take it that his readers knew this context, and he clearly meant it to be kept in mind by Malik, and by anyone else reading this letter on governance.

Joseph gives this description of man's base inclinations after his innocence of every charge has been established before the Egyptian king. Even so, he says, 'I do not exonerate myself. Truly, the soul incites to evil, unless my Lord has mercy.' Indeed, on the two occasions when he faced the seductive temptations of Zulaykha, the Quran makes plain that his success in resisting came not from his own moral resources but from the grace of God.

This theme of human helplessness before attractive but immoral gratification is strongly implied in the Imam's repetition of the Quranic phrase, 'Truly the soul incites to evil, unless God has mercy.' These are the two poles that pull at the ordinary soul, the soul not yet 'at peace.' One is its own base leaning toward the negative possibilities of the fleeting world of appearances; the other is the higher pull toward the grace of God, which draws the soul back toward the pure positivity of ultimate reality. The sheer sharpness of the contrast within this powerful Quranic sentence rouses an awareness of the soul's absolute and unconditional need of God in every circumstance. It is a most fitting way to open a letter of instruction to a man being given authority over a vast and wealthy country, where temptations lie in wait on every side. It stands as a solemn reminder that the soul is open to allurement on every plane, gross and subtle alike — an openness that the soul cannot neutralize by itself, but only by the mercy and grace of God. And yet, to return to Joseph's words, the 'vision of the evidence' of God implies active discernment and, as its moral fruit, resolute self-control. The human effort to avoid sin therefore does not contradict the need for grace in attaining virtue; rather, it expresses a grace already given, already present within the conscientious soul. The very capacity of the human will to strive for virtue is itself a grace already in place, set within the spiritual constitution, or fitra — though it is a grace that must be made present, or actualized, through the sincere effort to draw on this God-given power.

How this grace enters the soul as a shaping element of moral and spiritual life is made clearer later in the Imam's letter. For now, the negative side of the question needs further working out, for the ruler's ever-present temptation to abuse his power and commit injustice and tyranny deserves more than a passing mention. It could well be argued that the question of how to refrain from injustice is far harder and subtler than its positive counterpart, how to act with justice. The Imam lays out the positive rules of political governance directly and plainly. What is far less plain is whether any holder of political office can actually stay faithful to those rules when faced with the temptations that power offers. Laws can be framed to punish corruption, but what no law can supply is the will, or the power, to stay incorruptible before the enticements of political authority. This helps explain why the Imam's exhortations take on so markedly negative a character in the early part of the letter, as in the command to 'break the soul in the face of passionate desires', to 'dominate your inclination', to 'withhold yourself from that which is not lawful for you'. It also explains why the ethical demand of justice must be set firmly within a lived spirituality. Without that grounding, commands to be fair, just, and honest will lack the lived quality that turns them from abstract propositions into unquestionable realities, and that turns formal obedience to externally imposed rules into a spiritual kinship with the source of those rules.

To live by just precepts then ceases to be a matter of complying with something imposed from outside, and becomes instead the moral counterpart of one's inner being. In other words, a whole approach to justice leads to an identification with what is right, with al-Haqq itself, rather than merely to the doing of what is right. There is a shift of consciousness from doing to being, from action to contemplation — and this in no way weakens or undermines action. On the contrary, action comes to reflect, on its own plane, the divine object of contemplation, and so becomes more effective, not less. An orientation toward the Real gives a deeper degree of reality both to what one does in the realm of action and to one's own mode of being. One does what is right in the measure that one is real; the Islamic spiritual tradition calls this process of realization tahqiq, 'making real'. One's ethical conduct is 'made real' through spirituality, and spirituality, in its turn, is expressed and made more concrete through the practice of virtue.

The Imam then issues a stern warning: وَلاَ تَنْصِبَنَّ نَفْسَكَ لِحَرْبِ اللهِ, 'Do not set your soul up for war with God.' It is fair to ask what this means, and what kinds of actions or attitudes set the soul at war with God. The allusion seems to reach deeper than simple disobedience, deeper than the many breaches of religious obligation named at the letter's outset. That is the most obvious sense of 'fighting' against God, but it does not rule out subtler forms of opposition to divine reality — attitudes that may accompany outward acts of obedience yet contradict their very spirit. One example is the cardinal vice of pride (takabbur, or kibr), against which the Imam warns again and again: the inner state in which a person feels superior and acts highhandedly toward others, its signs so plain in his conduct that they cannot be hidden. A ruler's pride is a seizing of a divine attribute for himself, a claiming of a greatness that belongs to God alone, who declares: الْكِبْرِيَاءُ رِدَائِي، وَالْعَظَمَةُ إِزَارِي، فَمَنْ نَازَعَنِي وَاحِدًا مِنْهُمَا، قَذَفْتُهُ فِي النَّارِ, 'Pride is My cloak and greatness is My robe; whoever contends with Me over either of them, I shall cast him into the Fire.' God alone has the right to takabbur, and God alone may be called, without offense, al-Mutakabbir, 'the Proud'. To exalt oneself before others is, inwardly, to defy God's authority, and so amounts to an implicit denial of His unique and incomparable majesty. This denial of something that belongs to divine sovereignty can itself be understood as a kind of 'war' with God, for a subject who openly defies his king is, in effect, waging war against him. In short, enmity toward transcendent reality is the invisible substance that runs through all pride, and through every act of arrogance or oppression that a ruler commits against those beneath him.

The paradox here, one that only a sense of spiritual values can illuminate, is this: an attitude of superiority toward those 'below' is in fact an act of rebellion against that which is 'above'. An injustice done to creatures not only draws the Creator's justice down upon the one who commits it; it is, in itself, outright opposition to the Creator. So what looks like a horizontal vice — 'horizontal' in the sense that it belongs to this world — is in reality a spiritual disorder. The outward immorality of corruption is only the symptom of an inner spiritual sickness: an implicit denial that the source of all power and authority is absolute, and therefore unique. It is a forgetting of the truth expressed in the Quranic verse لِّمَنِ ٱلْمُلْكُ ٱلْيَوْم لِلَّهِ ٱلْوَحِدِ ٱلْقَهَّارِ, 'To whom belongeth the dominion this day? To God, the One, the All-Conquering' (Ghafir: 16). This truth should sound constantly in the ears of every ruler, with 'this day' understood as every day, not only the Day of Judgment. In the Imam's sermon known as al-Qasia (القاسیۃ), 'the Abasement' — so named because it humbles the proud — Iblis (Satan) is described as the first of those who 'competed with God' in grandeur, and so as the very embodiment of pride and arrogance. When commanded to prostrate before Adam, Iblis refused, as the Quran recounts:

إِذْ قَالَ رَبُّكَ لِلْمَلَٓئِكَةِ إِنِّى خَلِقٌۢ بَشَرًۭا مِّن طِينٍۢ ۝ فَإِذَا سَوَّيْتُهُۥ وَنَفَخْتُ فِيهِ مِن رُّوحِى فَقَعُوا۟ لَهُ سَجِدِينَ ۝ فَسَجَدَ ٱلْمَلَٓئِكَةُ كُلُّهُمْ أَجْمَعُونَ ۝ إِلَّآ إِبْلِيسَ ٱسْتَكْبَرَ وَكَانَ مِنَ ٱلْكَفِرِينَ, 'When your Lord said to the angels: Truly I am going to create man from clay; and when I have fashioned him and breathed into him of My spirit, then fall down before him prostrate. So the angels prostrated themselves, all of them together; save Iblis: he was proud and was one of the disbelievers' (Sad: 71-74).

He refused because 'vehement passion' overcame him. He judged himself superior to Adam on the grounds that he was made of fire while Adam was made of clay, خلقتنی من نار و خلقتہ من طین. By taking pride in this presumed superiority, and so rebelling against God's command, Iblis becomes not only the first 'enemy of God' but also 'the leader of the bigots and the forerunner of the proud' (imam al-muta assibin wa salaf al-mustakbirin, امام المُتَعَصِّبِین و سلف المستکبرین). The Imam warns his listeners: 'Beware lest he infect you with his disease' through his insinuations, whisperings, and machinations. He urges them: 'Be resolute in placing self-abasement over your heads, casting self-glorification beneath your feet, and removing pride from your necks. And take up humility as the fortified watchtower between you and your enemy.'

Humility is the principal weapon in the war against Iblis, whose own chief aim is the reverse: to instill pride. For pride is not only the worst of vices but the poison that infects and destroys every virtue. A virtue turns into a vice the moment pride accompanies it. 'The sin that grieves you is better, in the sight of God, than the virtue that makes you proud.' Bound up with this is what was noted earlier about the dangers of excessive praise and the conceit it breeds — these being among 'Satan's most reliable opportunities to efface the virtue of one who is virtuous'.

Taken together, these statements form a commentary on the Quranic account of Iblis' disgrace. They grasp a simple act of disobedience at its spiritual root, and in doing so bring home the ever-present danger of falling prey to that 'hidden' form of shirk (polytheism) to which the Prophet refers in these words: الشِّرْكُ فِي هَذِهِ الْأُمَّةِ أَخْفَى مِنْ دَبِيبِ النَّمْلِ علی حَجَرِ الأَسوَد فِی لِیلَۃِ الظُّلْما — 'The creeping of shirk in my community is more hidden (akhfa) than the creeping of a black ant over a hard rock on a dark night.' The superficial notion of shirk as the worship of material idols — شرک الجلی, its open and manifest form — must be understood more subtly as شرک الخفی, the hidden form: false gods concealed within one's intentions, attitudes, and orientations, all the elements of hidden pride, vanity, ostentation, and pretentiousness. As the Imam says, 'Know that the slightest pretension (riya — ریا) is polytheism.' To act in order to earn others' praise is to act for others rather than for God; it is therefore an implicit attribution of partners to God: 'Whoso acts for other than God, God entrusts him to those for whose sake he acted.'

In this light, the meaning of Iblis' disobedience can be understood more deeply. It is the outward moral consequence of an inner spiritual sickness, one rooted in the pride that amounts to 'hidden polytheism' and that brings a disgraceful humiliation in its wake. Iblis is disgraced because he disobeyed God, and he disobeyed God on account of pride. Pride is therefore, in itself, a disgrace — a state of 'dis-grace' — a perversion of the human condition that carries within it the seed of its own inexorable inversion. 'Do you not see how God humiliated him through his pride?' the Imam asks. To be proud is already, in reality, a form of humiliation, for self-aggrandizement amounts to self-destruction.

Returning to the pride of those in power, the Imam captures in a few words the train of thought set going in the mind of a ruler who is stepping onto the slope toward oppression: وَلاَ تَقُولَنَّ: إِنِّي مُؤَمَّرٌ! آمُرُ! فَأُطَاعُ! إِنَّ ذلِكَ إِدْغَالٌ فِي الْقَلْبِ، وَمَنْهَكَةٌ لِلدِّينِ — 'Do not say, "I have been given authority, I order and am obeyed", for this leads to corruption in the heart and the erosion of religion.' This self-glorification is seen to produce first an inner, spiritual corruption — that of the 'heart' — and only then the destruction of religion. This order of priority shows that the outward prescriptions of religion require the right inward attitude if they are to be applied with integrity and kept stable. The Imam's idea might be put this way: when power goes to the head, faith departs from the heart; and conversely, for faith to be rightly manifested in the world, it must flow from a heart steeped in humility. This is another aspect of 'putting each thing in its proper place', وضع کل شئی حق محلہ.

The Imam continues: وَإِذَا أَحْدَثَ لَكَ مَا أَنْتَ فِيهِ مِنْ سُلْطَانِكَ أُبَّهَةً أَوْ مَخِيلَةً، فَانْظُرْ إِلَى عِظَمِ مُلْكِ اللهِ فَوْقَكَ، فَإِنَّ ذلِكَ يُطَامِنُ إِلَيْكَ مِنْ طِمَاحِكَ، وَيَكُفُّ عَنْكَ مِنْ غَرْبِكَ،يَفِيءُ إِلَيْكَ بِمَا عَزَبَ عَنْكَ مِنْ عَقْلِكَ — 'If the authority of your position engenders vanity and arrogance, then look at the grandeur of God's dominion above you … this will calm your ambition, restrain you from your own vehemence, and restore to you what had strayed from your intellect.' The relationship between intellect and humility is significant here. The part of the intellect that 'strays' when vanity and arrogance take hold is precisely the part that is conscious of God's absolute reality, and of the relative, derived, and ultimately illusory character of everything else. It is telling that the word for 'arrogance', makhila (مَخِيلَةً), comes from the same root as the word for imagination, khayal (خیال), and is therefore bound up with the realm of the imaginary, the fanciful, the illusory, and the unreal: when imagination fastens onto the unreal, it becomes infused with pride and turns into مخیلۃ, arrogance. Arrogant pride is therefore an intense form of self-delusion rooted in a defect of the intellect. It shows itself as a vice, but its cause runs deeper than the moral level, for it involves not merely an overestimation of oneself but, more fundamentally, an underestimation of the Absolute. In this light, the open egotism of the tyrant can be seen as an intensification of the inborn egocentricity that clings to anyone who has not realized the truth of God's sole reality and the ultimately illusory nature of all else. The sage alone fully assimilates the truth expressed in such Quranic verses as: كُلُّ شَىْءٍ هَالِكٌ إِلَّا وَجْهَهُ — 'Everything is dying away except His countenance' (Al-Qasas: 88); كُلُّ مَنْ عَلَيْهَا فَانٍۢ ۝ وَيَبْقَىٰ وَجْهُ رَبِّكَ ذُو ٱلْجَلَلِ وَٱلْإِكْرَامِ — 'Everything that is thereon is perishing, and there subsisteth but the Face of thy Lord, possessor of Glory and Bounty' (Ar-Rahman: 26-27); and وَقُلْ جَآءَ ٱلْحَقُّ وَزَهَقَ ٱلْبَطِلُ ۚ إِنَّ ٱلْبَطِلَ كَانَ زَهُوقًۭا — 'The Truth hath come and falsehood hath passed away; indeed, falsehood is ever passing away' (Al-Isra: 81). Only the sage draws from this truth the humility that dissolves all individualism and egocentricity — the roots of pride, vanity, and arrogance, which are in turn the lifeblood of tyranny and oppression.

The Imam's warning can now be better appreciated: إِيَّاكَ وَمُسَامَةَ اللهِ فِي عَظَمَتِهِ، وَالتَّشَبُّهَ بِهِ فِي جَبَرُوتِهِ، فَإِنَّ اللهَ يُذِلُّ كُلَّ جَبَّار، وَيُهِينُ كُلَّ مُخْتَال — 'Beware of comparing [yourself] with God in greatness and likening [yourself] to Him in might, for God abases every tyrant and disgraces every braggart.' The Imam commands, أَنْصِفِ اللهَ وَأَنْصِفِ النَّاسَ مِنْ نَفْسِكَ، وَمِنْ خَاصَّةِ أَهْلِكَ، وَمَنْ لَكَ فِيهِ هَوىً مِنْ رَعِيَّتِكَ، فَإِنَّكَ إِلاَّ تَفْعَلْ تَظْلِمْ; and one who does not act with justice toward God and His creatures will find that not His creatures alone but God Himself becomes his adversary: وَمَنْ ظَلَمَ عِبَادَ اللهِ كَانَ اللهُ خَصْمَهُ دُونَ عِبَادِهِ، وَمَنْ خَاصَمَهُ اللهُ أَدْحَضَ حُجَّتَهُ، وَكَانَ لله حَرْباً حَتَّى يَنْزعَ وَيَتُوبَ — 'He remains at war with God until he desists and repents.' The tyrant will in the end be compelled to repent, even if only at the moment of death and imminent judgment. Even if he appears to have escaped the consequences of his tyranny in this life, he cannot escape them in the Hereafter.

As for the nature of the tyrant's war against God in this world — that is, before repentance or judgment overtakes him — it consists in his implicit opposition to ultimate reality: a violation of the deepest nature of things, and of the true nature of his own being, his فطرۃ. This war takes various forms.

One form is the very persistence of tyranny itself: the longer it continues, the more wretched the tyrant's soul becomes, and the more firmly the seeds of misery are planted in his being. This is what is implied by the Quranic doctrine of istidraj (اِسْتِدْرَاج), the drawing out of punishment by degrees. The word derives from درج, meaning stages — as even in Urdu one says درجہ بدرجہ, step by step. God employs it in Surat al-Araf: ‏وَمِمَّنْ خَلَقْنَآ أُمَّةٌۭ يَهْدُونَ بِٱلْحَقِّ وَبِهِ يَعْدِلُونَ ‎۱۸۱‏وَٱلَّذِينَ كَذَّبُوا۟ بِـَٔايَتِنَا سَنَسْتَدْرِجُهُم مِّنْ حَيْثُ لَا يَعْلَمُونَ ‎۱۸۲ — 'Of those We have created are people who guide with truth and dispense justice therewith. But those who reject Our signs, We will lead them on by degrees [to punishment] from whence they know not' (Al-A'raf: 181-2). The punishment meted out by degrees can take the form of apparent worldly success, as the verse makes clear: ‏وَلَا تُعْجِبْكَ أَمْوَلُهُمْ وَأَوْلَدُهُمْ ۚ إِنَّمَا يُرِيدُ ٱللَّهُ أَن يُعَذِّبَهُم بِهَا فِى ٱلدُّنْيَا وَتَزْهَقَ أَنفُسُهُمْ وَهُمْ كَفِرُونَ ‎۸۵ — 'Let not their wealth nor their children please thee. God desireth only to punish them thereby in the world, that their souls shall pass away while they are disbelievers' (At-Tawbah: 85). A tyrant or oppressor can be a Muslim in the formal sense and yet, at the same time, guilty of a kind of disbelief — taking the word kafir in its strict etymological sense, as one who 'covers over' the truth and its moral demands, both in his very being and in his actions. This connects directly with the contrast drawn in the verses just quoted (Al-A'raf: 181-2): between those who dispense justice in accordance with the truth and 'those who reject Our signs' — the oppressors and tyrants, precisely.

## 8. Conscience, Compassion, and the Ordering of Society

One form this enmity with God takes is the inner war against one's own conscience — in Urdu, the ⟪ar:ضمیر⟫. Secular ethics treats the voice of conscience as its whole foundation, an overgrown ground on which all individual morality is made to stand. Seen more fully, that voice can be grasped in its true context: the source above the individual from which each person's conscience springs — the discernment God breathes into the soul, the inspiration that lies beneath every act of telling good from evil. In this way the divine quality of justice becomes, within the soul that bears moral responsibility, the voice of inner conscience:

> وَنَفْسٍۢ وَمَا سَوَّىٰهَا ۷ فَأَلْهَمَهَا فُجُورَهَا وَتَقْوَىٰهَا ۸

'And the soul and that which perfected it, inspiring it with [consciousness of] its wickedness and its righteousness' (Ash-Shams: 7-8). The war against one's conscience, even if the oppressor barely acknowledges it, is in fact — and with even greater force — a war against God.

At a deeper level, this war against God shows itself as a war against the very reality that makes the soul a soul. For the soul holds not only a moral conscience — a built-in, unremovable awareness of the difference between good and evil, placed in it by God — but also an inborn awareness that God is Lord and that the creature is His servant. Spiritual awareness is therefore the source of moral conscience. The Quran describes how this spiritual awareness dwells within each soul in words that stress its standing above and before ordinary existence, calling up a 'moment' at the very dawn of creation — a moment, or level of being, that comes before the human condition itself and yet enters into the making of the human soul:

> وَإِذْ أَخَذَ رَبُّكَ مِنۢ بَنِىٓ آدَمَ مِن ظُهُورِهِمْ ذُرِّيَّتَهُمْ وَأَشْهَدَهُمْ عَلَىٰٓ أَنفُسِهِمْ أَلَسْتُ بِرَبِّكُمْ ۖ قَالُوا۟ بَلَىٰ ۛ شَهِدْنَآ ۛ أَن تَقُولُوا۟ يَوْمَ ٱلْقِيَمَةِ إِنَّا كُنَّا عَنْ هَذَا غَفِلِينَ ۱۷۲

'And when thy Lord brought forth from the children of Adam, from their loins, their seed, and made them testify against their souls [saying], "Am I not your Lord?" They said, "Yes, verily, we testify." [This was] lest ye say on the Day of Resurrection, "Truly, of this we were unaware" (Al-A'raf: 172). The moment He created them He made them witness His glory and testify to it, so that they could plead no ignorance on the Day of Judgment.

What comes into view is that the source of morality lies above the human level even as it forms part of what makes a human being human. In this light, what makes the soul fully human is exactly that higher element which infinitely surpasses it and yet, in a way past understanding, supplies its own deepest being. Tyranny and oppression then take on a subtler meaning: they draw close to the lived reality of every soul, rather than belonging only to those who hold political power and abuse it. The tyrant is, before all else, one who violates his own soul, one who lets it fall under the sway of 'the soul which commands evil', al-nafs al-ammara (⟪ar:النفس الامارۃ بالسوء⟫). So the saying the Imam cites as summing up the attitude of one on the way to corrupting his own soul and becoming an oppressor — وَلاَ تَقُولَنَّ: إِنِّي مُؤَمَّرٌ آمُرُ فَأُطَاعُ, 'I order and am obeyed' — is to be read not only in social terms but also within the small world of the soul. It becomes the saying of the evil element inside the soul: the nafs al-ammara, which literally 'orders' (amara) and is obeyed by every other faculty of the soul — intelligence, imagination, will, sentiment, sensibility, and the rest.

The struggle against one's own soul (mujahadat al-nafs, ⟪ar:مجاھدۃ النفس⟫) is a theme the Imam returns to again and again. This need to wrestle with and overcome one's own faults is an essential part of justice as defined earlier: to place each thing in its proper place begins with putting oneself right before presuming to reform or govern others; it means restoring the divine nature within oneself. As the Imam says, 'If your aspiration is to reform the people, begin with yourself, for your pursuit of the reform of others while your own soul is corrupt is the greatest of faults.' In his letter appointing Muhammad b. Abi Bakr (⟪ar:محمد بن ابی بکر⟫), Malik's (⟪ar:مالک اشتر⟫) predecessor as governor of Egypt, the Imam draws a firm link between the granting of power and the duty to resist one's own soul: 'And be aware, O Muhammad b. Abi Bakr, that I have appointed you as governor over the most immense of the forces at my disposal, the people of Egypt, so you are obliged to oppose your soul and protect your religion.' The bond between the scale of the power placed in one's hands and the spiritual responsibility that follows from it is a close one. One must be on guard, first over one's own soul and its desires, and second over the religion. The greater the power at hand, the greater the duty to control and restrain oneself; for if the soul gives way to its desires and satisfies them through that power, the outcome is the corruption not only of oneself but of the very religion one is charged to uphold. Here again priorities are set by a principle that is essentially spiritual: the outward form and practice of religion depends for its soundness on the inward state of the Muslim's soul. This is another form of the principle of putting each thing in its proper place. The commanding soul that claims for itself the right to self-rule and mastery is described with carved exactness by the Quranic verse:

> كَلَّآ إِنَّ ٱلْإِنسَنَ لَيَطْغَىٰٓ ۶ أَن رَّآهُ ٱسْتَغْنَىٰٓ ۷

'Truly man is rebellious, in that he deemeth himself independent' (Al-'Alaq: 6-7). As far as the soul pretends to self-sufficiency and tries to cut itself off from its complete dependence on God, it sets itself up as a god in its own right — the person coming actually to believe that he himself is God. Here lies the true portrait of the tyrant: the one who, as the Quran puts it, 'taketh his own desire as his god' (Al-Jathiyah: 23): أَفَرَءَيْتَ مَنِ ٱتَّخَذَ إِلَهَهُۥ هَوَىٰهُ. Knowledge of one's nothingness before God, and therefore of one's unconditional need of Him, may be taken here as the chief means of loosening the grip of the soul that commands to evil. For only the soul that already commands itself to evil will command others to evil; and only the soul that is lowly before God will be humble, compassionate, and just in its dealings with people.

This principle comes out clearly in the following words of the Imam: 'Let the most beloved of affairs to you be those most centered upon the right, the most comprehensive in justice, and the most inclusive of popular approval, for the disapproval of the common folk undermines the approval of the elite ...' This stress on the importance of the common people is not only sound practical counsel for fair and effective government; it can also be read as reflecting the spiritual duty to recognize man's real lowliness before God, and so as a way of urging the ruler to side with the poor rather than the rich, in keeping with the principle:

> يَٓأَيُّهَا ٱلنَّاسُ أَنتُمُ ٱلْفُقَرَآءُ إِلَى ٱللَّهِۖ وَٱللَّهُ هُوَ ٱلْغَنِىُّ ٱلْحَمِيدُ

'O mankind, ye are the poor before God, and God, He is the Rich, the absolutely Praiseworthy' (Fatir: 15). The Imam's own poverty and strict austerity were famous. Yet that this was far from a mere turning away from the world for its own sake is made clear in his exchange with an ascetic, 'Asim b. Ziyad, whose family complained to the Imam that he was too severe with himself. The Imam told him to think of his family and not to cut himself off from the good things (al-tayyibat) that God has permitted. Asim retorted, O Commander of the Faithful, and yet here you are, in your rough clothes and your coarse food!' The Imam replied, 'Woe to you, I am not like you. God, the Exalted, has made it incumbent on true leaders (aimmat al-haqq) that they proportion themselves to the weakest among the people (yaqaddiru anfusahum bi-dafatil-nas), so that the poverty of the poor will not engender covetousness.' Here is a strict statement of the duty of the most powerful to place themselves, in material terms, alongside the most powerless. It is hard to imagine a more effective way of lessening worldliness and raising spirituality in a society than this deliberate self-lowering, in purely material terms, to the level of the weakest people one rules. He who could have the most chooses to have the least. The Imam implies that if a leader is to stand among the aimmat al-haqq, he must show his attachment to al-Haqq, the ultimate spiritual reality in whose light material wealth counts for nothing. So although the most powerful and the most powerless may both be poor in material terms, both alike have access to the treasures of the spiritual realm, in proportion to how far they realize the truth expressed in the verse cited above: 'O mankind, ye are the poor before God, and God, He is the Rich, the absolutely Praiseworthy'. On the level of social psychology too, the Imam's insistence that rulers live like the poor, as his own life showed, is an effective means of ensuring that the inevitable inequalities of society will not breed envy among the poor.

The compassion of justice

Siding with the poor also deepens the ruler's awareness of his own unending need of God, helping him to live more fully in reliance on divine mercy, in hope and trust; and the natural companion of such a life is the giving of mercy and compassion to one's fellow human beings. The capacity to act with compassion in no way conflicts with the demands of justice; rather, it is a part of justice understood in terms of the nature of being — that is, in accordance with the fundamental nature of reality. To act with compassion agrees with justice not only in the sense of putting things in their right place, so that one extends compassion wherever and whenever it is called for; it also agrees with justice by being at one with the true nature of things, with the innermost character of the Real. It is for this reason that at the head of every chapter of the Quran (except one) the name 'Allah' is followed by 'the infinitely Compassionate, the ever-Merciful' (al-Rahman, al-Rahim). In other words, the capacity to act with compassion springs not so much from personal feeling as from an inborn kinship with the very nature of divine reality — that higher reality in which, as the Quran says, 'My mercy encompasseth all things' (Al-A'raf: 156); a reality in which 'thy Lord has prescribed for Himself mercy' (Al-An'am: 54); a reality whose essential character is set by the principle, 'My mercy takes precedence over My wrath'. Among the many sayings of Ali (ع) on the need to show mercy are these: 'The dispensing of mercy brings down [divine] mercy.' 'As you grant mercy, so will you be granted mercy.' 'I am astounded by the person who hopes for mercy from one above him, while he is not merciful to those beneath him.'

Neither the stern aspect of the nature of things nor the 'wrathful' side of God is being denied here, but both are plainly set below the higher principle of mercy, which belongs to the nature of being itself. There is, in other words, no common measure between the passing, relative things that are subject to wrath and punishment and the realities, blessed in themselves, to which divine mercy and compassion give access. One is therefore more 'real' the more one's soul reflects this predominance of mercy, both in spiritual bearing and in moral conduct. And in human society, it lies in the very nature of justice, understood in this sacred way, to lean toward compassion wherever possible, even though there must also be a place for the strict application of corrective punishment where that cannot be avoided. In his letter the Imam goes on to instruct Malik to inflict corporal punishment on any officer found guilty of stealing public funds. The Imam's counsels and letters are full of stern warnings of swift and severe punishment to his governors, and the severity of these warnings matches his unbending faithfulness to the demands of fairness and honesty in handling public revenues. For example, he writes to Ziyad b. Abihi, deputy to the governor of Basra, Ali (ع)'s cousin Abd Allah b. al-Abbas:

I swear by God an oath in all sincerity: if news reaches me that you have misappropriated the revenue of the Muslims, whether a small or large amount, I shall inflict a severe punishment upon you, one which will lighten your wealth, burden your back, and degrade your affair.

The Imam was equally severe toward his own kin at even a hint of a request for favors that would require the abuse of public funds. In one sermon he refers to an incident in which his blind brother, 'Aqil b. Abi Talib, asked for funds from the treasury beyond what was his due. He thought I would sell my religion for him,' the Imam said. His response was to bring a red-hot piece of iron close to his brother's body, telling him that by making such a request he was urging Ali (ع) himself to enter a far fiercer fire. Likewise, when told of the misappropriation of funds by his cousin and long-standing ally 'Abd Allah b. al-Abbas, the Imam answered with a thundering rebuke. He ordered his cousin: Fear God and return their property to these people. If you do not, and God gives me authority over you, I excuse myself before God in regard to you [and your wrongdoing], and I shall indeed strike you with my sword, with which whomever I have struck enters the fire [of Hell]. By God, even if Hasan and Husayn had done the like of what you have done, they would not have been granted any leniency by me.'

The mention of the Prophet's grandsons carries weight. However close one's tie to a wrongdoer, however high his standing, the principle of justice demands complete impartiality: there are to be no favors for one's favorites. This stance is not only the exact opposite of the nepotism that had undermined the rule of the Imam's predecessor; it is also exactly what the Quran commands: 'O ye who believe, be staunch in justice, witnesses of God, even though it be against yourselves, your parents or your near of kin ...' (An-Nisa: 135).

The theme of mercy returns in a passage remarkable for setting the need for compassion in a universal frame. It stands as one of the Imam's most important statements on the unity of the human race and the equality of all human beings, and as a correction of every form of prejudice, group-favouritism, and sectarianism that would confine justice or compassion to members of one's own group, however defined:

Infuse your heart with mercy for the subjects, love for them and kindness toward them. Be not like a ravenous beast of prey above them, seeking to devour them. For they are of two types: either your brother in religion or your like in creation. Mistakes slip from them, defects emerge from them, deliberately or accidentally. So bestow upon them your forgiveness and your pardon, just as you would have God bestow upon you His forgiveness and pardon; for you are above them, and the one who has authority over you is above you, and God is above him who appointed you ... and through them He tests you.

Here the universal reach of compassion is joined to a reminder of God's absolute sovereignty. No man — neither a governor nor the ruler who appointed him — is anything but a slave of God, wholly dependent upon His mercy. So everyone who stands in a position of relative superiority over others must keep his own lowliness before the Absolute always in mind. That awareness works in two directions at once: it moves him to compassion toward those below him, and it draws down upon him the compassion of God above him.

One incident shows how the Imam put this compassion into practice, and gives vivid form to the principle already noted, that all people are 'your like in creation'. Coming upon an old, blind beggar, the Imam asked who he was, and was told that the man was a Christian. He turned to those around him and said, 'You have employed him until he is old and infirm, and now you refuse to help him. Give him maintenance from the public funds (bayt al-mal).' In a single sentence this states the Islamic principle of social welfare (maslaha), which rests on redistributive justice and on a strict refusal to discriminate between Muslims and others. Social justice and religious equality flow from compassion — compassion understood not as mere sentiment but as an intrinsic dimension of the Real. For that reason they become a fundamental religious duty, binding upon all Muslims, rulers and citizens alike.

The last sentence of that passage — warning the governor to regard his power over his subjects not as a privilege but as a trial from God — plainly expresses the Imam's own attitude to rulership. In the famous sermon al-Shiqshiqiyya he says that he accepted power only as an unavoidable duty:

Had God not taken from the learned [a promise] that they would not acquiesce in the rapacity of the tyrant nor in the hunger of the oppressed ... I would truly have flung its reins [that of the caliphate] back upon its withers ... and you would indeed have discovered that this world of yours is as insignificant to me as that which drips from the nose of a goat.

This is a striking embodiment of Plato's ideal stance toward power — the stance of the true 'philosopher', who rules solely for the sake of the people, 'not regarding it as a distinction, but as an unavoidable task ... caring only for the right and the honors to be gained from that, and above all, for justice as the one indispensable thing in whose service and maintenance they will reorganize their own state.'

The principle behind this whole approach to authority is plainly that of noblesse oblige. To be granted the grace, the blessing, or the privilege of knowledge, together with a clear sense of justice, is to be placed under a correspondingly greater obligation: one's duty to God grows in proportion to the graces one has received. The Imam gave this memorable form in a sermon delivered after one of his supporters had praised him: 'The blessings of God upon a person do not become immense without the right of God over him becoming immense.'

'A good opinion of God'

A subtle point in the letter is easily missed, both because it is put so concisely and because its positive counterpart is left unstated. It arises where the Imam instructs Malik on the kind of advisers to keep close, telling him to avoid misers and cowards:

> وَلاَ تُدْخِلَنَّ فِي مَشُورَتِكَ بَخِيلاً يَعْدِلُ بِكَ عَنِ الْفَضْلِ، وَيَعِدُكَ الْفَقْرَ، وَلاَ جَبَاناً يُضعِّفُكَ عَنِ الاْمُورِ، وَلاَ حَرِيصاً يُزَيِّنُ لَكَ الشَّرَهَ بِالْجَوْرِ، فَإِنَّ الْبُخْلَ وَالْجُبْنَ وَالْحِرْصَ غَرَائِزُ شَتَّى يَجْمَعُهَا سُوءُ الظَّنِّ بِاللهِ.

Do not include among those you consult a miser who would hold you back from generosity and warn you of destitution; nor a coward who would make you feel too weak for your affairs; nor a greedy person who would make the amassing of wealth by evil means seem attractive to you. For miserliness, cowardice and avarice are so many diverse inclinations, all united by a bad opinion of God.

The phrase 'a bad opinion' (سُوءُ الظَّنِّ بِاللهِ, su al-zann) points to a basic misunderstanding of the divine nature, one that breeds a whole series of false ideas about the qualities of God. Once the divine nature is misunderstood, these very vices follow. The miser holds the bad opinion that God may fail to provide in time to come; the coward, that God may fail to protect him; the greedy person, that God has given him less than he needs. Seen this way, every human vice traces back to a failure of the intellect, which is itself the fruit of weak faith, or even of outright atheism — for to disbelieve in God is to deny His reality, and so is itself a form of 'bad opinion' about Him. The converse follows: a strong and deep faith generates a 'good opinion' (حُسْنُ الظَّنّ, husn al-zann) of God, an accurate perception of the divine Reality in both intellect and contemplation. That good opinion then leaves the soul truly impressed — not merely on the surface — by the divine qualities that Reality holds. Those qualities are to be understood in two ways at once. They are transcendent, absolutely one with the divine Essence and wholly beyond the reach of the human soul; and they are also models, exemplars, patterns, and perfections that the soul is to imitate as far as it can.

This is the very opposite of 'comparing oneself with God in greatness'. One must be as much 'like' God as possible, yet this spiritual growth unfolds entirely within the unalterable context of one's servitude to Him; it is the emulation of God's qualities, not the personification of Him. In other words, one must be generous because God is al-Karim (اَلْکَرِیم), the Generous, and compassionate and merciful because He is al-Rahman (الرحمن الرحیم), the Compassionate, not because one is generous or merciful of oneself. But one must equally be lowly, humble, and self-effacing, for no measure of virtue or intelligence can lessen the gulf between the Absolute and the relative, between the Creator and the created. Humility and self-effacement are ways of expressing the human being's utter dependence on God, and of showing forth the transcendence of the Absolute. So between the human and the divine there runs an analogy that is at once positive and inverse. In its positive form: 'He who shows not mercy will not have mercy shown to him.' In its inverse form, as already cited:

> يَٓأَيُّهَا ٱلنَّاسُ أَنتُمُ ٱلْفُقَرَآءُ إِلَى ٱللَّهِۖ وَٱللَّهُ هُوَ ٱلْغَنِىُّ ٱلْحَمِيدُ

'O mankind, ye are the poor before God, and God, He is the Rich, the absolutely Praiseworthy' (Fatir: 15). There must therefore be a synthesis: on one side the active human virtues, which are positive reflections of the divine qualities; on the other the spiritual awareness of one's own nothingness before the divine Reality, which is the inverted reflection, on the human plane, of divine transcendence. The image is that of a mirror. What one sees in it is oneself, yet seen in reverse; and to look into the divine mirror is to see, on the human plane, an inverted reflection of divine transcendence. Man is at once slave and representative of God — 'abduLlah (عباد اللہ) and khalifatuLlah (خلیفۃ اللہ). Awareness of his servitude keeps him from sliding, even unawares, from representing God to rivalling Him in grandeur; while an active commitment to representing God keeps that awareness of servitude from collapsing into forgetfulness of the dignity and the responsibilities that belong to the human state — a forgetfulness that would breed spiritual paralysis and the neglect of one's duty toward the world. The balance between these two sides of human existence is itself another aspect of sacred justice, of setting things in their right order, and it tunes the quality of moral action to the harmony of pure being.

State and society

The Imam then describes the different classes of people within state and society: the army, the scribes, the judiciary, executive officers, tax-collectors, peasants, merchants, artisans, and last of all the destitute — each class with its own rights and duties. These instructions may be seen as justice at its sharpest edge in practice, taking full account of the demands of social and political life. The governor is to treat every class under his authority as holding both rights and obligations; yet the whole character of his relationship to society is once again fixed by his primary duty to God. For it is God who has assigned to each class its share, and who has laid down, as a binding covenant from Him, the limits and duties of each, according to His Book or the Sunna of the Prophet:

> وَكُلٌّ قَدْ سَمَّى اللهُ سَهْمَهُ، وَوَضَعَ عَلَى حَدِّهِ وَفَرِيضَتِهِ فِي كِتَابِهِ أَوْ سُنَّةِ نَبِيِّهِ (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَآلِهِ) عَهْداً مِنْهُ عِنْدَنَا مَحْفُوظاً

God has ordained the share of every one of them and laid down His covenant about the limits of each in His Book and the Sunna of His Prophet by way of a settlement which is preserved with us.

The governor's duty to society is therefore part of the universal covenant between man and God — the 'ahd (عہد) that recurs so often in the Quran. By reaching for this Quranic term to reinforce the duty of acting justly toward every class, the Imam knew that the weight of his words would be deepened immeasurably by their echo of verses such as these:

> أَفَمَن يَعْلَمُ أَنَّمَآ أُنزِلَ إِلَيْكَ مِن رَّبِّكَ ٱلْحَقُّ كَمَنْ هُوَ أَعْمَىٰٓ ۚ إِنَّمَا يَتَذَكَّرُ أُو۟لُوا۟ ٱلْأَلْبَبِ ۱۹ ٱلَّذِينَ يُوفُونَ بِعَهْدِ ٱللَّهِ وَلَا يَنقُضُونَ ٱلْمِيثَقَ ۲۰

'Is one who knoweth that what is revealed to thee from thy Lord is the truth like one who is blind? But only men of substance take heed, those who abide by the covenant (ahd) of God, and break not the tryst (mithaq) (Ar-Ra'd: 19-20); and:

> وَلَا تَقْرَبُوا۟ مَالَ ٱلْيَتِيمِ إِلَّا بِٱلَّتِى هِىَ أَحْسَنُ حَتَّىٰ يَبْلُغَ أَشُدَّهُۥ ۚ وَأَوْفُوا۟ بِٱلْعَهْدِ ۖ إِنَّ ٱلْعَهْدَ كَانَ مَسْـُٔولًۭا ۳۴

'Approach not the wealth of the orphan except with that which is more right, until he attains maturity; and abide by the covenant, truly, the covenant will be asked about' (Al-Isra: 34).

It is significant, too, that just before those instructions the Imam advises Malik to study long with the 'scholars' (al-ulama) and to converse long with the 'sages' (al-hukama):

> وَأَكْثِرْ مُدَارَسَةَ الَعُلَمَاءِ، وَمُنَافَثَةَ الْحُكَمَاءِ، فِي تَثْبِيتِ مَا صَلَحَ عَلَيْهِ أَمْرُ بِلاَدِكَ، وَإِقَامَةِ مَا اسْتَقَامَ بِهِ النَّاسُ قَبْلَكَ

The aim is to 'consolidate that which brings well-being to your lands, and to further entrench that which has already been established by your predecessors.' The distinction between scholars and sages matters here. The scholars (عُلَمَاء) are those who know the formal rules as set down in the Quran and the Sunna — the letter of the law, the Sharia (شریعۃ). The sages (حکما) are those who know how to apply that knowledge; theirs is the spirit of wisdom, for wisdom is knowledge put into practice. It is the distinction between the letter of the law and the spirit behind it — the spirit without which the rules cannot be applied with care and sensitivity.

There follows a passage that describes, with admirable concision, how each class or segment of society depends on the others. It begins with the army as the foundation of the state and ends with the governor himself as the center around which the various elements turn. The image is that of a wheel. The governor is the hub, working on the horizontal plane with creation; the classes — the army, the scribes, the executive officers, the dispensers of justice, the tax-collectors, the merchants, the artisans, and the lowest class of the needy and destitute — are the spokes that radiate from him to the rim. Yet the hub itself is nothing without the axle that turns it. This horizontal center is nothing without the vertical axis that joins it, and through it the whole of society, to the Real. As a hub is useless without its link to the axle, so all the governor's efforts are useless without his link to the Absolute, on which he must depend wholly if society is truly to flourish. The soldiers are called the 'fortresses' of the subjects; and at the close of the passage the governor is told that he cannot accomplish what God requires of him without resolute determination and recourse to Him (al-ihtimam and al-istiana). This point is reinforced later in the letter, where the needs of the poor are addressed. As for the instructions concerning the various classes of state and society, a few salient points are enough. Of the commander of the troops, Malik is told to choose the man who is 'the most sincere in relation to God, the Prophet and your Imam, the purest of heart, the one most excellent in forbearance (hilm), who is slow to anger, happy to pardon, kind to the weak, severe with the strong; one who is neither stirred by violence nor held back by weakness.'

This portrait of the ideal commander reflects the Imam's own character. What stands out is the stress laid on kindness, gentleness, and forbearance — qualities not usually linked with fighting men. Here is a spiritually refined approach to an activity that, left to itself, easily tramples the norms of decency and propriety. Once again the Imam's description of the perfect warrior resembles Plato's description of the 'guardian', the protector of the ideal state. Plato states the central difficulty in raising a force to protect and defend the city: how are men whose nature is 'spirited' and courageous 'to be kept from behaving pugnaciously to one another and to the rest of their countrymen'? For 'they must be gentle to their own people and dangerous only to their enemies.' This echoes the Quranic description of those with the Prophet as 'severe against the disbelievers, compassionate among themselves' (Al-Fath: 29) — and it is why the Imam insists that the gentle qualities in a warrior are the essential complement to the martial ones.

For the next category, the judges, the Imam names the key virtues that those who dispense legal justice must have: they must not be confused by complexity or provoked by litigants; they must be unflinching before the truth, free from greed, unsatisfied by superficial solutions, rigorous in argument, steadfast in seeking the truth of every matter, and proof against praise and temptation. 'Such people,' the Imam adds, 'are indeed rare.' He also urges that judges be paid generously, 'so that any deficiency will be removed, thus diminishing the need for help from people' — a condition for keeping any judiciary incorruptible.

For the administrators, Malik is told to test their honesty before employing them, and to take scrupulous care that no partiality or favouritism sways his choices. He must go on watching and investigating their conduct. As already noted, stringent punishments are to follow whenever there is evidence of abuse of privilege or misappropriation of public funds.

Of those who till the soil, from whom the land tax is collected, the Imam offers this wise maxim: 'Let your concern with the cultivation of the land outweigh your concern with the collection of the tax, for no tax will be collected if there be no cultivation. And whoever exacts the tax without cultivating the land ruins the land and destroys the people.' His further urgings — to attend to the needs of the tillers and to deal fairly with all who seek redress of wrongs — close with this warning:

The devastation of the land only comes about through the destitution of its inhabitants; and the destitution of its inhabitants only comes about when the desire to amass wealth rules the souls of the governors, when they have doubts about what endures, and when they profit little from exemplary teachings.

As for the phrase 'having doubts about what endures' (su zannihim bil-baqa), the commentators offer two readings. It may mean that the governors think they will endure for ever, forgetting that they will die and be judged in the Hereafter; or that they doubt their position will last, and so seize whatever opportunities they can while they can. The first reading seems the more likely, so closely does it echo the Quranic verses below — verses that may themselves be counted among the 'exemplary teachings' (ibra) the Imam names at the very end of the sentence: 'Woe be to every slandering backbiter, who amasseth wealth and counteth it. He thinketh that his wealth will render him immortal. Nay, but he will be flung to the all-consuming Fire' (Al-Humazah: 1-4).

The Imam uses the same word, jamaa, for the amassing of wealth that occurs in this verse. The lesson is plain: greed is a symptom of spiritual short-sightedness. To forget what truly endures gives rise to greed in its various degrees — greed understood here as the desire for a false material richness in place of a true spiritual fullness — and this in turn breeds exploitation and oppression. Once more the Imam's vision shows how the vice of injustice not only produces corruption but is itself the product of a chain of causes rooted in a fundamental inversion of spiritual values, a failure to see the true nature of things.

Conscience, Compassion, and the Ordering of Society

The same vision that traces injustice to a disorder within the soul governs the more practical counsel that follows, in which the ordering of society is shown to rest on the conscience of those who hold authority. The advice on appointing scribes is itself sealed with a spiritual message. Malik must resist the temptation to appoint only those who have shown him their best side. The governor is to weigh each candidate's past record together with the impression he has left on ordinary people, and then appoint only those well known for trustworthiness. All of this is called 'proof of your sincerity toward God.' The choice of officials, then, is not a matter of administration alone; it is a measure of the ruler's own truthfulness before his Lord, and the care he takes in it answers to God rather than to the men who court his favor.

Merchants and craftsmen the Imam then describes as those who bring society many benefits and must therefore be treated well. On the whole they are a peaceable class, and should be left in peace to carry on their work. Yet the vices to which they are prone must be held in check: hoarding, miserliness, and cheating. Here again the outward regulation of trade rests on an inward discernment — one that distinguishes the honest labor which enriches a community from the greed that preys upon it.

<!-- editorial:begin -->
> **Editorial note (tradition-grounded).** 
> The primordial "Am I not your Lord?" and the letters appointing Egypt's governors are bound by a
> single word. In the wider tradition the root -h-d (ع-ه-د) is the language of solemn promise —
> to bind oneself by a pledge and be charged with keeping it. Its noun, ahd, names a covenant of
> either kind: the compact between God and humanity that each soul affirms before the Day of
> Resurrection, and the charge of office that Ali lays on his governor Malik al-Ashtar. Read side
> by side, the two are one obligation seen at two scales — the witness a soul gives to its Lord
> and the trust a ruler accepts over others spring from the same act of being pledged and held to
> keeping it. This is why the greater the power placed in a hand, the heavier the covenant that
> comes with it.
<!-- editorial:end -->

## 9. Justice for the Poor and the Sincerity of Intention

The Imam next turns Malik toward those who need justice most, opening the passage with the exclamation "Allah, Allah" to mark its urgency. His instruction concerns those who have no resources at all: the destitute, the crippled, orphans, and the elderly. These are the people most in need of justice from a governor, and they must be treated so that "God may excuse you on the day you meet Him." All of them, together with others who are in need yet too proud to beg, are to be provided for by the governor. He ought to appoint an officer charged specifically with uncovering the needs of the most destitute and attending to them. The Imam then adds words that reach the heart of the matter: "This is onerous for the governors, and [the fulfillment of] all rights is onerous, but God makes it light for those who aspire to the Hereafter, who restrain their souls in patience, and trust in the truth of that which is promised them by God."

This spiritual dimension is precisely what makes an otherwise crushing ideal workable. Divine help is assured to those whose attention is not confined to this world but whose aspirations reach beyond it to the Hereafter. Only when this world is seen through to the next does a fully just attitude toward this world become possible. Those whose talab, their aspiration, does not reach beyond this world are far more likely to take the easy path and neglect the destitute; for in the horizontal calculus of politics the cost to a ruler of a policy of charity toward the poor may well outweigh any benefit he can see for himself. An ethics tied only to this-worldly reckoning, to pragmatic politics, or to personal interest exposes its inherent flaws here. But when the idea of what is owed to the poor is charged with firm faith in God's inexorable justice and infinite compassion, and filled with aspiration for the Hereafter, an unflinching fidelity to the needs of the destitute is generated and continually renewed.

Such an attitude goes far beyond ordinary notions of charity or generosity. A person who has realized it fully cannot be swayed by any contrary suggestion rooted in self-serving pragmatism or individualism, still less by indifference or willfulness. Once this attitude is firmly in place, the next quality the Imam names, patience, follows naturally. When the absolute values at stake are sensed concretely rather than merely in the abstract, patience can be extended without limit, for it no longer depends on personal will alone but is sustained by the empowering grace that flows from heartfelt faith. The Imam completes the point by observing that those with the right aspirations therefore trust in the truth of God's promise. If that promise, of His reward, His unfailing justice, His infinite compassion, is true, then the motive behind the governor's effort to show mercy to the poor and deal justly with them is immeasurably deepened. The task the Imam calls onerous, the duty to help the weak and helpless, those whose assistance yields no political benefit, is transformed into an inescapable obligation that follows directly from spiritual conviction. That conviction rests on the certainty that such generosity and compassion are not merely good, right, and proper, but accord with the true and inalienable substance of ultimate reality. The moral implications of spiritual reality, and equally the spiritual consequences of virtue, are hidden from worldly rulers by their covert prejudices at best and their overt vices at worst. Such rulers are also blind to the divine promise of ultimate felicity, and equally blind to the warnings of retribution against the oppressors, al-zalimun. The zalim here may be understood as one who defies the divine commandments concerning justice and, more fundamentally, violates the compassion that lies at the very heart of the divine nature.

These considerations open onto the crucial question of intention. Even when the just person's effort to live by justice is frustrated by the unpredictability of worldly life, his right intentions, together with his awareness of the immutable divine qualities as the models and principles that guide his work, produce a real, inward "taste" of the grace that gives his moral life its spiritual foundation. If God's promise is true, and one is absolutely certain of that truth, then the effect of that truth upon the soul of the sincere believer turns a personal ethic into an unconditional imperative, and a desirable value into an undeniable reality. Morality is thus deepened by spirituality, and into it: an all-encompassing mode of being in which the burden of individual duty is not only immeasurably lightened but becomes part of one's taste of spiritual reality. One then acts justly out of gratitude for what one has already received, the joy of spiritual certainty, and not only out of hope for something not yet attained.

The Quran speaks of the reward of Paradise as something already granted to the true believer; for one whose certainty of Paradise is absolute, the future has already arrived: "We have given thee [the paradisal fountain of] al-Kawthar; so pray to thy Lord and sacrifice ..." (Al-Kawthar: 1-2). To worship God because the blessedness to come is certain, and has therefore, in one respect, already come, is well expressed in the following saying of the Imam:

Indeed there is a group who worship God out of desire [for something not yet attained]; and this is the worship of the merchants. And there is a group who worship God out of fear, and this is the worship of the slaves. And there is a group who worship God out of gratitude, and this is the worship of the free.

The just person is, furthermore, freed from the material consequences of his actions, in proportion to the rectitude and sincerity of his intentions, since in the Islamic view actions are judged not by their consequences but by their intentions. His intention is to be just: not for the sake of any earthly reward or tangible outcome in this world, but purely for the sake of justice itself. And this essence, or principle, of justice is in turn inseparable from the divine nature. Justice is at one with God not simply because whatever God commands is just; rather, God commands just acts precisely because they are just, and because justice is one with His own nature: "I bear witness that He is Justice and He acts justly." Justice is not one attribute among others, something merely present in the divine nature; it is that nature. Were it only in His nature it would stand alongside His other qualities; but because it is His very essence, all His other qualities essentially flow from it, so that His mercy no less than His wrath, His creation no less than His destruction, all proceed from His justice. If God acts justly by virtue of His very nature, which is pure justice, then the spiritual person will act justly out of a desire to conform to God's nature, and not merely out of obedience to His commands, and never out of desire for any earthly reward.

This can be seen in any single virtue. When one speaks the truth without grasping the reason for it — that الحق is the very nature of the Creator, and that the goal in this world is to take on the qualities of that Creator — truthfulness soon becomes situational and self-serving, open to compromise whenever circumstances seem to warrant it: the truth is told when it brings advantage and set aside when a lie brings advantage instead. But once truth is seen as the nature of the divine, it becomes everlasting and eternal, and the consequences of speaking it cease to matter; one speaks it simply in emulation of الحق, the absolute truth. Virtue practiced on an ad hoc basis is no virtue at all, merely a series of pragmatic adjustments to shifting circumstances. This is why the life of Ali (ع), when studied, reflects an unflinching adherence to the divine virtues: his was a life governed not by his نفس but wholly by the divine nature of Allah, his every celebrated deed the fruit of an irresistible attraction toward the nature of الحق. His gaze was never fixed on the action itself but on the source of the principles that gave rise to it; by fixing his attention on the beauty of the divine nature and seeking to take it on, good actions emerged from him naturally and inevitably. As the Quran says:

The most pious will be far removed from it [the punishment of Hell], he who giveth of his wealth to purify himself. And nobody possesseth, in the sight of such a person, any favor that might be bestowed on him as reward [for his generosity]. [His action is performed] seeking only the Countenance of his transcendent Lord, and he will be content (Al-Layl: 17-21).

The same principle appears in the description of the righteous, the abrar. Commentators on the Quran agree that the occasion for the revelation, sabab al-nuzul, of the following verses was a time when Ali (ع) and his family went three days in a row without food. At the end of each day, just as they were about to break their fast, someone in greater need came and asked for what they had. The Quran relates it thus: "They feed, out of love for God, the needy, the orphan and the prisoner, saying, 'We feed you only for the sake of God; we desire from you neither reward nor thanks'" (Al-Insan: 8-9).

In the very measure that a good action is performed for the sake of that absolute goodness which God is, the one who performs it is serenely content, untouched by the blame or praise of others, neither cast down by outward adversity that thwarts his good actions nor puffed up by their success. This understanding of the relation between action and intention illuminates the Imam's saying that "their hearts are in the Gardens [of Paradise] while their bodies are at work." Here too there is a difference between a secular ethic of justice, one without any spiritual or transcendental point of reference, and a spiritual conception of it. A secular ethic stands or falls by the degree of outward success in realizing its ideal of justice; a spiritual conception already sees its ideal fulfilled in the sincere intention to be just. Even so, the fulfillment of a spiritual ideal of justice essentially requires an unyielding struggle to achieve it, for without that struggle the intention is shown to lack sincerity. Without matching action, aspirations and intentions remain shallow and merely theoretical. To hold an intention with total sincerity strictly requires diligent, ceaseless striving toward its realization. And so, however formidable the obstacles, however persuasive the arguments of political pragmatism, however thoroughly his strenuous efforts are undone by the unavoidable demands of outward life, the just person never despairs; he never gives up the effort to achieve justice, precisely because achieving justice in this world is not the whole of his aim. He knows that the intention is already, in an invisible but spiritually palpable way, its own fulfillment; and he knows, with sober realism, that the outward realization of any intention depends on circumstances beyond his control and, ultimately, on the grace of God.

On the surface this may look as though it excuses the failure to achieve justice in society, and could serve as a pretext by which real injustice is justified through an appeal to good but thwarted intentions. In fact the opposite is true. Far from implying any weakness of resolve in one moved by a sacred conception of justice, such a person's efforts are on the contrary as ceaseless and unyielding as the eternal and unfailing reward in which he fervently believes. Those efforts are also sustained by an awareness of the subtle continuity between just intentions and the divine quality of justice, and through it the divine nature itself. An inalienable sense of the sacred upholds the just person in his pursuit of justice. The claim is not that every secular or atheistic conception of justice is without value; only that, set beside a sacred conception, such views lack the immeasurable and inexhaustible spiritual resources that sincere faith nourishes. The theories that dominate the present age bear this out. Relativism holds that knowledge, truth, and morality are not absolute but exist only in relation to culture, society, or historical context. Nihilism rejects all religious and moral principle and holds that life is essentially meaningless. Utilitarianism counts an action right only insofar as it is useful or benefits the majority, reducing good and bad to a calculus of gain and loss with no fundamental principle beneath them. And opportunism seizes whatever advantage presents itself regardless of principle, after the manner of the manipulator who flatters, performs, and pretends, saying or doing whatever it takes to get what he wants. These are human constructions, and the world arrived at them by severing practice from principle. Whatever value emerges from them is not rooted in the divine, so that even where they coincide with the values of faith they do so by accident, and remain, for all their machinery, without spirit, dead corpses. "With men this is impossible, but with God all things are possible," as Jesus says in the Gospel. Put into the present terms, the principle might be stated thus: when justice is conceived apart from spiritual principles, personal idealism is acutely vulnerable to outward circumstance and all too likely to fall apart under the pressure of difficulty and disappointment; in a sacred conception of justice, by contrast, divine grace holds the incorruptible ideal of justice far above the frailties and vicissitudes to which both the human soul and human society are subject. In the measure that one's ideals are rooted in spiritual knowledge, one's vulnerability to disappointment and despair in the face of difficulty is lessened.

The importance of this ideal of justice is plain in the Imam's own experience as caliph. An unbroken succession of bloody conflicts against enemies who professed Islam kept him from enacting, in outward fact, many of the principles and policies that flowed from his conception of truth and justice. Yet none of this produced dejection, despair, or cynicism, nor did it lessen in the slightest his effort to achieve justice wherever and whenever he could, as the quality of his own administration amply shows, along with the steady stream of letters and counsels to his officers and governors, the letter to Malik among them. Such despair is almost unthinkable in one whose inner life is shaped by the conviction that "No act is negligible if it is accompanied by piety." For such a person, despair is ruled out by piety in the deepest sense of the word, the permanent awareness of divine Reality; for piety of this degree is the makhraj, the "way out," from the darkness of all earthly injustice. As the Imam says, "He who has pious awe toward God, for him God establishes a way out of tribulation, and a light out of darkness (man yattiqiLlah yajal lahu makhrajan min al-fitan wa nuran min al-zulam)."

<!-- editorial:begin -->

> The chapter's insistence that deeds are weighed by intention rather than outcome finds a natural
> complement in a related teaching preserved in this tradition: that knowledge is of no benefit
> until it is put into action, and action is of no benefit until it is accepted by God — and the
> only thing God accepts is sincerity. On that view the governor's mercy toward the destitute
> counts not for the political result it never secures but for the sincerity it carries. The wider
> tradition roots such striving in the Quranic purpose of creation itself — "I created not jinn
> and mankind except that they should worship Me" (Adh-Dhariyat: 56) — and reads worship in its truest sense
> as pure monotheism of the Originator. This clarifies the Imam's saying that the highest servants
> worship out of gratitude, not out of the merchant's desire for reward or the slave's fear of
> punishment.
<!-- editorial:end -->

## 10. Prayer, Remembrance, and Gazing on the Good

The lynchpin of the whole epistle now comes into view: prayer, the most important means by which the ruler takes into himself the sacred substance of all the virtues. At the opening of the letter the Imam had begun with a counsel to observe the obligatory prayers. Here, after stressing that every administrative task be performed at its proper time, he instructs Malik to set apart the most excellent portion of his available time for what lies between himself and God, that is, for acts of worship and devotion. Malik is urged to give special attention to the obligatory prayers, the means by which he purifies his religion for God and renders his devotion to Him sincere. This is more than an exhortation to perform the formal prayers. It must be understood above all in the light of that deeper purification of one's religion for God, the purifying of the very substance of the relationship between the soul and God, for the depth of that relationship is what determines the quality of all human virtue.
