As the Imam put it in a saying already quoted, "Know that your every act is dependent upon your prayer" — that is, every action depends upon the way one gazes upon God, the source of all good. What matters is not only the formal, physical, and verbal performance of the prayer, but the inward quality that accompanies it and enlivens it from within. Sincerity, intensity, and frequency are essential to its efficacy. The Imam continues, "Give unto God of your vital energy in your nights and your days, and perform fully that by which you draw near to God, doing so perfectly, without becoming dull or deficient, taking your body to its limits (balighan min badanika ma balagha)."

The whole emphasis here falls on the word taqarrub, drawing near. Divine nearness is attained most directly through deep devotion, offered up alone, in the dead of night, with all one's heart. Once that nearness is realized, all one's other actions in the world are imbued with something of the spiritual quality gained through divine proximity. In this way all action can be assimilated to devotion in the wider sense, every outward action being truly governed by the sincere intention to give oneself to God. If the inner life is dominated by prayer, prayer comes to penetrate the outer life as well. Hence, immediately after telling Malik to set apart the best of his time for God, the Imam adds, "all times [and the actions performed therein] are for God if the intention underlying them is good and if your subjects derive security as a consequence." The actions of a ruler, in other words, will partake of the sacred to the degree that his devotion truly reaches into the source of the sacred. One may then envisage an overflow, as it were, from the fountain of obligatory and voluntary prayer into the outward domain of action. This subtle truth is expressed in the famous hadith qudsi of the Prophet, often called the hadith of taqarrub:

> My slave draws near to Me through nothing I love more than that which I have made obligatory for him. My slave never ceases to draw near to Me through supererogatory acts until I love him. And when I love him, I am his hearing by which he hears, his sight by which he sees, his hand by which he grasps, and his foot by which he walks.

To see and to act through God can be understood, at one level, as seeing things as they really are and then acting upon that vision — which is to say perfect justice, the placing of each thing in its proper order. This is why the Imam repeatedly draws the reader of his sermons back to the vision of God, setting that vision as the true foundation of a life of virtue. In prayer the ego is effaced through prostration, and this submission to God through self-effacement is itself what puts all things in their right place — including, and especially, the ego. The hadith qudsi marks a distinction between the obligatory and the supererogatory acts of devotion. Both are means of drawing near, but it is chiefly through the latter that the slave comes to perceive and act through the divine. The Imam's phrase, taking your body to its limits, carries the same weight. Strenuous effort is called for; it is far from enough to perform the prayers and devotions in a mechanical or perfunctory manner. "He who prays without making an effort," says the Imam, "is like one who shoots arrows without a bow." The sincerity (ikhlas) demanded by the Imam's conception of the oneness of God calls, as noted earlier, for the integration of the soul, and it is in prayer that the deepest dimensions of the soul are expressed, cultivated, and integrated. The prayer must therefore be accomplished not merely with the tongue or the body, but with all that one is.

Elsewhere, Ali (ع) points to the key supererogatory practice by which the spiritual station of nearness is attained. He does so in a commentary on the words of a Quranic verse, "… men whom neither commerce nor trade diverts from the remembrance of God" (An-Nur: 36).

> Truly God has made the remembrance (al-dhikr) a polish for the hearts, by which they hear after suffering from deafness, and see after being blind … There have always been slaves of God … with whom He held intimate discourse through their thoughts and spoke with them through the essence of their intellects. They diffused illumination through the awakened light in their hearing and their seeing and their hearts, calling unto the remembrance of the days of God.

The dhikr is paramount in the Imam's perspective, both as a principle of awareness of ultimate reality and as a concrete means of attaining that awareness. By the word dhikr the Imam implies both a methodic practice of invoking a divine Name and a principle of permanent awareness of God that embraces every mode of worship, meditation, and reflection. It is to be understood not only as being aware of God or remembering God, but also as an intense mode of prolonged, prayerful meditation on ultimate reality, represented both symbolically and sacramentally by one or more of the divine Names. This remembrance is not the learning of new things from scratch. It is the recollection of ultimate reality — of the immutable, unchanging principles and divine attributes that are inherent in the very substance of our intellects, by which everything can be understood, and which are ultimately rooted in the divine nature of our Creator. This aspect of remembrance receives fuller treatment in the chapter that follows. The relationship between the remembrance of God and the cultivation of justice is made clear in this sermon, where the Imam describes those illuminated by the dhikr:

> Truly there are people who belong to the dhikr; they have adopted it in place of the world, such that "neither commerce nor trade" distracts them from it; they spend the days of their life in it … they instruct people about justice and themselves are steadfast therein.

It is those souls who truly belong to the dhikr who are not only steadfast in justice but also able to instruct others in it. Putting first things first, they can show how to place all things in their proper order. Fervent, lived spirituality is therefore not opposed to dedicated activity in the world; it infuses that activity with an ethical depth that springs from direct contact with the divine, a contact articulated by prayer in general and by the dhikr in particular. Just as the sincerity of a good intention is proven by the effort to realize it, so the sincerity of prayer is proven, and rendered efficacious, through the sustained effort to prolong and intensify it — taking your body to its limits. What this has to do with justice is plain. If justice is grasped as a quality rooted in the Real (al-Haqq), then nearness to the Real cannot but draw one nearer to justice and to all positive qualities, spiritual, intellectual, and moral. The relationship between worship and justice must therefore be firmly established if the conception of justice is to be qualified as sacred. From this perspective, justice is both cause and consequence of worship. On the one hand, justice requires that everything be given its due, and God must accordingly be given His due, which entails, sacramentally, worship and prayer. On the other hand, justice emerges ethically as a consequence of that worship, as do all the virtues, in proportion to the nearness granted by God to His worshipper and in accordance with the radiant repercussions of that nearness.

In stressing prayer, together with its essence, the remembrance of God, the Imam offers a devotional principle that illuminates and, in a sense, sublimates Plato's philosophical position. It might be seen as making explicit in spiritual terms what remains implicit in Plato's pedagogy. After discussing the question of justice at great length, Socrates comes to the crux of the matter, the nature of the Sovereign Good, which is of paramount concern to the ideal ruler — the guardian, whose moral and intellectual formation lies at the heart of the dialogue's practical dimension:

> … the highest object of knowledge is the essential nature of the Good, from which everything that is good and right derives its value for us … I need not tell you that, without that knowledge, to know everything else, however well, would be of no value to us …

Socrates declines to answer Glaucon's entreaties to describe the nature of this Good, offering instead to speak of its offspring. This leads him to draw an analogy between the Good and the Sun: what the Sun is to visibility, the Good is to intelligibility and being. He proceeds with the famous allegory of the cave, and then describes the educational program that would turn the attention of his prospective rulers from the world of shadows to the world of real objects — a program comprising mathematics, arithmetic, geometry, solid geometry, astronomy, harmonics, and dialectic. Having passed successfully through all its stages, "when they are fifty, those who have come safely through and proved the best at all points in action and in study must be brought at last to the goal. They must lift up the eye of the soul to gaze on that which sheds light on all things; and when they have seen the Good itself, take it as a pattern for the right ordering of the state and of the individual, themselves included." Yet Plato nowhere states what this gazing actually means, nor how it is to be achieved, by what means — intellective, contemplative, or methodic.

At an important point in the Republic, Glaucon presses Socrates on the definition of the Sovereign Good: "But, Socrates, what is your account of the Good? Is it knowledge, or pleasure, or something else?" Socrates refuses to give a direct answer, saying after some further discussion, "I am afraid it is beyond my powers; with the best will in the world I should only disgrace myself and be laughed at." Very much in the manner of Glaucon, Dhilib al-Yamani provocatively asked Ali (ع) whether he had seen the object of his devotion. The Imam replied, "I would not be worshipping a lord whom I have not seen." Dhilib then asked, "O Commander of the Faithful! How did you see Him?" The Imam answered with a sentence that serves as one of the leitmotifs of this book: "O Dhilib! Eyes see Him not through sight's observation, but hearts see Him through the verities of faith."

The polishing of the heart effected by remembrance, by ذکر, comes to mind here, for this very word evokes the essence of Plato's approach to knowledge as recollection, anamnesis. Remembrance in this sense is not the learning of something new from scratch, but the recollection of ultimate reality — of the immutable divine principles and attributes inherent in the very substance of our intellects, by which everything else can be understood. Yet in none of his dialogues does Plato make a direct reference to the devotional, methodic, or contemplative counterpart of his educational, ethical, and intellective endeavor. He posits no explicit link between remembrance of the forms or ideas that engender true knowledge and the gazing upon the Good, the contemplation of that which sheds light on all things. It is not merely knowing and remembering the forms, but coming to know the Bestower of Forms, referred to in Islam by the divine Name al-Musawwir. Whether there was any spiritual method in Plato's teaching, and if there was, why he did not divulge it, remains a matter of speculation. One answer may be gathered from a statement in Plato's famous Seventh Letter, where he denies that any exposition can be given of the true reality of philosophy, and points instead to the disciplined way of life and rigorous daily routine that the subject demands if philosophy in the deepest sense is to be realized. "No treatise by me concerning it exists or ever will exist. It is not something that can be put into words like other branches of learning; only after long partnership in a common life devoted to this very thing does truth flash upon the soul, like a flame kindled by a leaping spark, and once it is born there, it nourishes itself thereafter." This returns to the importance of active spirituality for the practice of authentic virtue. If the Real, indefinable in its essence, is the foundation of all good, then the means of assimilating the Real existentially, and not merely comprehending it mentally, cannot be set aside. Plato's philosophical approach to the Good can be augmented and deepened by an appreciation of Ali (ع)'s means of drawing near to the Real, for in the Imam's perspective intellectual comprehension and spiritual devotion are inseparable and complementary. Integral, deep-rooted justice emerges as one of the fruits of this drawing near, just as, for Plato, gazing on that which sheds light on all things, the Good itself, results in the capacity to take the Good as a pattern for the right ordering of the state and of the individual.

These pages have sketched the outlines of a spiritual approach to the theme of justice as it presents itself principally in the Imam's letter to Malik al-Ashtar. Only the surface of this important subject has been skimmed, yet the Imam's illumination of the principle of justice — addressed in the first instance to a prospective governor of Egypt in the first century of the Islamic era — is by no means outmoded. Whether one operates in an avowedly Islamic or an overtly secular environment, whether the rule be autocratic or democratic, whether political institutions be strong or weak — wherever individuals are vested with power and authority, and by that very token exposed to the temptations that power offers, the approach to justice articulated in the Imam's ethos will not be irrelevant. This ethos presupposes no particular form of government. It engages the universal theme of moral responsibility, but does so in terms of that which transcends the level of morality, offering insights derived from a direct vision of ultimate spiritual realities. The principles contemplated through this vision surpass the domain of politics, and for that very reason are capable of penetrating deeply and refashioning, from above, the moral substance of political consciousness. Although the Imam's perspective is particularly pertinent to the interface between spirituality, morality, and governance within Islamic discourse and praxis, its relevance extends to every context in which the sense of the sacred plays a part in the articulation of moral sensibility and the cultivation of political responsibility.

The Imam's letters and discourses are a vast and largely untapped source of inspired ideas, capable of illuminating the role of spiritual principles in formulating ethical values and shaping the quality of political practice. In particular, any integral Islamic approach to politics and society must take into account the requirements and fruits of worship, understood in the widest sense. Sincere worship transforms the notion of the Absolute into a concrete reality, imparting to the soul an existential impetus and a spiritual resource that buttresses, stabilizes, and deepens one's moral life. What is derived from consciousness of the divine Reality is not so much a philosophy of ethics as a spiritual morality — an existential orientation that is at once the consequence of a lived spirituality and the cause of its continuous, dynamic, and ever-deepening realization of spiritual truth, the Real as such, al-Haqq. This means that the haqq, the right due to each person or group, will be given in accordance with the principle of justice, that of putting each thing in its proper place, وَضَعَ الشَّئُی حَقَّ مَحَلِّہِ. Virtue, from such a perspective, is a form of ethical realism — where realism denotes an assimilation of spiritual reality, and morally correct behavior translates that realism into action across every plane, individual, social, and political.

Should it be objected that this perspective is hopelessly utopian, the harsh exigencies of politics and the sublime ideals of spirituality being poles apart, the reply is this. If no effort is made to comprehend the deeper dimensions of Islamic piety and spirituality, then any ostensibly Islamic discourse — whether in politics, sociology, ethics, or any other field — will be reduced to an outer shell, stripped of the inner life that gives it all its meaning and, more importantly, its transformative power. For Islamic concepts to be grasped aright, they must be assimilated not merely by the mind but by the heart, failing which they remain mere abstractions. As the Imam insists, it is the heart, the inmost point of consciousness, that sees the true nature of things. This is why the vision of God as if one could see Him lays bare the crucial relationship between worship and virtue, between spirituality and ethics, and equally between devotion to the Creator and kindness toward His creation. One cannot have the one without the other, for to conduct oneself as though one could see God is to live in the consciousness of standing constantly in the divine presence, since even if we cannot see Him, He sees us. Without this vision there is no ihsan in the ethico-spiritual sense discussed earlier, and without ihsan the very integrity of the Islamic way of life is fatally undermined. It is precisely the denial and subversion of this tradition that bears ultimate responsibility for the atrocities committed by a tiny minority of Muslims in our times — people who betray the highest ideals of the Islamic ethical tradition by resorting to unbridled violence in its name, as though one might hurt creation in order to please the Creator. Without ihsan, religion is left acutely vulnerable, transformed into an ideology, driven by individual passion and subject to political manipulation. It is no surprise that those most prone to terrorism in the name of Islam have emerged from the currents most hostile to the ihsani or mystical tradition.

This returns to the devotional practices that are all too often regarded as the preserve of a minority — viewed as either mystical or misguided, depending on one's standpoint — rather than being understood as a God-given means of narrowing the gap between what one aspires to and what one is, of moving from the actual toward the ideal, of making concrete what would otherwise remain abstract, of living what is only thought about, and of rendering existentially galvanizing what might otherwise be merely theoretically compelling. While the principles established here are universal in their import and applicability, it would be wrong to decontextualize the Imam's perspective by dismissing, as so much unnecessary superstructure, his repeated, insistent, and uncompromising emphasis on the imperative of prayer. The principle of justice requires that all the elements of one's life — devotion, worship, ethics, action — be bound together in a harmonious, indissoluble, and interconnected whole, in accordance with the ideal of تَوْحِيد. And تَوْحِيد means precisely this integration, the making of many into one, so that when a person's theory and practice have themselves become one, that person has become a true bearer of that oneness. There can be no question of detaching the domain of the virtues from that of spirituality, for in the Imam's perspective the only true virtue is one that is rooted in, sustained by, and oriented toward the divine Reality. A virtue that is not so rooted — even one that happens by coincidence to mirror an Islamic value — is baseless and without spirit. Cut off from its source, it becomes situational and self-serving, open to compromise the moment circumstances warrant it, and ceases to be a virtue at all, dwindling into a mere series of pragmatic adaptations to shifting conditions. Justice, together with all the other virtues, can be brought to fruition only in a soul, and in a society, permeated and penetrated by spirituality, the essence of which is the principle and practice of the remembrance of God. And this remembrance, this dhikr, is not the learning of something entirely new. It is a recollection of the ultimate Reality — a recollection of the immutable principles and unchanging qualities inherent in the very substance of our intellects, principles by which everything else can be understood, and which are ultimately rooted in the divine nature of our Creator. Remembrance of this kind is nothing less than the cognitive and philosophical foundation of ethical thought in Islam. In the last analysis, it is the remembrance of God that determines the quality of human virtue. It is to the spiritual realization intended and cultivated by this remembrance that the following chapter is addressed.

<!-- editorial:begin -->
> **Editorial note (tradition-grounded).** 
> This chapter's talk of prayer "integrating the soul" rests on an understanding the wider
> tradition makes explicit: the self is singular in essence, yet its forces emerge at different
> times and stations — first the mere power to grow in the womb, named the vegetative soul, then
> the awakening of the five senses, and onward. To integrate the soul is thus to draw these graded
> faculties back toward the one essence from which they arise. A related teaching in this
> tradition also roots the soul in the human heart, which lets the dhikr that "polishes the
> hearts" work precisely where those faculties are gathered — a reminder that the remembrance the
> Imam commends is less an act of the tongue than a recollecting of the whole self toward its
> single source.
<!-- editorial:end -->

## 11. Dhikr: The Polish of the Heart

The subject here is the meaning of dhikruLlah, 'the remembrance of God', one of the most fundamental principles in Islamic spirituality. It is approached in two ways: through the sayings of the Imam, read essentially as commentaries on the Quranic idea of dhikruLlah, and through the doctrinal formulations that have grown up within the mystical traditions of Islam. To speak of Ali (ع) is to speak of the very heart of Islamic spirituality. His influence is felt wherever spirituality has flourished in Islam — in the more clearly defined disciplines of Sufism and Shii 'Irfan, and equally in the realms of futuwwa (spiritual chivalry), the craft guilds, music, poetry and the arts. As the gate to the city of prophetic wisdom, he leaves his mark on all the noblest expressions of the spirit of Islam.

Within the Sufi tradition in particular there is a uniquely organic bond with the Imam. The Sufi orders differ in every outward way — Shii or Sunni, Arab or Persian, Turk or Malay, Mauritanian or Bosnian — yet they are united in the figure of Ali ibn Abi Talib (ع), who stands at the summit of every chain of affiliation (salasila, sing. silsila) by which the masters trace their spiritual lineage back to the Prophet. This unifying role appears just as clearly in relation to the deepest internal division within Islam, that between Sunnism and Shiism. The two traditions share vast common ground in essential beliefs and practices; beyond that, they are drawn together in a particularly direct and existential way by a shared orientation toward sanctity (walaya) and gnosis (marifa) as the goal of the religious life. Seyyed Hossein Nasr writes: 'Islamic esotericism or gnosis crystallized into the form of Sufism in the Sunni world, while it poured into the whole structure of Shiism, especially during its early period.' The esoteric core of Islam thus expresses itself in Sunni Sufism and in Shiite 'Irfan with different accents, taking on different forms and expressions, yet without any change to this fundamental attunement to the essence of تَوْحِيد. Indeed, according to Sayyid Haydar Amuli — one of the great figures in whom Shiism and Sufism converge — the true Shii cannot but be a Sufi' if he plumbs the mystical depths of the Imams' teachings; and conversely, the Sufi is a Shii once he recognizes that the most important roots of Sufism lie in the Imams of the Ahl al-Bayt, the first poles (aqtab, sing. qutb) of the spiritual tradition, through whom the mystical blessings of the Prophet were transmitted in an initiatic setting. For Amuli the differences of outward form between the two traditions dissolve in the fire of walaya — sanctity, the true substance and goal of both alike. And for both, Ali (ع) is the wali Allah, the 'friend and saint of God', par excellence.

How Ali (ع) is regarded within the Sufi tradition is captured well by Ali Hujwiri, author of the first manual of Sufism written in Persian, the Kashf al-mahjub: 'Among them [the Sufis] is the son of Muhammad's uncle, he who drowned in the ocean of trying affliction, and was consumed in the fire of loving passion (ghariq-i bahr-i bala' wa hariq-i nar-i wala), the leader of the saints and the pure ones (muqtada-yi awliya wal-asfiya). In this Path he holds a place of tremendous honor and elevated degree. He had complete mastery over the precise expression of the principles of the supernal realities, such that Junayd said of him, "Ali is our master as regards principles and affliction (shaykhuna fil-usul wal-bala)." ... That is, Ali (ع) is the Imam of this Path in respect of both theoretical knowledge and practical application (andar 'ilm wa muamalat imam-i in tariqat), for the adepts of Sufism call the knowledge of this Path its "principles", while its practice is summed up in the endurance of affliction.'

The phrase 'endurance of affliction' recalls the Imam's description of the sages as those who 'befriend that by which the ignorant are estranged'. One reading of that elliptical phrase is this: among the things the sages befriend and the ignorant flee is prayer, and more precisely the act of pure concentration on the Absolute, which shuts out every other thought. Such concentration is a kind of death, for the ego and all its preoccupations are, as it were, 'sacrificed' before the divine Reality on which the mind is fixed, and fixed alone. The remembrance of God can be understood in two ways at once: as the fulfillment of that concentration, and as the path leading to the summit of spiritual realization — self-effacement before the sole reality of God.

The body of Ali (ع)'s teachings sheds remarkable light on the meaning, the principle and the practice of dhikruLlah, a theme of central importance in both the Quran and the Sunna of the Prophet. The Imam's sayings form a valuable commentary on those Quranic verses that speak of dhikr; and, since the dhikr stands at the center of Sufi practice and doctrine, they help explain why the Sufis regard the Imam as the first spiritual Pole (qutb) of Sufism after the Prophet himself. The close bond between remembrance and spiritual realization is illuminated both by the Imam's sayings and by the expositions of the Sufi tradition — above all those of the school of Ibn al-Arabi, known in the tradition as al-Shaykh al-akbar, the greatest Master', for his towering influence on later Sufi thought. These expositions may be read as doctrinal echoes of the Imam's dense, often elliptical pronouncements. Taken together, the Imam's sayings and the Sufis' elaborations bring key aspects of Islamic spirituality into sharp focus.

The term dhikr calls for consideration first, and with it the reason for its prominence in Islamic devotional life. Its meaning is extraordinarily rich, and no single English word can capture its full range. It carries the senses of remembrance, recollection, invocation, repetition, incantation, recitation; also of memory, mention, telling, the relating of a story, praise, glorification, celebration, fame, eminence, honor, exhortation and warning; and it can denote the Quran itself, or any book of revelation or religion. The definition that comes closest to the spiritual principle the word expresses is the one given by al-Raghib al-Isfahani in his Quranic dictionary. He writes that dhikr is that by means of which 'man preserves that knowledge (marifa) which he has attained, so it is akin to memory (hifz), except that memory is spoken of in respect of its function of acquisition (ihraz), whereas dhikr is spoken of in respect of its function of rendering present (istihdar). Dhikr is also used to refer to the presence of a thing in one's consciousness (lit. "heart") or in one's speech. Thus, it is said that there are two types of dhikr: dhikr of the heart and dhikr of the tongue, and each comprises two further kinds: a dhikr which follows forgetfulness, and a dhikr which does not follow forgetfulness but which, on the contrary, expresses a continuous remembering.'

Much in this definition chimes with the Imam's own perspective. The key point is this: when the thing remembered, or 'rendered present' to consciousness, is God, the importance of dhikruLlah as a principle becomes self-evident. DhikruLlah is to be understood first of all as a principle of awareness, a deep assimilation of the divine presence; but it is also an action — a practice, or a series of practices — and so a spiritual discipline. It can therefore be grasped both as the end and as the means to that end: both as spiritual awareness and as the methodical practice that cultivates that awareness. It is within the Sufi tradition that the relationship between the practice of the dhikr and the state of consciousness it aims at is worked out most fully. There the dhikr appears in two lights at once: as a contemplative discipline built on the methodical invocation or repetition of a Name of God, and as the cognitive outcome and mystical fruit of that same discipline.

The dhikr is thus the spiritual method par excellence of the Sufis, a discipline that helps make real, within the heart, what is already present to the mind — turning an abstract, notional grasp of certain ideas into a concrete perception of spiritual realities. Insofar as dhikruLlah is understood as the permanent awareness of the divine Reality, it may be presented as the very essence of religion itself, since it is what 'binds once again' (re-ligare) the human being to God, and so as the reason for which all religious rites and practices exist. If prayer is the core of religious practice, dhikruLlah is, as the Quran states plainly, akbar — 'greater' or 'greatest':

> ٱتْلُ مَآ أُوحِىَ إِلَيْكَ مِنَ ٱلْكِتَبِ وَأَقِمِ ٱلصَّلَوٰةَ ۖ إِنَّ ٱلصَّلَوٰةَ تَنْهَىٰ عَنِ ٱلْفَحْشَآءِ وَٱلْمُنكَرِ ۗ وَلَذِكْرُ ٱللَّهِ أَكْبَرُ ۗ وَٱللَّهُ يَعْلَمُ مَا تَصْنَعُونَ

'Truly, prayer keepeth [one] away from lewdness and iniquity, and the remembrance of God is greater' (Al-'Ankabut: 45).

Many sayings of the Prophet affirm the pre-eminence of the remembrance of God. It is related that the Prophet asked his companions: 'Shall I not tell you about the best and purest of your works for your Lord, and the most exalted of them in your ranks, and the work that is better for you than giving silver and gold, and better for you than encountering your enemy, with you striking their necks and them striking your necks?' Those he addressed replied: 'What is that, O emissary of God?' He said, 'The perpetual invocation of God, exalted and glorious (dhikruLlah azza wa jalla daiman).'

Ali (ع) affirms the same: 'Perpetuate the dhikr, for truly it illumines the heart, and it is the most excellent form of worship (huwa afdal al-ibada).' The dhikr receives strong emphasis in the Shii tradition, which records many sayings of the early Imams on the virtues of invoking the Names of God and various sacred phrases. Imam Jafar al-Sadiq described his followers in exactly these terms: 'Our Shia are those who, when alone (idha khalau), invoke and remember God much.' The khalwa — the spiritual retreat in which one seeks to be 'alone' with God — is described by 'Allama Tabatabai, one of the foremost masters of Shii gnosis in the twentieth century: As to the particular spiritual retreat (khalwat-i khass) it is distancing oneself from people ... it is especially required during certain types of verbal invocations (adhkar-i kalamiyya) ... and is considered essential by the masters of the path.'

Within the Sufi tradition, this statement on the dhikr by Shaykh Ahmad al-Alawi may be taken as wholly representative:

Remembrance is the mightiest rule of the religion ... The law was not enjoined upon us, neither were the rites of worship ordained but for the sake of establishing the remembrance of God ... In a word, our performance of the rites of worship is considered strong or weak according to the degree of our remembrance while performing them.

Al-Ghazali asks why the dhikr should be the most meritorious practice when it is so simple to perform, compared with all the other acts of worship that demand so much effort. His answer is that this superiority can be fully understood only through the 'science of unveiling' ('ilm al-mukashafa). What can be said from the standpoint of the practical religious sciences (ilm al-muamala) is that the most efficacious dhikr is that which is performed continuously with the presence of the heart (hudur al-qalb). This is the aim of the dhikr, and it is this which takes precedence over all acts of worship; nay, it is through this that the other acts of worship are dignified; this is the ultimate fruit of the practical acts of worship.' The principle of dhikruLlah thus reaches to the highest levels of religious practice, contemplative discipline and spiritual realization, and so to the very heart of Islamic spirituality. For all their variety across time and place, and all their divergent perspectives and emphases, the Sufis are unanimous on the centrality of dhikruLlah to their way. And for all their differences of name, orientation, school of thought or mode of practice, the Sufi orders are united in the pivotal figure of Ali (ع), the one who connects every later master of the spiritual path to the Prophet himself. This convergence of the Imam and the dhikruLlah is far from coincidental. The Imam's unfolding of the meaning of the dhikr, together with his actual initiation of disciples into its methodical practice, is a key aspect of his role as spiritual guide — the wali Allah, or the Pole (qutb) of his age — and is therefore central to his esoteric function within the spiritual tradition of Islam, in both its Sunni and Shii forms.

As regards this esoteric function, very little historically firm information survives about the Imam's initiatic role in this domain — that is, his actual initiation of disciples into the practice of invocation (known in the tradition as talqin) — just as little survives about his own initiation into the esoteric practice by the Prophet. This is hardly surprising, for the practice is, precisely, esoteric; it is not enjoined upon all as an obligatory rite. Rather, within both Sufism and Shii 'Irfan, the invocation is bestowed upon those with a particular vocation, those who have received a calling to practice something beyond the obligatory rites that bind everyone. Even so, several historical accounts offer prima facie evidence of the Imam's role in transmitting the dhikr. The individuals whom the earliest sources describe as devoted to the dhikr as a particular practice were known as close associates, if not disciples, of the Imam. His role in this initiatic domain is stressed in the orally transmitted sources of the Sufi way, and is set down in several written sources as well. These preserve reports of the transmission of the dhikr from the Prophet to the Imam, and from him to several other disciples who went on to initiate others into what later came to be called the way of tasawwuf.

Historically sound material on the earliest use and transmission of dhikruLlah may be scarce, but there remains the extremely important literary evidence of the sayings recorded in the Nahj al-balagha and the Ghurar al-hikam, as well as in the supplications attributed to the Imam. Taken together, these sayings form a homogeneous corpus, a distinct body of doctrine that offers a rich source of philosophical reflection, spiritual meditation and metaphysical insight into the nature and meaning of a practice that occupies so central a place in the mystical tradition of Islam.

These sayings shed light not only on the meaning of the practice of dhikr but also on the philosophy and epistemology that underlie it; and they give precious hints of its fruits, its consummation in spiritual realization. On one side, the Imam's sayings are an important source of commentary on the Quranic verses that bear on this theme; on the other, they clearly foreshadow the more elaborate doctrines of the dhikr found in the later Sufi and 'Irfani traditions. They thus serve as a bridge, joining what came before the Imam — prophetic revelation — with what came after him: the mystical elaboration. The texts and manuals of the mystical tradition seem to make explicit what was, more often than not, implicit in the Imam's sayings. The mystics drew out esoteric truths that the Imam had conveyed in sayings that were concise, often paradoxical and elliptical, but always intellectually demanding and spiritually fruitful. Reflection on these sayings — read in the context of the Quran and the Sunna they illuminate, and in the light of the later Sufi doctrine they anticipate — can bring one closer to a proper understanding of the role of dhikruLlah in the practical, operative and visionary dimensions of Islamic spirituality.

Reflection on those sayings finds its most fitting starting point in the Imam's comment on the dhikr in sermon 213 of the Nahj al-balagha. There the Imam first quotes words from verse 37 of Sura 24 of the Quran, entitled al-Nur ('The Light'), where the invocation of God's Name is mentioned, and then comments on them. The words are these: 'men whom neither trade nor merchandise diverts from the remembrance of God'. This fragment belongs to the preceding 'verse of light', the ayat al-nur, so rich in mystical symbolism, and this bond gives the Imam's comment a far deeper resonance. The Imam surely knew that reciting the words of verse 37 would call the earlier verses to his listeners' minds; by choosing not to quote the verse of light in full, he only sharpened the effect of what he left unsaid, for such hints, implications, evocations, and allusions are a mark of Arabic balagha. His words are therefore best read alongside the verse of light in full:

God is the light of the heavens and the earth. A similitude of His light is a niche wherein is a lamp; the lamp is enclosed in a glass; the glass is as it were a shining star. [The lamp] is lit [by the oil of] a blessed olive tree, neither of the East nor of the West. The oil well-nigh shineth forth, though fire touch it not. Light upon light! God guideth to His light whom He will; and God striketh similitudes for mankind; and God knoweth all things (An-Nur: 35).

After it come the following two verses:

In houses which God has allowed to be elevated, and that His Name be remembered therein, He is glorified in the mornings and in the evenings by men whom neither trade nor merchandise diverteth from the remembrance of God, and from establishing the prayer, and from the bestowing of alms ... (An-Nur: 36-7).

After reciting the words 'men whom neither trade nor merchandise diverteth from the remembrance of God', the Imam begins his sermon:

Truly, God has made the remembrance (al-dhikr) a polish for the hearts, by which they hear after being deaf, and see after being blind and yield after being resistant ... There have always been slaves of God ... with whom He held intimate discourse through their thoughts and spoke with them through the essence of their intellects. They diffused illumination through the awakened light in their hearing and their seeing and their hearts, calling unto the remembrance of the days of God ... Indeed, there is a special group (ahl) who belong to the dhikr; they have adopted it in place of the world, such that 'neither trade nor merchandise' distracts them from it. They spend the days of their life in it ... It is as though they had left this world for the Hereafter, and they are there, witnessing what is beyond this world ...

The weight of this passage lies in the Imam's description of the dhikr as a 'polish for hearts' (jila 'an lil-qulub), set beside his reference to the awakened light' in the hearing, sight, and hearts of those who have applied that polish. The link with the verse of light is clear. The heart's own hidden light is uncovered by the remembrance of God, and this inner light then shines together with the light from on high: 'light upon light'. Once the heart is lit, every other faculty shares in the radiance the invocation sets going, and so there is, once more, 'light upon light'. The word chosen for 'polish', jila, can itself be read as a quiet commentary on the light verse, for it comes from the verb jala, whose root meaning is 'to become clear' — exposed to view, disclosed, brought to light. The Quran speaks of 'the night when it enshroudeth in darkness, and the day when it shineth forth in brightness' (idha tajalla) (Al-Layl: 1-2), and again, 'By the day, as it bringeth to light [the sun] (idha jallaha) (Ash-Shams: 3). The consciousness that lies hidden within the heart is brought to light' through the polishing that the remembrance of God performs.

What is at stake is the clarifying of the organ of consciousness, the spiritual 'heart'. Polished, it can serve as a mirror that reflects truths from above, and it also opens its own hidden depths, disclosing truths from within — those buried contents of consciousness the Imam calls the 'buried treasures of the intellect' (dafain al-uqul). The dhikr is presented as a means of purifying this spiritual mode of perception, the 'heart', which is not to be identified with the reasoning faculty, and still less with the organs of the senses, as discussed in Chapter 1.

The Quran points to the deeper meaning of the heart and its own manner of 'vision': 'Have they not traveled in the land, that they might have hearts with which to comprehend, and ears with which to hear? Indeed, it is not the eyes that are blind, but blind are the hearts that are in the breasts' (Al-Hajj: 46). The eye's outward sight reaches only the surface of things, while the vision proper to the heart is an insight that passes beneath that surface to the reality hidden there. And again: 'They have hearts with which they understand not, eyes with which they see not, ears with which they hear not. They are like cattle, nay, even further astray. They are the heedless (al-ghafilun) (Al-A'raf: 179). The mention of the heedless', the ghafilun, at the close of verse 179 leads back to the dhikr, for the ghafil is the very opposite of the dhakir, the 'rememberer'; and the verse that immediately follows lays down one of the most important methodic foundations of the mystical practice of dhikr: 'Unto God belong the most beautiful Names, so call Him by them. And leave behind those who desecrate His Names; they will be requited for what they do' (Al-A'raf: 180). The bond between remembrance and the heart is stated plainly in these verses: ... Those who believe and whose hearts are at peace in the remembrance of God, is it not in the remembrance of God that hearts are at peace? (Ar-Ra'd: 28); 'Truly, therein is a reminder (dhikra) for him who hath a heart' (Qaf: 37); 'Those are true believers whose hearts quake with awe when God is invoked' (Al-Anfal: 2); 'Is he whose breast God hath opened up to submission (islam), such that he followeth a light from his Lord [like he who remains closed to submission and in darkness]? So woe be to those whose hearts are hardened against the remembrance of God, they are in plain error' (Az-Zumar: 22).

By contrast, the verse that follows speaks of hearts that 'soften' to dhikruLlah: 'God hath revealed the most beautiful saying, a Book [containing] similitudes and paired [threats and promises] at which the skins of those who fear their Lord quiver; then their skins and their hearts soften to the remembrance of God' (Az-Zumar: 23).

The Imam's comment on verse 37 of the 'Light' chapter should therefore be read as shedding light on all those Quranic verses that speak of the 'understanding', or spiritual vision, of which the heart is capable in principle, and that point to dhikruLlah as the means by which this possibility is made real. The heart needs to be 'polished' by the invocation of God. The Imam's comment echoes and strengthens a saying of the Prophet often quoted in Sufi circles: 'For everything there is a polish (siqala), and the polish of the hearts is the dhikruLlah.'

What is meant here is plainly an actual discipline — a practice, an act, or a regular series of acts — though this in no way rules out the sense of dhikr as awareness, or as a state of recollectedness in the divine presence, a cast of soul shaped by the knowledge that 'if you cannot see Him, He sees you'. The 'polishing' can be understood both as the work of purifying thought through reflection and meditation and as a regular methodic practice, whether it takes the form of glorification (tasbih), magnification (tamjid), praise (tahmid), sanctification (taqdis), the declaration of divine unity (tahlil), or the dhikr in the strict sense — the invocation of God's Names — by which the 'rust' of forgetfulness is rubbed away. Ali (ع) also speaks in his comment of a particular group who 'belong' to the dhikr, calling them an ahl. The Prophet likewise spoke of the ahl al-dhikr: 'God has angels who rove the pathways, seeking out the ahl al-dhikr, and when they find a group of people (qawm) invoking God, they call out to one another, "Come to that which you desire!"

Later, one of the greatest of the qawm — one of the names by which the Sufis call themselves — gave the image of the heart polished by the dhikr its poetic form. It is the custom of kings, Rumi tells in the Mathnawi, to seat the Sufis directly before them, for they are a mirror for the soul, and better than any mirror: breasts polished by invocation and meditation, so that the mirror of the heart receives the pure, virginal image.

Another side of the heart's polishing deserves attention: the nature of the 'vision' known by the heart that the dhikr has purified and faith has quickened. In the Imam's view this vision is divinely inspired; it is nothing less than the spiritual vision of God mentioned already: 'Eyes see Him not through sight's observation, but hearts see Him through the verities of faith.'

In another saying the Imam calls 'pure hearts' the 'places' from which God can be seen, 'so whoever purifies his heart sees God'. The bond between the heart's purification and the remembrance of God is confirmed in this saying: 'Where are they whose actions are accomplished purely for God, and who purify their hearts [so that they become] places for the remembrance of God?'

Taken together, these sayings of the Imam on how the dhikr acts upon the heart can be read as a commentary on the important hadith qudsi cited in Chapter 2: 'My slave never ceases to draw near to Me through supererogatory acts (nawafil) until I love him. And when I love him, I am his hearing by which he hears, his sight by which he sees, his hand by which he grasps, and his foot by which he walks.' The Sufis take this 'divine utterance' to set out a founding principle of their metaphysical doctrine and, at the same time, to point to the goal of their mystical practice.

A mutual confirmation stands between the Imam's saying and the hadith qudsi. Through the dhikr, the supererogatory act above all others, a person's perceptions and actions are mysteriously assimilated to the divine Reality, and the heart thereby attains spiritual vision. Once this happens, the heart, as the center of consciousness, can no longer be viewed in its merely relative, human dimension alone. Its inmost essence, or secret (sirr), belongs in truth to the divine, and this alone can see as God sees and, in the end, 'see' God Himself. As the Sufi master Abu Talib al-Makki wrote, 'None sees Him to whom nothing is similar but Him to whom nothing is similar.' If God does indeed enter into the human acts of perception that belong to the outward senses, then all the more is the supreme act of spiritual awareness, the vision of God, in reality not a vision of God by man but a vision of God by God, brought about through the individual's 'heart' rather than by it. The Imam says, 'Know God through God (irafuLlah biLlah).' In the last analysis, then, it is God who both 'sees' and is 'seen'. On a characteristic Sufi reading of the Quranic verse 'by a witnesser and that which is witnessed' (Al-Buruj: 3), it is God Himself who is both the 'witnesser' (shahid) and the 'witnessed' (mashhud). Al-Kashani, for instance, writes in his esoteric commentary — until quite recently wrongly attributed to Ibn al-Arabi — that the human witness is effaced in his witnessing, so that it is God who witnesses Himself through the individual whose separative consciousness is extinguished, such that 'He is the identity of the witness, there being no difference between the two except in terms of notional expression.'

This looks ahead to the final section, which explores the ultimate fruits of invocation as spiritual realization through self-effacement. The concern for the moment is a more relative side of the heart's vision of God: the way in which the reality of God is made clear and present to the heart through the 'verities of faith'. Here the one who perceives is the inner dimension of human consciousness, and what is 'perceived' is the divine Reality. In other words, deep faith uncovers the objective presence of God — a presence that is everywhere and cannot be taken away, and that forms the very being of all existing things. As the Imam's famous Dua Kumayl puts it, noted earlier: 'By Thy tremendousness (azmatika) which has filled all things ... by Thy Names which have filled the foundations of all things, by Thy knowledge which encompasses all things, by the light of Thy face, through which all things are illumined ...' (emphasis added).

Nothing can exist without the 'foundation' — the underlying support, as it were — that a Name of God, or a combination of Names, supplies for it. What makes up the true being and the positive qualities of each manifested thing is therefore, in the end, a divine Name or combination of Names. To see 'things' rightly is to see the divine attributes shown by and through them, and so to see them as reflections, within existence, of the higher realities that are their principle — as so many tajalliyyat, or theophanies, self-disclosures of the divine Reality. The 'heart' purified by invocation comes to see God everywhere.

This vision of the divine in all things rests on two principles affirmed in the Quran. The first concerns the divine 'object' — the objective, metaphysical nature of reality. The second concerns the human subject, and the mystical assimilation of that reality. On the one hand, nothing that exists, down to the most outward features of material existence, can be shut out from the divine Reality, for 'He is the First and the Last, and the Outward and the Inward' (Al-Hadid: 3), and 'Wherever ye turn there is the Face of God' (Al-Baqarah: 115). On the other hand, 'We shall show them Our signs on the horizons and in their own souls, until it be clear to them that He is the Real' (Fussilat: 53). Everything around us thus offers a sign that points back to its Creator.

The heart that the dhikr has polished and purified can read these signs and so 'see' God everywhere and in everything; yet the mere possibility of such a vision in no way brings the divine Reality down to the level of things perceived by the senses. As noted earlier, the first sermon of the Nahj al-balagha declares that 'God is with every thing, but not through association; and other than every thing, but not through separation.' This 'withness' (maiyya) of God implies no lowering of God, no association or joining (muqarana) of the divine Reality with created things, and no lessening of divine transcendence. The play of ideas here can be read as a commentary both on the Quranic verses that proclaim divine immanence — the presence of God to and within all things — and on those that stress divine transcendence, God's surpassing all things and being beyond compare with them. God may be 'absolutely' close to us, and yet we are infinitely far from Him. On the one hand, 'We are closer to man than the neck-artery' (Qaf: 16) — He is as near to us as our own jugular vein — and on the other, ... there is none comparable unto Him (Al-Ikhlas: 4).

Nothing that exists can stand altogether apart from the all-encompassing reality of God, and yet that reality shares no common measure with anything that exists. His oneness at once includes and excludes all things. To affirm His immanence within the world, His being 'with every thing', therefore takes nothing away from His transcendence; and to affirm His transcendence above the world, His being 'other than every thing', does not make Him absent from the world. The oneness of God carries all of this, and it rules out any merely numerical oneness, as the first chapter noted: in the Imam's understanding, God is One not as a countable 'one', a unit or entity among other units, but in the sense that His oneness admits no 'other'. In his words, 'That which has no second does not enter into the category of numbers.'

This may be read as an inner commentary on the Quranic verse 'Say: He, God is One' (Al-Ikhlas: 1). The opposite of this divine oneness is the multiplicity of gods implied in the error of shirk; the Imam's conception refines that error and turns it into a statement about being rather than about theology alone. Shirk, the act of associating 'partners' with God, is thereby raised to a higher register and shown to mean not simply the affirmation of false deities but the attribution to God of a merely numerical oneness. To reduce His oneness to number is to shut it inside the wholly relative class of particular things. Each such thing is a unit, and on this reckoning God would be no more than one among other similar 'beings'. But the Imam insists that God 'has no second': there is no other 'being' in being but Him. To call Him 'one' in the ordinary sense is itself imprecise, for it implies that a second could follow in sequence. He is better described as unique. Nothing that seems to exist can truly be set beside His being, which is one in an infinitely all-encompassing sense and unique in an absolutely exclusive sense — a oneness that includes all reality and excludes all otherness. This radical idea of a oneness that wholly excludes any 'otherness' in Being implies that created things are so many false gods, precisely to the extent that their apparent 'oneness' in existence is set against God's true oneness, or rather against God's 'one-and-onliness'.

In another sermon, Ali (ع) turns to divine immanence, and this time to the kind of awareness the human intellect can have of the divine Reality — which brings back the question of what vision of God is possible for a human being:

"Praise be to God who is present in the hidden inwardness of things, and whose being is indicated by the signs of manifest things. The eye of the onlooker cannot behold Him, so the eye of one who does not see Him cannot deny His reality, nor can the heart of one who affirms His reality see Him. He is utmost in elevation, so no thing is more elevated than He; but He is also close in His nearness, so no thing is closer than He ... God has not made the intellects capable of defining His qualities, but He has also not veiled the intellects from essential knowledge of Him."

The passage says two things at once: that human intellects cannot define, still less know, the divine attributes; and that an 'essential knowledge' of Him is nonetheless attainable. The apparent contradiction resolves in another central dimension of dhikr — that of recollection, remembrance, recognition. What takes place is a re-collecting of fragments of a knowledge that already exists but has been forgotten. One recognizes what one has always known, and it is exactly for this reason that one can know it once again. This does not mean the knowledge is made of parts to be reassembled, which would be empirical knowledge. The remembrance here is more like an awakening, an illumination that dawns as the clouds of inborn forgetfulness are dispelled.

This approach to knowledge through remembrance runs throughout the Imam's sayings. In Sermon 1 of the Nahj, for instance, comes the phrase, "Foremost in religion is knowledge of Him (awwal al-din marifatuhu)." The word awwal literally means "the first", and may be taken as the starting-point, the foundation, and so the foremost element of religion. The word marifa means not merely knowledge but also carries the sense of "recognition." The point is plain: the basis of all religious orientation is man's already-existing knowledge of the divine Reality. One re-cognizes what one has always known at the deepest level of consciousness; one ac-knowledges what one already knows but had seemingly forgotten.

Further on in the same sermon, God sent to mankind "His messengers, dispatching prophets in succession, in order to claim from them [His creatures] the fulfillment of the covenant of His creation; to remind them of His forgotten graces; to remonstrate with them through communication [of His revelation]; to unearth for them the buried treasures of the intellects ..." (dafain al-uqul).

The "buried treasures" may be identified with the knowledge of divine realities ingrained in man's deepest nature — the realities to which humanity was made to bear witness at the dawn of creation: "And when thy Lord brought forth from the children of Adam, from their loins, their seed, and made them testify of themselves, 'Am I not your Lord?' They said, 'Yea, we testify.' [This was] lest ye say on the Day of Resurrection, 'Truly, of this we were unaware'" (Al-A'raf: 172).

These divine realities may also be understood by reference to the divine spirit breathed into man at the creation of the human soul: "Then He fashioned [man] and breathed into him of His Spirit" (As-Sajdah: 9); to the "names" taught by God to Adam: "And He taught Adam the names, all of them ..." (Al-Baqarah: 31); and to the fitra, the primordial nature of man, patterned upon the divine nature: "... the nature of God (fitrat Allah), that according to which He created man" (Ar-Rum: 30). The primordial nature of the human soul thus holds within it the very thing, knowledge of God, to which all of humanity is made to bear witness in the pact (mithaq) named in the verse above. Imam Muhammad al-Baqir makes this identity between the fitra and the knowledge of God explicit, in a saying quoted by al-Khui as a commentary on that sentence of the first sermon. Al-Baqir was asked about the meaning of the Quranic words, "the nature of God, that according to which He created man." He answered, "He created them [such that their primordial nature was] in conformity with تَوْحِيد, at the covenant regarding knowledge of Him, [when they testified] that He is indeed their Lord."

Knowledge of God, then, defines human nature at its core; and for this reason the human intellect cannot be veiled, as the Imam said, from "essential knowledge of God." Yet although this knowledge is ingrained in human nature, forgetfulness (ghafla) has become man's second nature. In Quranic terms, the mere passage of time smothers the heart and so hardens it: "Hath not the moment come for the believers, that their hearts be humbled to the remembrance of God, and to what hath been revealed of the truth; and that they not become as those who were given the Book aforetime. Then the term was prolonged for them, so their hearts were hardened; and many of them are evil-doers" (Al-Hadid: 16).

The verse speaks both of this hardening of the heart over time — which may be identified with the ghafla of second nature — and of its opposite: the heart's being present, here and now, to the dhikruLlah. To cure forgetfulness, more is needed than a plain factual reminder, some item of information, some piece of formal, conceptual, reasoned knowledge. No amount of empirical data, arriving wholly from without, can bring man to knowledge of God. Rather it is integral remembrance — a spiritual awareness that wells up from within and takes shape on contact with revelation from without — that makes the innate knowledge of God present once more to heart and mind, the knowledge that lies "buried" deep in the intellect. Between this consciousness of God and ordinary empirical knowledge of phenomena there is no common measure. The Imam expresses this lack of any common measure in sayings such as, "Praise be to God who is well-known (maruf) without being seen." A further wording of the same principle sharpens the paradox: "By things their Creator manifests Himself to the intellects; and by things He is guarded from the sight of the eyes." This opens onto a complex theme in the Imam's thought — the nature of the "veil" formed by created phenomena, and how it is to be understood in the light of the infinite and inalienable reality of God. On one hand, all created phenomena are a veil, in that they keep the divine Essence from being seen by outward vision; on the other, no phenomenon can help manifesting the divine, bearing witness to its creativity and revealing something of its reality — plain to the intellect in its normal state, and dazzlingly evident to the heart illumined and purified by the remembrance of God.

The aphorisms of Ibn 'Ataillah, one of the great masters of Sufi gnosis or marifa (معرفۃ), are wholly to the point here. They read as a series of reflections on the paradox the Imam states so briefly. Ibn Ataillah writes: "That which shows you the existence of His omnipotence is that He veiled you from Himself by that which has no existence alongside of Him." In one respect God is "guarded from the eyes," in the Imam's words, and so veiled by "things"; but insofar as God alone truly "is," these "things" do not really exist, so God is veiled by "nothing." Through their very "appearance" — both their coming into existence and their "apparent" existence — it is "by things" that "their Creator manifests Himself." The mystery of this self-manifestation of God by and through all things is pressed home with full force by the following rhetorical questions of Ibn Ataillah:

How can it be conceived that something veils Him, since He is the one who manifests everything?

How can it be conceived that something veils Him, since were it not for Him the existence of everything would not be manifest?

It is a marvel how Being has been manifested in non-being, and how the contingent has been established alongside of Him who possesses the attribute of Eternity!

The sense of all these sayings can be grasped within a paradox the Imam states tersely: "The veils do not veil Him (la tahjubuhu al-hujub)." The veils cannot help revealing what they are meant to conceal; they are not merely transparent to the reality they cannot hide — in and of themselves they reveal it rather than conceal it. This recalls the Imam's famous saying, so often quoted by the Sufis: "Were the veil to be removed, I would not increase in certitude" (لو کشف الغطاء ما ازدت یقینا). The veils of creation reveal in the end what they at first conceal, yet only the eye of the heart, purified through the dhikr, perceives this revelation — the heart that now "sees after being blind." The Imam continues, deepening the paradox further:

"The veil between Him and His creatures is His very creation of them. [This veil is] for the sake of His prevention of that which is possible in their essences, and for the sake of making possible that which is prevented in regard to Him; and to distinguish the Creator from the created, and the limit from the limited, and the Lord from the vassal."

All of creation, every "veil" it forms, is thus nothing other than the divine dimension of al-Zahir: the Apparent, the Clear, the Evident, the Manifest. Yet creation is al-Zahir not in its own right, but by virtue of the divine reality of which it is a projection, a form of tajalli. God, as al-Zahir, projects something of His "hidden treasure" within the creation and as the creation. The world manifests the "apparent" aspect of God — that which "appears" from Him, that which He can manifest or bring into being — while still concealing the mystery of God: that which stays "hidden," al-Batin, the supra-manifest source of being, the uncreated essence. The veil of creation is therefore nothing other than the self-revelation of God. It must be stressed, though, that it is so only by virtue of what pierces the veil of its own apparent otherness — that divine creativity which the creation cannot in fact conceal, even though in principle it has the character of a veil.

The veil formed by creation prevents what the essences of creatures make possible. What the essences make possible is the realization of inmost identity with the divine Reality — تَوْحِيد at its highest level — a possibility that follows from the fact that this same divine Reality is the very ground of being of "their essences." It is the divine Names, qualities, and attributes that "fill the foundations of all things." At the foundations, or within the "essences," there is but the one and only divine Reality. If the realization of this truth of identity is thus "possible," what the veil "prevents" is the affirmation of that identity at the wrong level of being — that of empirical existence — rather than at the deepest level, where the "essences" of things are nothing other than the divine qualities, and thus the divine Reality itself. To equate created phenomena with God amounts to pure shirk, the cardinal sin of "association," and it is against this that a vision of divine oneness must guard. One must always "distinguish the Creator from the created, and the limit from the limited, and the Lord from the vassal."

The veil of creation, in other words, acts as a barrier against pantheism — the reduction of the Creator's infinite transcendence to the finite forms of creatures. Yet insofar as the veil has no existence apart from the self-manifestation of the Real, it also, paradoxically, makes possible "that which is prevented in regard to Him": made transparent to the divine Reality of which it is one expression, the veil cannot keep the immanent reality of God from shining through all things and within them. On one side stands essential identity, with no diminution of divine transcendence; on the other stands existential distinction, with no weakening of divine immanence. This returns to the founding sentence of the first sermon of the Nahj: "He is with every thing, but not through association; and other than every thing, but not through separation." It recalls too a central theme in Sufi metaphysics: the living relationship between tanzih, the declaration of divine incomparability and transcendence, and tashbih, the affirmation of similarity and, at the deepest level, of immanence. In the words of Ibn al-Arabi:

"He is not declared incomparable in any manner that will remove Him from similarity, nor is He declared similar in any manner that would remove Him from incomparability. So do not declare Him nondelimited and thus delimited by being distinguished from delimitation! For if He is distinguished then He is delimited by His nondelimitation. And if He is delimited by His nondelimitation, then He is not He."

To return to the Imam's powerfully evocative statement, "The veils do not veil Him." Both tanzih and tashbih can become veils unless each is seen in the light of the other; both transcendence and immanence can become veils unless the two perspectives are held together in spiritual vision; and both the creation, as divine manifestation, and the uncreated Essence, above and beyond all manifestation, can become veils unless they are integrated in the spiritual vision proper to تَوْحِيد, and so made transparent, each "revealing" the other. In the same way, a one-sided stress on God as "the Apparent" becomes a veil by blinding one to God as "the Hidden," and the reverse holds too: for even the hidden aspect of God, taken in isolation, will — despite its non-manifest nature — form a conceptual veil that prevents the seeing of God's "evidence," His "evident-ness," in the very heart of the created world. The "veils" are thus not only created phenomena but also the limits inherent in every human conception of God, including the divine qualities and attributes. Hence the need for a radical negation, on the plane of human conception, of those divine attributes. Only through such negation can consciousness begin to fathom the true nature of divine transcendence and see through the veils that "do not veil Him."

These perspectives on the nature of the veil are better understood as the evocation of a spiritual vision than as the logical statement of empirical observation. They are the fruits of contemplative synthesis, not the conclusions of rational analysis. Such a vision resolves apparent contradiction through an intuitive awareness of the unitive simplicity of reality; it is not the product of some tortuous mental effort to reconcile the complexities of existence. It is at once a vision reflected by the heart burnished by the remembrance of God and itself a form of that very remembrance. What the rational faculty will always perceive as irreducible contradiction, the spirit of the intellect can grasp as a fruitful paradox.

This aspect of the Imam's teaching finds vivid expression in the words of the famous Sufi Abu Said al-Kharraz, who, asked how he came to know God, replied: 'through the fact that He brings opposites together (jamuhu al-diddayn).' He then recited the Quranic verse, 'He is the First and the Last, and the Outward and the Inward.'

It is spiritual intuition that gives the capacity to see as one, in the unifying vision of تَوْحِيد, what appears on the surface as many. In sharpening the paradox on the conceptual plane, Ali (ع) is, in modern parlance, 'deconstructing' the artifices of mental conception, and in doing so he prepares the way for a receptivity to intuitive apprehension and spiritual contemplation. The very work of the intellect, عقل — a word whose root means to bind or restrain — is one of binding together. The realities the heart discloses through intuition and inspiration are drawn by it into the organic unity of تَوْحِيد through an exercise in logic, a movement of negation and affirmation, of constriction and expansion.

It is only at these deeper levels of consciousness that the answering work of reconstruction can take place: a re-cognition of the Real, al-Haqq, arising from the heart's vision of God through 'the verities of faith' (haqaiq al-iman). Only these haqaiq are truly of the same measure as al-Haqq. Only these realities, hidden within the depths of faith and brought to light in a heart polished by the remembrance of God, can do justice to the reality of God.

<!-- editorial:begin -->
> **Editorial note (tradition-grounded).** 
> When this chapter names the Imam as the first "Pole" (qutb) of the spiritual tradition, the
> image is more exact than it may first appear: the root of the word names the fixed pin at the
> center of a millstone around which the whole stone turns, so a qutb is the still axis on whom an
> entire order depends. It is worth setting beside the chapter's own subject. If remembrance
> polishes the heart, its opposite in the wider tradition is ghafla — a term whose root describes
> an untended emptiness, land with no landmark, a mind with no attention — the heedless
> forgetfulness of God that wakeful dhikr is meant to dispel. Read together, the two terms mark
> the poles of the inner life this chapter describes: the axis one turns upon, and the inattention
> that turning is meant to overcome.
<!-- editorial:end -->

## 12. Remembrance as the Quintessence of Worship

The practice of the remembrance of God, dhikruLlah, is best approached first through those Quranic verses that name it as a devotional discipline:

And invoke the Name of thy Lord morning and evening (Al-Insan: 25).

And invoke the Name of thy Lord, devoting thyself to Him [or: it] with utter devotion (Al-Muzzammil: 8).

O ye who believe! Invoke God with much invocation (Al-Ahzab: 41).

Call upon thy Lord in humility and in secret (Al-A'raf: 55).

And invoke thy Lord within thyself, in humility and awe, and beneath thy breath, in the morning and in the night (Al-A'raf: 205).

All of these verses point to an actual practice, one to be sustained in various modes: 'within thyself', 'in humility', 'in awe', 'in secret', 'beneath thy breath', and so on. Above all, the invocation is to be performed abundantly, dhikran kathiran. The intensity and length of the devotions of the Prophet and the first Muslims are recalled in the Quranic chapter al-Muzzammil ('The Enwrapped'), which speaks of the long night vigils these early Muslims kept: 'Truly, thy Lord knoweth that thou standest in prayer for almost two-thirds of the night, and a half of it, and a third of it, as doth a group from those with thee' (Al-Muzzammil: 20). The opening of this Sura contains the verse instructing the Prophet to 'remember/invoke the Name of thy Lord, and devote thyself to Him with utter devotion' (Al-Muzzammil: 8). The pronoun in the word ilayhi, 'to Him', can refer either to Him as 'thy Lord' or to 'it' as the 'Name of thy Lord'. The operative sense of the verse thus becomes: 'Invoke the Name of God and devote yourself to this Name with utter devotion.' This echoes the explicit injunction, 'Glorify the Name of thy Lord, the Exalted' (Al-A'la: 1). On the plane of devotional practice, then, devotion to the Name of the Absolute amounts to devotion to the Absolute itself. The Absolute in itself lies above and beyond all forms, including every verbal form; yet it is made accessible through the divine Name. That Name is not defined by any human linguistic invention but bestowed through divine self-disclosure, God revealing the sacrament of His own Name. To be distracted from the remembrance or invocation of the Name is, equally, to be distracted from God Himself, as two verses make clear: 'The devil hath overcome them, causing them to forget the remembrance of God' (Al-Mujadila: 19), and 'O ye who believe, let not your wealth nor your children distract you from the remembrance of God' (Al-Munafiqun: 9).

This identification between devoting oneself to God and devoting oneself to His Name flows from a principle stated by Ali (ع) in a simple affirmation: 'He who loves a thing dedicates himself fervently to its invocation (man ahabba shayan lahija bi-dhikrihi).' The lover of God should therefore give himself utterly to the invocation of the Name of God, and give himself to it in abundance, kathiran. The word lahija means both 'to be devoted to' and 'to mention repeatedly'; its pairing with dhikr, which denotes the constant verbal repetition of the invocation, appears also in the Dua Kumayl, there with an added explicit reference to the tongue: ... my tongue has devoted itself to thy invocation (lahija bihu lisani min dhikrika). He implores God, 'Make my times in the night and the day inhabited by Thy remembrance', and 'make my tongue invoke Thee with utter dedication (ijal lisani bi-dhikrika lahijan). The invocation as a practice is confirmed in his words: O He whose Name is a remedy and whose invocation is the cure (ya man ismuhu dawa' wa dhikruhu shafa).

Here the Name of God is far more than a name among other names, a word or sound among other symbols: it is the remedy for man's inborn forgetfulness, the spiritual amnesia, ghafla, that has become his 'second nature'. But to be effective the Name must be actively invoked, not merely contemplated, just as a medicine must be taken rather than merely thought about if it is to exercise its healing power. As the Imam puts it in one of his sermons, describing the greatness of the Quran: 'It has made the dhikr incumbent upon your tongues.' And the invocation is to be performed kathiran, much, in abundance. The following hadith of the Prophet makes clear that dhikr has an operative, practical dimension and cannot be reduced to a purely theoretical reading of its meanings:

'Abd Allah b. Busr reported that a man said, O Messenger of Allah, the laws of Islam are too many for me, so tell me something that I may cling to.' He replied, may God bless him and give him peace, 'Let your tongue never cease to be moist from invoking Allah.'

The verses following that Quranic exhortation to invoke much continue: 'O ye who believe, invoke God with much invocation, and glorify Him early and late. He it is who blesseth you, as do His angels, so that He may bring you forth from darkness into light; and He is ever merciful to the believers' (Al-Ahzab: 41-43).

A reciprocity holds here between the dhikr of the believers and the salat — the prayer or blessing of God — upon the believers. The relationship between the human act of invocation and the divine act of blessing is more complex than it first appears. Exploring this relationship, and the ontological dimensions of the invocation, brings one close to the mystery expressed by the verse, 'Remember Me, I will remember you' (Al-Baqarah: 152).

Within these same verses (Al-Ahzab: 41-43) stands a further relationship: that between invocation and illumination, the being brought from darkness into light. The Imam speaks of the way human invocation issues in divine illumination, and his words may be read as a commentary on this dimension of those verses: 'Whoever invokes God, glorified be He, God enlivens his heart and illuminates his inner substance (lubb).' The word lubb (pl. albab) here evokes the Quranic verse describing the ulul-albab, the 'possessors of substance', in which the dhikr again figures prominently: 'Truly in the creation of the heavens and the earth, and in the alternation between night and day, there are signs for those of substance, those who invoke God standing, sitting and reclining on their sides, and reflect upon the creation of the heavens and the earth ...' (Ali 'Imran: 190-191).

Beyond reinforcing the practical and methodic dimension of remembrance, this passage indicates the relationship between dhikr and fikr, between invocation and reflection or meditation. The practice of invocation is understood to polish the inmost organ of awareness, and so to deepen the capacity for meditation upon the 'signs of God' in creation and within oneself. When the element of love is set between invocation and meditation — for 'he who loves a thing dedicates himself fervently to its invocation' — these three elements enter a virtuous circle, each strengthening and deepening the other two. The more seriously one ponders the 'signs' of God and appreciates their beauty, the more one comes to love God; the more one loves God, the more one invokes His Name; the more one invokes, the more keenly one perceives the sacred message of those 'signs' in the universe. Thus fikr intensifies dhikr, and dhikr deepens fikr.

These verses (Ali 'Imran: 190-191) also highlight the supra-formal dimension of invocation. The dhikr is presented here as the quintessence of all religious activity, the spiritual act par excellence. Its modes are universal: standing, sitting, reclining, as the verse states, and 'within thyself', 'in humility', 'in awe', 'in secret', 'beneath thy breath', as the verses cited above make clear. These modes transcend the formal rules governing the fixed canonical prayers, which prescribe specific words, movements, and conditions.
