The dhikr, by contrast, is to be performed at all times, in all places, and in all postures; it is to be woven into the texture of everyday life rather than imposed upon life as an external, formalistic practice. According to Imam Jafar al-Sadiq, invocation is the one practice that should be carried out in all circumstances and at all times, with no limit placed upon it: 'Everything has a limit, at which it terminates, except the dhikr Allah. God has made obligatory the religious duties (al-faraid), so whoever fulfills them, that fulfillment constitutes their limit. For the month of Ramadan, whoever fasts therein, that constitutes its limit ...' He goes on to mention other rites, then cites the Quranic verse: 'O ye who believe, invoke God with much invocation', and adds that his father, Imam Muhammad al-Baqir, never ceased invoking, even while eating and walking.

In this light one can more easily understand those whom the Quran describes as 'men whom neither trade nor merchandise diverts from the remembrance of God'. The verse also illuminates why believers are described as those who are 'perpetually at prayer (ala salatihim daimun) (Al-Ma'arij: 23). Such people are not merely regular in performing the canonical prayer at its appointed times; for the men of substance, there is no moment when they are not at prayer', if one understands 'prayer' as the remembrance of God. The Quran instructs mankind to 'establish the prayer for the sake of My remembrance' (Ta-Ha: 14). The very purpose and goal of prayer — its spiritual value and substance — is thus the remembrance of God.

A saying of Ali (ع) bears directly on this: 'Continuous invocation is the food of the spirit and the key to prayer (miftah al-salat).' This sheds further light on why the Quran says that 'prayer keepeth [one] away from lewdness and iniquity, and the remembrance of God is greater' (Al-'Ankabut: 45). The formal, canonical prayer is described here in negative or constraining terms: it is a preventative. Its formality is a ritual necessity, but also, unavoidably, an existential limitation — performed and operative at one time and not another, expressed in certain forms and not others. The remembrance of God is greater in that it is the positive, enlivening substance of prayer; it is what liberates consciousness from the verbal, mental, and existential limits by which all formal prayer is defined. Since formal prayer is inescapably bounded by such limits, its baraka, its blessedness, can all too easily become confined, subjectively, within those same limits, so that one feels close to God only during formal prayer and not in everyday life outside the prayer-times. It is dhikr — in the sense of recollectedness and constant awareness of God — that allows prayer, or the consciousness shaped by prayer, to open outward into its intended essence: an awareness of the unconditional presence of God. Dhikr thus transforms a preventative measure against moral transgression into a positive apprehension of spiritual reality. The dhikruLlah may be called 'greater' than the ritual or canonical prayer in the measure that spiritual awareness transcends moral conscience, or that essence transcends form. The 'lesser' is not negated by the 'greater' but comprised within it, enriched by it, and brought to a fullness and scope it could never attain on its own. In this light one can better understand at least some of the reasons why the Imam, following the Prophet and the Quran, presents dhikruLlah as the quintessence of worship and devotion.

There remain the spiritual fruits of the remembrance of God, and in particular 'realization', tahqiq: the process by which al-Haqq, the Real, is 'made real' within oneself. This is closely related to the process the Imam earlier described as tawahhud, self-integration — the spiritual assimilation of oneness within oneself, as distinct from the simple theological affirmation of oneness, تَوْحِيد, in its conventional sense. In keeping with the Imam's characteristic use of paradox, the fruit of remembrance can be approached first through its very opposite: ghafla, heedlessness, or sahw, forgetfulness and distraction. The saying that follows is another striking example of how the Imam's words function implicitly as a commentary on Quranic verses, while opening onto a rich terrain of mystical speculation and spiritual contemplation concerning the ultimate consequences of the invocation and remembrance of God. The Quran is severe in its condemnation of those who pray ostentatiously and hypocritically, those who 'give the lie to religion': 'Woe to those who pray, those who are heedless of their prayer, those who make a show, and who withhold simple assistance' (Al-Ma'un: 4-7). The Imam likewise warns against remembering God heedlessly or distractedly, and in describing its opposite — perfect or complete dhikr — he articulates a paradoxical return to a kind of 'forgetting' that goes to the very heart of the Sufi principle of 'extinction in remembrance' (al-fana fil-dhikr):

Do not remember God absent-mindedly (sahiyan), nor forget Him in distraction; rather, remember Him with perfect remembrance (dhikran kamilan), a remembrance in which your heart and tongue are in harmony, and what you conceal conforms with what you reveal. But you will not remember Him according to the true reality of the remembrance (haqiqat al-dhikr) until you forget your own soul in your remembrance.

This saying offers a summary of the entire mystical path. Implied in it is the whole sequence of spiritual 'stations' (maqamat, sing. maqam) through which total sincerity is attained. Sufism itself is often described simply as 'sincerity', that is, sidq or ikhlas. To be totally sincere in this sense is to orient oneself and one's desires purely toward God, without any taint of impurity — impurity meaning the presence, in one's devotion, action, or intention, of any desire for something other than God. On one hand, the dhikr itself helps to realize sincerity by 'polishing' the heart; on the other, it is sincerity that confers 'perfection' or 'completion' upon the spiritual efficacy of the dhikr. What matters at this stage of 'perfect' dhikr is that the outward verbal form of the invocation must harmonize with heartfelt intention. There must be no contradiction between what the tongue articulates and what motivates the heart. To bring heart and tongue into harmony is to let the divine Reality invoked by the tongue reverberate in the heart, the very source of the soul's fundamental dispositions, its virtues.

The divine qualities contained within the Name of the Absolute must, to some degree at least, be reflected in the heart and soul of the invoker, in the form of virtues — virtues summed up in the sincerity or 'purity' of soul to which ikhlas refers. When the soul is completely pure, sincerely and utterly at one with the highest ideals proclaimed by the tongue and the intellect, it becomes transparent to the spiritual qualities embedded within its own substance: the fitra, or primordial nature. These are the qualities 'brought to light', and brought to life, by the dhikr; they are the qualities that shine forth from the heart once it has been polished by the dhikr. In their absence, the purely technical act of invocation is 'heedless' and devoid of all efficacy. One who makes no effort to attain sincerity, who does not engage with the 'greater' jihad, is heedless of God, neglecting the moral concomitants of authentic remembrance and ignoring the moral preconditions for its efficacy.

Yet what is most remarkable in this saying of the Imam is his presentation of the ultimate stage of dhikr in terms of its apparent opposite. The 'reality' of dhikr, its haqiqa, opens outward into the supreme haqiqa, divine Reality as such. This degree of dhikr requires the 'forgetting' of the individual: the veil of the self must be cast aside if the spiritual vision of divine Reality is to be attained. There can be no marifa without self-effacement at the outset, and 'extinction' (fana) in the end. The reality of 'remembering' calls for the most radical 'forgetting'.

There is, then, a clear distinction — indeed a radical break — between 'perfect' dhikr and the 'reality' of dhikr, such that to move from 'perfection' to 'reality', from human perfection to divine Reality, consciousness must make a leap in its very being. At the first level, the aim is to eliminate hypocrisy and to attain sincerity, integrity, and wholeness — in short, perfection on the plane of the human soul. Here the individual, as the agent of the dhikr, keeps his identity. The harmonious reverberation between hidden intent and outward act is, at this stage, what is called 'perfect' dhikr. But it remains a dhikr that belongs to the individual as its agent. At the higher level, by contrast, the dhikr can no longer be defined by reference to the individual, for the individual has been 'forgotten' — forgotten by himself. This can only mean that the center of consciousness has shifted from the invoker, al-dhakir, to the Invoked, al-Madhkur. The haqiqa of the dhikr is thus one with the object of invocation, the Real itself, al-Haqq. Inwardly, then, the true agent of the invocation is God Himself. Man's apparent invocation is no more than an outward appearance, an illusion that the very act of invocation extinguishes.

The question of agency returns here. A hadith qudsi bears upon it: "My slave never ceases to draw near to Me through supererogatory acts (nawafil) until I love him." The Imam's commentary on the Quranic verses about those whom the world does not distract from the remembrance of God made clear that the nawafil in question center upon the practice of invocation. Invocation, then, can be understood to disclose the reality of God's agency, in the very order of being, within all acts: God is the hearing and the seeing of the individual whom He loves as a result of his devotions. This account of the Divine penetrating the faculties of soul and body describes a universal reality, the true state of affairs — not any change of state, whether in God or in the individual. God does not "change" in order to enter into the acts and perceptions of one person rather than another.

His "becoming" one with the acts of perception must be understood as a reality "becoming clear" to the soul that has been unveiled, so that it sees through the illusory appearance of its own agency. By virtue of the invocation, the soul is enabled to see the deeper meaning of two Quranic verses:

> وَٱللَّهُ خَلَقَكُمْ وَمَا تَعْمَلُونَ

"God hath created you and what ye do" (As-Saffat: 96), and:

> وَمَا رَمَيْتَ إِذْ رَمَيْتَ وَلَكِنَّ ٱللَّهَ رَمَىٰ

"thou didst not throw when thou threwest [the stones], but God threw" (Al-Anfal: 17).

The kind of "vision" that the dhikr makes possible is lit up by the very title of one of Ibn al-Arabi's treatises, Kitab al-fana fil-mushahada (The Book of Extinction in Contemplation). The title seems to point directly to the Imam's paradox of forgetting at the very heart of remembrance, for to be extinguished in contemplation is akin to being forgotten in invocation. At the opening of this treatise, Ibn al-Arabi writes that the divine Reality is too exalted to be witnessed by the creaturely eye. The vision of God is impossible as long as any "trace of the condition of the creature in the eye of the witness" remains. Only near the end of the treatise is this seemingly insurmountable barrier removed. Ibn al-Arabi resolves the problem by means of the Prophet's definition of ihsan: "It is to worship God as though you see Him, and if you see Him not, yet truly He sees you." The Arabic wording is such that, by placing a stop in the middle of the phrase "if you see Him not (in lam takun: tarahu)," the meaning is wholly transformed into: "if you are not, you see Him."

This vision of God cannot, then, belong to the individual — "forgotten" in the Imam's words, or non-existent in Ibn al-Arabi's. The vision of God can only be by God, not by man. The metaphysical basis of this "ontological transference," in relation to the divine Names, is treated further on. The message of "extinction in contemplation" is reinforced by a dialogue that Ibn al-Arabi narrates between himself and Moses in the sixth heaven, set within his account of his own visionary ascent through the heavens to the divine presence within his own soul.

He addresses Moses: "You requested the vision (of God), while the Messenger of God (Muhammad) said that 'not one of you will see his Lord until he dies.'"

Moses replies: "And it was just like that: when I asked Him for the vision, He answered me, so that I 'fell down stunned' (Al-A'raf: 143). Then I saw Him in my (state of) being stunned."

Ibn al-Arabi asks: "While (you were) dead?"

Moses: "While (I was) dead. I did not see God until I had died."

Here is a supra-personal vision entirely in accord with Ali (ع)'s unselfconscious remembrance. What is implicit in both forms of spiritual assimilation is the "ontological transference" of agency. The true vision of God cannot take place as long as anything creaturely remains in the "eye" of the beholder. The spiritual principle of this dialogue — and even some of its language — is rendered in condensed form in a supplication attributed to the Imam. He pleads with God to make him "one of those whom You have called and he has replied to You, upon whom You have glanced and who falls unconscious before Your majesty." The word saiqa ("to fall unconscious," "be stunned") used in this supplication is the same as the one used in the Quranic verse (Al-A'raf: 143) for Moses' state when God revealed Himself to the mountain. A single "glance" from the Real is enough to bring about this undoing of self-awareness — the shadow cast, upon the plane of individual consciousness, by the blazing light of God's self-disclosure, His tajalli.

This divine "glance" (lahiza) brings the discussion back to Ibn al-Arabi's evocative phrase, "extinction in contemplation." In his Tarjuman al-ashwaq he speaks of divine wisdom as a peacock that "kills with her glances," and adds in his own commentary that this refers to "the station" of al-fana fil-mushahada (extinction in contemplation). Earlier in the same commentary he speaks of the "murderous glances" of this divine wisdom "which accrues to a man in his hours of solitude, and which assaults him with such violence that he is unable to behold his personality, and which exercises dominion over him." His commentary on the following lines is also instructive in underscoring the relationship between dhikr and fana: "Wild is she, none can make her his friend; she has gotten in her solitary chamber (bayt khalwatiha), a mausoleum for remembrance (dhikr)." The commentary adds: "because contemplation of the Essence is a passing away (fana). Her solitude is her looking on herself, for God says, 'Neither My earth nor My heaven contains Me, but I am contained by the heart of My believing servant,' and since the heart which contains this essential wisdom of the race of Jesus is bare and empty of all attributes, it is like a desert and she is like a wild animal."

Realization through remembrance, then, strictly entails the extinction of individual consciousness, the perishing of egoistic self-awareness before the Absolute. This principle is far from merely speculative. Quite apart from the wealth of Sufi literature on the states of consciousness (ahwal, sing. hal) through which the mystic passes, there is the evidence of the Imam's own immersion in such states. Abul-Darda, an eminent companion of the Prophet, reported that he once found Ali (ع) lying in one of the palm orchards of Medina, his body seemingly lifeless, as stiff as wood. He went to offer his condolences to Ali (ع)'s family, but his wife Fatima told him, "My cousin has not died. Rather, in fear of God he has fainted. This condition overcomes him often." The "fear" in question is to be understood as "awe." It belongs to the heavenly aspect of khawf — heavenly because Paradise is said to follow from realizing this kind of fear to perfection, as the Quran indicates: "But whoso feareth (khafa) the station of his Lord and restraineth his soul from caprice, verily, the Garden will be his abode" (An-Nazi'at: 40-41). "Fear of God," in the spiritual rather than the moralistic sense, does not so much terrify as beatify. It produces not merely a quaking awe before the transcendent Absolute, but also an intimate absorption in the immanent divine presence. As is often quoted in Sufism: when one fears creatures one flees from them, but when one fears God one flees to Him:

> لَّا مَلْجَأَ مِنَ ٱللَّهِ إِلَّآ إِلَيْهِ

"There is no refuge from God except [fleeing] to Him" (At-Tawbah: 118).

This report also shows that the following saying of the Imam is not mere extravagant rhetoric. It seems, rather, to point to a genuine state of spiritual intoxication, a total re-absorption of consciousness into its divine source — a description of the conditions leading up to the state that "overcomes him often," as Fatima put it:

Truly, God has a drink for His friends (awliyaihi). When they drink it, they are intoxicated (sakara); and when they are intoxicated, they are enraptured ('araba); and when they are enraptured, they are blessed (taba); and when they are blessed, they dissolve (dhaba); and when they dissolve, they are free (khalasa); and when they are free, they devote themselves purely (akhlasa); and when they devote themselves purely, they seek (talaba); and when they seek, they find (wajada); and when they find, they arrive (wasala); and when they arrive, they are at one (ittasala); there is no difference between them and their Beloved.

This saying of the Imam echoes one of the Prophet's, who said that he had states, or "moments" (waqt), that no "angel brought close" nor "prophet sent forth" could encompass — the implication being that neither angel nor prophet can subsist as such in the ineffable "moments" of absolute تَوْحِيد. This is not "union"; it is, rather, the realization of oneness. Union implies two entities becoming one, whereas God is, as the Imam put it, "that which has no second." Even to call Him "one" falls short, for that word implies that a second might stand in sequence to Him. He is more truly named unique — a reality that admits no otherness. This ultimate realization of تَوْحِيد can be seen as the fruit of the invocation, the most potent of all intoxicating "drinks," if prayer may be called a drink. It leads to a careful consideration of the true agent of the invocation, the true "drinker." What appears to be man's invocation of God is transformed into the divine invocation of man. At the outset it seems that man "drinks" God through His Name; but the ultimate result of the invocation, unitive realization, reveals that it is in fact God who "drinks into Himself" the essence of human consciousness — an essence from which the impurity of self-consciousness has been drawn out. The true nature of the dhikr, masked by the outward form of man's invocation of God, is alluded to in another saying of the Imam: "The invocation is not a formality of speech nor a way of thinking; rather, it comes forth firstly from the Invoked, and secondly from the invoker (awwal min al-madhkur wa thanin min al-dhakir)."

It is thus God Himself who performs the invocation, first and foremost; man's invocation is but a shadow, a reflection, a consequence of this divine invocation. The many-sidedness of the Imam's brief sentence opens onto a number of fruitful meditations upon the meaning, the origin, the end, and the very substance of the dhikr. Among these stands the Quranic verse cited earlier: "Remember Me, I will remember you."

This perception of the reality of the invocation reveals its spiritual power more clearly. The reality that the term dhikr denotes is no longer confined within the boundaries of the individual, still less definable by his verbal or mental activity. What dhikr means in its primary, metaphysical sense opens out into the realm of engendering: it implies the function of bringing things into existence, the outpouring of Being. The creative Word that God utters, "Be! (kun)," which brings the possibilities of being into existence, can be seen in this light as a form of dhikr: God utters the "name" of the possible thing, "calling" it out of nonexistence into existence. This passage from nothingness into being is what renders the possible thing "remembered," madhkur. Here again, the Imam's sentence can be read both as a commentary on a Quranic verse and as an anticipation of the highly elaborate cosmogony associated with Ibn al-Arabi.

The Quran poses the following question:

> هَلْ أَتَىٰ عَلَى ٱلْإِنسَنِ حِينٌۭ مِّنَ ٱلدَّهْرِ لَمْ يَكُن شَيْـًۭٔا مَّذْكُورًا

"Hath there come upon man any moment in time when he was not a thing remembered?" (Al-Insan: 1). Man's being a "thing remembered" here amounts to his coming into being, and his nothingness amounts to his being "not remembered" — that is, not yet called forth from the abyss of pre-existential hiddenness into the light of manifest existence. In this pre-existential state, the human being is, according to Ibn al-Arabi, "known," malum, but only by God. He refers to this verse in the following lines of al-Futuhat al-makkiyya (The Meccan Illuminations):

God said of Himself that He is al-Badi, the Originator, that is, He created that which had no like or exemplar in all the degrees of being, for He knows, by His all-encompassing nature, every single thing that enters into the degrees of being. Therefore He says, as regards the creation of man, that he "was not a thing remembered," for His is the remembrance, so a thing has being in the remembrance of the one who remembers it.

God's "remembrance" of man thus precedes man's remembrance of God — in the order of being, not merely in time. This yields another reading of the verse cited earlier: "and the remembrance of God is greater," infinitely greater, that is, than man's remembrance of Him. The true being of man resides in the "remembrance" God has of him. Man's devotion to God is voiced through the utterance of the divine Name, while God's creation of man is voiced through His utterance of the creative Word. In Ibn al-Arabi's cosmogony, the utterance of this Word is not simply an event in time; that Word never ceases to reverberate, furnishing each human soul with its divine ground, support, or foundation, its mustanad ilahi. This Word "breathes" existence upon the immutable entity, al-ayn al-thabita, which, though it has no existence in itself, determines the contours, form, and configuration of the being that will be manifested through it — just as a prism refracts the light that passes through it, or as water takes on the color of its cup, an image used by Junayd and often quoted by Ibn al-Arabi.

The hadith al-haqiqa, attributed to Ali (ع), contains the essence of the Akbari doctrine of the tajalli of the unique Light of God upon the differentiated "receptacles," the loci of manifestation for that Light. The Imam's disciple Kumayl asks him about the meaning of al-haqiqa. The response includes the following affirmations:

Al-haqiqa is the unveiling of the splendors of Majesty, without any allusion; the effacement of that which is erroneously imagined (al-mawham), together with the clarity of that which is truly known (al-malum); the attraction of absolute unicity to the quality of تَوْحِيد; a light dawning from the morn of eternity, its traces shimmering on the temples of تَوْحِيد.

Al-Kashani comments on this last image: "That is, the manifestation of the Light of the one Essence upon the loci of manifestation of the qualities of the Real, the Essence, these being the loci of the entities of existents (ayan al-mawjudat), which the Imam calls 'temples of تَوْحِيد,' that is, the forms of the divine Names in the station of تَوْحِيد." This divine support of each individual soul, the mustanad ilahi, is therefore its particular Lord, the divine Name that engenders and sustains it. The soul is only the locus of manifestation, the mazhar, where God discloses Himself in a particular and unique mode.

The Futuhat al-makkiyya opens with a khutba whose very first words are these: "Praise be to Him who engendered things from nothing, then negated it; and established their being through the orientation toward them of His Words." This orientation (tawajjuh) of the divine Word toward the possible thing brings the Word's properties into manifestation according to how much of them that possible thing can receive; the possibility is then breathed into existence and becomes like a body whose heart is the divine Name that gives it existence: "every slave has a Name which is its Lord; so it becomes the 'body' of that Name, which is its 'heart.'"

The analogy recalls the Imam's image of the dhikr as a polish for hearts. The Name revealed from above is answered by a Name that resounds from within. Their reciprocal reflection appears once the rust dulling the surface of the heart is lifted by the polish of the dhikr. What is evoked here is a mode of union between two divine realities, and it calls to mind once more the phrase "light upon light."

Earlier the Imam had spoken of the Names of God "which have filled the foundations of all things," a phrase that anticipates Ibn al-Arabi's understanding of the divine Names as the supports and substrata of all things. Yet a question arises, and its answers draw one closer to the powerful and mysterious efficacy of the dhikr. What is the difference between the divine Names understood as the metaphysical roots of all things and the particular Names of God revealed in the Quran? On one side, the whole of creation is the "Book of God," and every phenomenon is one of the "words" of God. On the other, the revealed text holds specific words and Names, and it is these that are to be recited and invoked. Why?

One clear answer is given in the following verse:

> قُلِ ٱدْعُوا۟ ٱللَّهَ أَوِ ٱدْعُوا۟ ٱلرَّحْمَنَ ۖ أَيًّۭا مَّا تَدْعُوا۟ فَلَهُ ٱلْأَسْمَآءُ ٱلْحُسْنَىٰ ۚ وَلَا تَجْهَرْ بِصَلَاتِكَ وَلَا تُخَافِتْ بِهَا وَٱبْتَغِ بَيْنَ ذَلِكَ سَبِيلًۭا

"Call upon Allah or call upon al-Rahman. By whatever Name you call upon Him, His are the most beautiful Names" (Al-Isra: 110).

The Quran itself commands believers to invoke these specific, revealed Names, and no others. These Names are therefore wholly unlike the names of the idolaters' gods:

> إِنْ هِىَ إِلَّآ أَسْمَآءٌۭ سَمَّيْتُمُوهَآ أَنتُمْ وَآبَآؤُكُم مَّآ أَنزَلَ ٱللَّهُ بِهَا مِن سُلْطَنٍ ۚ إِن يَتَّبِعُونَ إِلَّا ٱلظَّنَّ وَمَا تَهْوَى ٱلْأَنفُسُ ۖ وَلَقَدْ جَآءَهُم مِّن رَّبِّهِمُ ٱلْهُدَىٰٓ

"They are but names that ye have named, ye and your fathers, for which God hath revealed no authority" (An-Najm: 23).

The names of idols resemble the conventional names of phenomena: they are merely arbitrary labels or signs, bearing no intrinsic relationship to the phenomena themselves, still less to the principles underlying those phenomena. The Names of God, revealed by God, are of another order. They are penetrated by the very substance of the reality they denote. For this reason they possess "authority" (sultan), and by virtue of that authority they have the power to transform and not merely to inform. They are, in other words, charged with a theurgic power, the capacity to render present to mind and heart the immanent reality of God.

God's presence cannot be taken away, since nothing that exists can lie "outside" His all-encompassing presence; yet that presence can still become veiled, at least for the subject. The heart can be veiled from God by the "rust" of forgetfulness that covers it, so that the divine Reality comes to be hidden by the very phenomena that manifest it. The Imam raises the question of how it is "by things" that the Creator manifests Himself, and yet also "by things" that He is hidden from sight.

This being so, what is needed to open oneself to the divine presence within and beyond all things is twofold: a polishing of one's own "within" through the dhikr, and an unveiling or revelation from that "beyond." This latter disclosure can be understood as a direct revelation of God within that indirect revelation of Him which is the whole of the created cosmos, a cosmos that at once reveals and veils the divine Reality. Even if, from one point of view, the two revelations, cosmic and scriptural, are both defined as manifestations of God, from another point of view the scriptural revelation carries a spiritual power that makes it incomparably more revealing than the created cosmos. In terms of spiritual method there is no common measure between the cosmos as manifested divine "Word" and Allah as a revealed Name of God. On one side there is differentiated manifestation; on the other, a unique revelation. The divine Name may also be called the quintessential manifestation, at once summing up the entire realm of manifestation and transcending it.

The difference between cosmic manifestation and scriptural revelation may be seen, in one respect, as analogous to that between shadow and light. Revelation is like a projection of the pure Light of God, and is therefore of one substance with the source from which it emanates, whereas manifestation is like the shadow cast by that light. Both are projections of one and the same reality, yet this does not lessen the incommensurability between light and darkness. In the words of Ibn al-Arabi, "The very Being of the Real is a verified Light, the very existence of creation is a shadow following after."

There is a remarkable supplication attributed to Ali (ع), the Dua al-Mashlul, the "supplication of the lame man." It consists of one hundred and twenty-two verses containing a multitude of divine Names, attributes, and titles. Toward the end of the supplication one reads a verse that can be taken as the key to the entire text: "I ask Thee by every Name with which Thou hast named Thyself." It is the fact that God "names Himself" that gives His Names their theurgic power, their sacramental substance, their mystical efficacy. It is the revealed substance of the Names that accounts for these distinctive properties and makes them incomparable to any other names. This is why, as Sufi texts so commonly assert, the Name is the Named (al-ism huwal-musamma).

These reflections on the ontological relationship between the Names of God and the nature of being illuminate certain aspects of the dhikr as a mystical practice, yet they still fall short of accounting for that ontological leap of consciousness, that extinction of self-awareness, to which the Imam refers in the words, "you will not remember Him according to the true reality of the remembrance until you forget your own soul in your remembrance." The question then follows: why and how does the dhikr produce this extinction of consciousness, this effacement of individual being, especially when the function of dhikr appears to be precisely the opposite, namely, to actualize or precipitate a heightened mode of consciousness and to deepen one's quality of being?

## 13. Extinction and the Name: The Death of the Saint

Extinction and the Name: The Death of the Saint

The first response to that question begins with a limit. On his own, the individual cannot leap from remembrance to forgetfulness. A conscious person concentrating on the act of invocation cannot make himself unconscious of himself in the course of that invocation. The Imam's phrasing is exact: the soul is to be forgotten 'in' the remembrance. Any effort to will oneself into unconsciousness comes from a conscious will, and so deepens self-consciousness rather than erasing it. The point, then, is not to force oneself to forget oneself while invoking God. It is rather to concentrate on the Name so completely that no room remains in one's consciousness, no 'space', mental or existential, for anything but the Named, the reality being invoked. A first degree of self-effacement thus follows naturally from total concentration.

The final fulfillment of self-effacement in fana, however, comes as a divine grace, as something beyond all individual effort — even though the act of invocation is understood to draw down that grace, or to remove from the heart the 'rust' that hides from a person his own nothingness before the sole reality of God, 'that which has no second'. An esoteric reading of the Quranic words sums up this part of the response: ... thou wilt not forget, unless God willeth it so (Al-A'la: 6-7). Al-Kalabadhi, author of one of the most important early manuals of Sufism, opens his chapter on dhikr with words that echo the Imam's: 'The haqiqa of the dhikr is this: that you forget everything other than the madhkur (the Invoked) in the dhikr. For He says: "So remember thy Lord when thou hast forgotten" (Al-Kahf: 24). That is, when you have forgotten what is not God, then you have remembered God.'

The second response turns on the principle of inverse analogy. Human invocation resembles divine invocation, being a kind of reflection of it; and like any reflected image, it both shares in what it reflects and inverts it. In sharing, the human act of invocation brings the invoker a fuller consciousness and a fuller share in being. In inverting, it reaches its fulfillment in what looks like the very opposite: the effacement of the individual in the object invoked. Yet this only seems to oppose the deepening of consciousness and being. In truth it fulfills that same process, though its last stage takes a sudden leap — the negation of the very limits that make an individual an individual. The individual is limited, and limitation is itself a negation; so to negate this negation is pure affirmation: the affirmation of unconditional, supra-individual being. On the plane of the self, this negation of negation reflects that negation of nothingness expressed in Ibn al-Arabi's line, 'Praise be to Him who engendered things from nothing, then negated it.'

Here the Imam's enigmatic saying returns: 'I am the death of the dead.' It recalls the words Rumi places in the Imam's mouth in these lines of his Mathnawi:

He said, 'I am wielding the sword for God's sake, I am the servant of God, I am not under the command of the body.

I am the Lion of God, I am not the lion of my passion: my deed bears witness to my religion.

In war I am (manifesting the truth of) thou didst not throw when thou threwest: I am (but) as the sword, and the wielder is the (divine) Sun.

I have removed the baggage of self out of the way, I have deemed (what is) other than God to be non-existence.

I am a shadow, the Sun is my lord; I am the chamberlain, I am not the curtain (which prevents approach) to Him.

I am filled with the pearls of union, like a (jewelled) sword: in battle I make (men) living, not slain.

The cosmic unfolding of creation, the movement from God to man, requires the negation of that relative nothingness which teems with the possibilities of being. The realization of God by man requires the negation of that same 'nothingness' once breathed into being by God. Ibn al-Arabi's treatise Extinction in Contemplation names what is at stake: 'the extinction of that which never was ... and the subsistence of that which never ceased to be.' The Quranic verses clearly implied are these: 'Everything thereon perisheth (fan), and there subsisteth (yabqa) only the Face of thy Lord, possessor of glory and bounty' (Ar-Rahman: 26-27).

Yet all things, insofar as they exist, have two aspects or 'faces': the divine aspect, which is the thing's true being, and the created aspect, which is 'nothing'. The 'Face' of the Lord that alone subsists is therefore not only the divine Reality beyond all things, but also that same reality within all things. This is the sense of al-Ghazali's esoteric commentary on the Quranic verse, 'Everything perisheth except His Face' (Al-Qasas: 88). Al-Ghazali comments:

[it is] not that it perisheth at some particular moment, but rather it is forever perishing, since it cannot be conceived except as perishing. For each thing other than God is, when considered in and by itself, pure nothingness; and if considered from the face (wajh) to which existence flows to it from the Prime Reality, it is viewed as existing, but not in itself, solely from the face which accompanies Him Who gives it existence. Therefore, the divine face is the sole thing in reality.

He then ties the mystical realization of this oneness to the invocation of God and to the forgetting of the self within that invocation, and so offers a valuable commentary on what this total dissolution of self-consciousness means. The Sufis who verify this oneness through mystical 'taste' (dhawq), he writes, are 'drowned in oneness', so that they become 'dumbfounded':

No capacity remained within them save to recall Allah; yea, not so much as the capacity to recall their own selves ... when this state prevails, it is called in relation to him who experiences it Extinction, nay Extinction of Extinction, for the soul has become extinct to itself, extinct to its own extinction; for it becomes unconscious of itself and unconscious of its own unconsciousness, since were it conscious of its own unconsciousness, it would be conscious of itself.

Relevant here too is the statement al-Qushayri attributes to Abu 'Uthman, which throws light on another side of the unconsciousness realized through invocation: Whoever has not tasted the loneliness of unconsciousness will not find the food of the intimacy of remembrance.' Here, to lose consciousness of oneself is called 'loneliness', while to become fully conscious of God through invocation brings 'intimacy'. True intimacy with the divine Reality comes only by stripping the self of its false 'intimacy' — that self-satisfied, habitual self-centeredness which is the shadow cast by self-awareness. It is this awareness of self that, in the Imam's statement, must be forgotten. Such higher forgetfulness can arise only on the ground of the remembrance of God; for only the source of Being, unconditional Consciousness, and eternal Life can extinguish, eclipse, or efface the relative existence, partial consciousness, and apparent life of the individual. This, the haqiqa of the dhikr, the Imam's own words express with perfect economy: ... forget your own soul in your remembrance.

The state of fana, the highest degree of this 'forgetting' of oneself, is therefore a grace. The individual cannot attain it, however great his effort. Yet this does not mean the invoker abandons effort. On the contrary, as the Sufi tradition amply attests, fana is closely bound to the practice of dhikr, and is indeed its very climax. After this state, or hal, of extinction comes the station, or maqam, of subsistence, baqa — a return of consciousness to the self, a self now transfigured by the knowledge of reality stamped upon the soul in the state of fana. For only in the state of fana is the ultimate 'vision' of absolute oneness attained, a vision that leaves no room for the ontological duality that always accompanies individuality. To posit the 'individual' is to posit its correlate, the Lord, and so to affirm duality.

Al-Kashani makes this point in his commentary on the Quranic verse, 'Remember Him as He hath guided you' (Al-Baqarah: 198), where he traces the guided ascent through the degrees of the dhikr. The first is the dhikr of the tongue, which corresponds to the soul (al-nafs); then that of the heart (al-qalb), corresponding to the divine acts, those that manifest His graces; then that of the 'secret' (al-sirr), corresponding to the vision of the divine acts together with 'the unveiling of the sciences of the manifestation of the divine qualities'; then that of the spirit (al-ruh), corresponding to 'the contemplation of the lights of the manifestations of the divine qualities, with an apprehension of the light of the Essence'; then the dhikr of 'the hidden' (al-khafi), which is 'the contemplation of the beauty of the Essence along with the subsistence of duality'; and finally the dhikr of the Essence, 'which is essential vision, attained through the removal of duality.'

The same point emerges from the counsel of Ibn 'Ataillah, which shows how the dhikr makes a person receptive to the divine presence, and to the grace by which alone the individual can truly forget' himself — that is, become 'unconscious of his own unconsciousness', as al-Ghazali put it:

Do not abandon the invocation because you do not feel the Presence of God therein. For your forgetfulness of the invocation of Him is worse than your forgetfulness in the invocation of Him. Perhaps He will take you from an invocation with forgetfulness (ghafla) to one with vigilance (yaqaza), and from one with vigilance to one with the Presence of God (hudur), and from one with the Presence of God to one wherein everything but the Invoked (al-Madhkur) is absent. 'And that is not difficult for God.'

In his commentary on the Hikam, Ibn 'Ajiba adds: So, in the measure that one is extinguished in the Name, one is extinguished in the Essence.' This follows from the principle that the Name is the Named. In another treatise, a commentary on a poem by Shushtari on the divine Name Allah, he states this cardinal tenet of the Sufi invocatory discipline more plainly. Referring to the letters of which Allah is composed, the poem's first line reads: 'The alif before two lams, then a ha': coolness of the eyes. Ibn 'Ajiba comments: The "coolness of the eyes", this is joy, for tears of joy, as one knows by experience, are cool. The author means this: this Name is the joy of my heart, its happiness, its beatitude.' He then cites some lines from al-Hallaj on the letters of the Name Allah, adds a verse of his own, and follows with this comment: 'The Name is not other than the Named, for joy cannot come from anything but the essence of a thing, not from the name as such.'

This sums up the doctrine that the Name and the Named are identical, the rationale for the Sufi practice of dhikruLlah. Yet here a problem arises. However well the Imam's pronouncements on the dhikr may agree with these later Sufi statements of the underlying principles, this particular stress on the identity of Name and Named appears, on the surface, to conflict with the many sayings of the Shii Imams that deny the Name is the Named. Imam Jafar al-Sadiq, for instance, is reported as saying:

The Name of Allah is other than Him. For everything to which the name of a thing is applicable is a creation, hence other than God. For whatever the tongue expresses, or is worked on by the hand, is a creation ... everything described is created, and the Creator of things cannot be described within the limits of a named object.

Allah has ninety-nine Names. If the Name were the Named, then every one of the Names would be a god. But Allah is the one meaning, or spiritual reality, mana, indicated by the Names, and all of them are other than Him.

This apparent contradiction can be resolved in two ways. First, one must attend carefully to what the word 'Name' means in each of the two contexts: the context of doctrinal distinction, in which the Absolute is rigorously distinguished from the relative and the Essence from all forms; and the context of methodic concentration, in which the Absolute is understood to be the reality of the very form through which it reveals itself. One can then turn to one of the most esoteric aspects of the Shii Imams' understanding of themselves: their references to their own selves as the 'most beautiful Names' (al-asma al-husna).

As regards the meaning of 'Name', Ibn al-Arabi offers a precise definition: [T]he Names have two connotations; the first connotation is God Himself who is what is named, the second that by which one Name is distinguished from another ... As being essentially the other, the Name is the Reality, while as being not the other, it is the imagined Reality. On the one hand, each Name is identified with every other Name by virtue of its essential identity with the Named, the one Essence that is the source of all the Names. On the other hand, each Name has its own particular meaning and quality, the relationship through which the one Reality engages the created cosmos. In this second respect, each Name is distinguished from the others; and distinction implies limitation, hence relativity, and in the end a kind of illusion before the one and only Reality. It is in this sense that the Name takes on the character of 'imagined Reality'. For Ibn al-Arabi, the Names have no separate ontological entities of their own; they all revert to the one and only Reality, that of the divine Essence. This dissolves the problem, raised in Imam Jafar's saying, of different Names implying different 'gods'.

The lexicon of Sufi terms Lataif al-ilam fi isharat ahl al-ilham (Subtle Disclosures in the Allusions of the Folk of Inspiration), attributed to al-Kashani, gives a definition of the divine Names that also helps to tell these two connotations apart. Under the term 'Names of the Divinity', the text reads:

These are, in the terminology of the Sufis, expressive of the manifest aspect of being, in respect of its determination within a specific meaning. For every divine Name is the manifest aspect of being, which, in turn, is identical to the Essence, but not as the Essence is in itself; rather, in respect of its specification and determination within a particular meaning, or what one might call a 'quality'. For example, the Name al-Hayy ('The Living') is a Name of manifest, determined being, but in respect of its specification and determination as a particular meaning, it is Life. So in regard to being itself, al-Hayy is identical to the Essence, while in regard to its determination as a particular meaning, it is other than the Essence. If you have understood what we have said, you will know what is meant by the saying: the Name is not identical to the Essence, the Named, neither is it other than It.

Between the Name and the Named there is a distinction in name and in thought, and by this measure the Name is not the Named. Yet at the same time there is a spiritual assimilation between them, by which the Name is the Named. A further distinction lies beyond this one: the verbal form of the Name must be told apart from the Name as an attribute of the Essence. Taken in this second sense, the Name is nothing other than the divine attribute it designates, and that attribute is in turn identical to the Essence. Taken in the first sense, there is the 'name' of this Name, which is no more than a verbal form. Qaysari, another key commentator on Ibn al-Arabi, draws out this distinction in the introduction to his commentary on the Fusus al-hikam.

The Essence, taken together with a determined quality and in relation to one of Its theophanies, is named by a Name. Thus 'The Compassionate' has an essence, which is compassion, and 'The All-Conquering' has an essence, which is all-conquering power. These spoken Names are Names of Names, and from this the saying may be understood: the Name is identical to the Named.

In his monumental commentary on Qaysari's introduction, Jalal al-Din Ashtiyani, a contemporary authority on Shii 'Irfan, makes an important observation after citing this passage. It returns the discussion to the principle of extinction through the invocation of the Name, and to the second point raised earlier — that the Imams themselves constitute the most beautiful Names'. Ashtiyani writes that the saints often speak of the Names as one with the Named because they are implicitly pointing to the referent (misdaq), which is the sifa, the divine attribute or quality: 'The sifat are in one respect the specifications of the Essence, while in the station of unicity they are annihilated in the Essence, and are thus identical to the Essence.'

This metaphysical account of how the Name, the sifa, and the Essence stand to one another mirrors — objectively and universally — the path that the soul traces subjectively and mystically when it is extinguished in and by the invocation of the Name. This extinction is what fana (فنیٰ) means: the perishing of egoistic consciousness before the Absolute. The sifat are 'annihilated' in the Essence, with which they are therefore absolutely and unconditionally one; in the same way, the soul extinguished by the Name attains, at one and the same moment, its total nullification and its ultimate realization — though no longer as an individual soul. This 'extinction' on the plane of the individual soul amounts to 'realization' on the plane of transcendence only by virtue of the divine theophany, the particular divine Name, the 'heart' that constitutes the soul's true being, its mustanad ilahi. Here the deepest reality of تَوْحِيد is revealed. The individual no longer affirms the notion that 'there is no god but God', for the individual has been extinguished by the reality that 'there is no reality but the one and only Reality'. God is 'that which has no second'. Just as 'No one has truly said Allah but Allah', so too no one realizes God but God. God alone comes to 'know' or 'be' Himself, and this is one aspect of the purpose of creation, as the hadith qudsi indicates: 'I was a hidden treasure and I loved to be known.'

Seen in this light, the saints (al-awliya) are perfectly self-realized theophanies, wholly effaced in that of which they are the theophanic manifestations; and this very effacement in the divine Reality is the ultimate source of their knowledge of it. It applies all the more to the Prophet himself, the prototype of all the saints and the 'perfect man', al-insan al-kamil. On this point Sabziwari, in his commentary on the divine Names, cites the saying of Ali (ع), 'We are the most beautiful Names': 'There is no doubt that they are His most exalted indicators and His greatest signs (ala imihil-uzma wa ayatihil-kubra), as the Prophet said, God bless him and his family, "Whoso sees me has seen the Truth (al-Haqq)." He then equates the spiritual station, or inner reality, of the Imams with that of the divine Names and attributes, stressing that the divine Essence transcends all Names and attributes and is forever ineffable, known only to itself. The Prophet, the Imam, the saint, the perfect man' thus manifest those aspects of the divine nature that can be perceived on the human plane. The more the divine Essence is exalted in transcendence above all Names and attributes, the more brilliantly those Names and attributes are reflected in the mirror that the 'perfect man' constitutes. The unknowable transcendence of the hidden 'treasure' is, to some degree, made up for by the faithful reflection of its communicable aspects in the 'friend of God'.

To identify the Imam with the divine Name or attribute has implications that repay reflection, for in one respect such an identification would appear to be shirk — the 'association' of a creature, however exalted, with the transcendent Reality. The same would seem to apply to the ecstatic utterances (shathiyyat) of many of the most celebrated Sufis, such as the anal-Haqq ('I am the Real') of al-Hallaj and the subhani ('Glory to me') of Bayazid. Yet once one takes up a viewpoint shaped by the Imam's own conception of the divine attributes, a very different picture emerges — one that, far from being tainted by shirk, in fact reaches the most profound application of تَوْحِيد. The first sermon of the Nahj al-balagha contains the following remarkable and highly important statements on the attributes of God:

The perfection of purification (ikhlas) is to divest Him of all attributes (sifat) because of the testimony of every attribute that it is other than the object of attribution, and because of the testimony of every such object that it is other than the attribute. So whoever ascribes an attribute to God, glorified be He, has conjoined Him with something else; and whoever so conjoins Him has made Him twofold; and whoever makes Him twofold has fragmented Him; and whoever thus fragments Him is ignorant of Him.

When the Imam says that pure devotion to God requires divesting God of all attributes, what he negates is not the divine attributes as such. He negates them only as separate, autonomous, or super-added realities — as things conceived to be substantially 'other than' the Essence of God, or ontologically distinct from it. The reality of God's attributes is plainly affirmed in an earlier phrase of the same sermon: 'He whose attribute has no binding limitation (alladhi laysa li-sifatihi hadd mahdud).' What is denied is the possibility of confining the reality of any divine attribute within the bounds of human conception and language. No divine attribute can be distinct from the divine nature; that nature is infinite; and so every attribute is of infinite scope as well, lying beyond all the limitations that formal conception and linguistic definition presuppose.

In one respect, then, the attribute or Name is to be affirmed, and in another it is to be negated: affirmed as not other than the Essence, negated insofar as it is conceived in itself, on its own account. This metaphysical reading of how the attribute or Name stands to the Essence can also be applied, mystically, to how the individual soul stands to the divine Reality. On its own account the individual is nothing; but in respect of the divine substrate — the divine Name, the mustanad ilahi that constitutes its essential reality — it is not other than the unique Real. According to Ibn al-Arabi, 'The final end and ultimate return of the gnostics is that the Real is identical with them, while they do not exist.' This perfectly expresses the dual aspect of the realized sage's soul: insofar as he is aware that 'he does not exist', he knows that the Real is his true identity. However paradoxical it may seem, the mystic's realization of absolute Reality goes hand in hand with the realization of his own non-existence. Ibn al-Arabi expresses these two aspects of one and the same realization elliptically. He describes the climax of his spiritual ascent, his miraj, recounting that God removed from him his 'contingent dimension (imkan), resulting in the attainment of the inner realities of all the Names'. He continues:

Thus I attained in this nocturnal journey the inner realities (maani) of all the Names, and I saw them returning to One Subject (musamma) and One Entity ('ayn): that Subject was what I witnessed and that Entity was my Being. For my voyage was only in myself and pointed to myself, and through this I came to know that I was a pure servant' without a trace of lordship in me at all.

This passage helps to explain so many of the ecstatic utterances of the Sufis and the apparently self-deifying statements of the Imams. Their personal servitude to God is not denied. On the contrary, it is fully realized through the knowledge of their true identity, for that identity as the Named is attained only when their individuality is effaced. Like the divine attributes, they are mustahlak, annihilated in the divine Essence, so that no individualistic appropriation of the glory of God can be in question. When the Imam appears to speak of his own greatness, this is nothing but the greatness of God expressed through him as a mazhar, a locus for the theophanic manifestation of the divine — and expressed precisely in the measure of his own effacement in that greatness.

The same point is reinforced by what 'Attar recounts about Imam Jafar al-Sadiq in his Tadhkiratul-awliya'. When someone accused him of being proud, the Imam replied: 'I am not proud, for pride pertains to the divine greatness. Since I have gone beyond the pride of self, His pride has come and taken the place of my own pride. With pride of self present, there can be no divine greatness, but through the divine greatness, pride will be present.' The divine qualities shine with greater brilliance, not lesser, through the prism of a soul made transparent to the divine Reality — a transparency that comes from the dissolution of all the clouds of egoism and passion.

A holy utterance bears on this: 'My slave draws near to Me through nothing I love more than that which I have made obligatory for him. My slave never ceases to draw near to Me through supererogatory acts (nawafil) until I love him. And when I love him, I am his hearing by which he hears, his sight by which he sees, his hand by which he grasps, and his foot by which he walks.'

In the light of all this, the paradox contained in this divine utterance can now be resolved. On the one hand, the thing most beloved to God appears to be the obligatory rites. On the other, it is the supererogatory rites that lead the slave to draw near to God until He loves him — and this love seems the greater, since it issues in the divine penetration of the human faculties, the unitive realization proper to تَوْحِيد. The paradox resolves, however, once one sees that God's 'love' for the obligatory rites is a love directed toward something apart from Himself, whereas His love for the 'slave' — the pure slave effaced in his Lord — is infinitely greater, since it partakes of God's love for His own Self-manifestation, and so for Himself. In the beginning God 'loved to be known', and so He created the world. In 'knowing' Himself through the effaced slave, the apparent 'other', His love to be known is realized through the saint whose greatest glory is precisely his own effacement, poverty, and emptiness of self.

It is in this sense that the following statement of the Imam is to be understood: 'Down from me torrents do pour; up to me no bird can soar.' Torrents flow down from him because — and in the measure that — he is extinguished in the source of the torrent. The image recalls the Quranic description of the 'slaves of God' who, unlike the 'righteous', drink directly from the celestial spring of Kafur, identified ultimately with the divine Essence: 'Truly, the righteous shall drink from a cup flavoured with Kafur, a spring wherefrom the slaves of God drink, making it gush forth abundantly' (Al-Insan: 5-6). These slaves make the spring flow more abundantly because, as al-Kashani explains, 'they are themselves the sources of this spring; there is no duality or otherness. Were it otherwise, it would not be the fountain of Kafur, because of the darkness of the veil of egoity (ana iyya) and duality.'

The final degree of the dhikr, as al-Kashani defined it, is the dhikr of the Essence, 'which is essential vision, attained through the removal of duality'. The dhikr 'polishes' away not only the impurities of the heart and mind but, in the end, through the grace of the madhkur, every taint of duality — and, above all, that most fundamental postulate of duality, the individual as such. It removes all subtle or hidden shirk, the 'partners' in being that are non-existent before the One 'that has no second', and it establishes the spiritual principle of تَوْحِيد, so that the heart, transfigured by the knowledge of the sole Reality, comes to perceive the light of that Reality reflected in all the mirrors of creation. To realize — to 'make real' in the fullest sense — this one and only Reality is, by the same stroke, to realize the unreality, or merely apparent reality, of everything else.

At this level of realization a measure of perplexity inevitably accompanies the revelation of the divine Reality on every side. Because the divine Essence is raised beyond all comprehension, it becomes all the more inescapable that creaturely consciousness must be utterly annihilated if that Essence is to be 'realized', if gnosis of it is to be attained. On the one hand, one 'knows' God by knowing that His Essence is unknowable, utterly other, utterly transcendent — the essence of the Creator lying beyond the veil that creation itself constitutes. On the other hand, the very distinction between creature and Creator is effaced once the creature has 'forgotten' itself — not merely as a notion but existentially, in a state of genuine contemplative extinction. Then it is no longer a matter of one form of self-consciousness replacing another, but of the 'self' being taken out of the equation altogether, leaving only pure consciousness as such — which cannot 'belong' to any entity without being reduced to the relativity proper to that entity. Thus consciousness of the Absolute, that is, 'being conscious of the Absolute', is transmuted into absolute consciousness. The Real is 'realized' not by another but through another, and hence the designation al-arif biLlah, literally 'the knower through God' rather than simply 'the knower of God'. The Imam says, 'Know God through God.' Knowledge of God at this deepest level is the Real knowing itself through the creature; and to say 'through' implies an unimpeded passage, the creature being qualified by perfect faqr — poverty, self-effacement, and, ultimately, extinction.

Realization through remembrance, however, means more than 'forgetting' everything in existence except God. It means discovering that in reality there is nothing in being but God. In the Imam's image of the 'polish' worked by remembrance, the heart, once blind and deaf, is made able to 'see' and to 'hear'. It sees the Face of God wherever it looks, and it hears the cosmic hymn of remembrance and praise that the whole of creation sings, for every creature that exists proclaims the infinite creativity of its Creator. As the Quran says:

> وَلِلَّهِ ٱلْمَشْرِقُ وَٱلْمَغْرِبُ ۚ فَأَيْنَمَا تُوَلُّوا۟ فَثَمَّ وَجْهُ ٱللَّهِ ۚ إِنَّ ٱللَّهَ وَسِعٌ عَلِيمٌۭ

'Wherever ye turn, there is the Face of God' (Al-Baqarah: 115).

> أَلَمْ تَرَ أَنَّ ٱللَّهَ يُسَبِّحُ لَهُۥ مَن فِى ٱلسَّمَوَتِ وَٱلْأَرْضِ وَٱلطَّيْرُ صَٓفَّتٍۢ ۖ كُلٌّۭ قَدْ عَلِمَ صَلَاتَهُۥ وَتَسْبِيحَهُۥ ۗ وَٱللَّهُ عَلِيمٌۢ بِمَا يَفْعَلُونَ

'Do you not see that God is the one who is glorified by everything in the heavens and the earth, and the birds with wings outstretched; each one indeed knows its prayer and its glorification, and God is All-Aware of what they do' (An-Nur: 41).

A final reflection concerns a passage from the Imam's famous and much-disputed Khutbat al-bayan, 'The Sermon of Clarifying Expression':

I am the Sign of the All-Powerful. I am the gnosis of the mysteries. I am the Threshold of Thresholds. I am the companion of the radiance of the divine Majesty. I am the First and the Last, the Manifest and the Hidden. I am the Face of God. I am the mirror of God, the supreme Pen, the Tabula secreta. I am he who in the Gospel is called Elijah. I am he who is in possession of the secret of God's Messenger.

This extraordinary declaration of the Imam's inmost reality may seem shocking to the exoteric mind, and it is all too easily misread as a blasphemous self-deification of the kind attributed to Hallaj. One key unlocks its correct meaning: the statement 'I am the gnosis of the mysteries.' Once that is grasped, every other statement falls naturally into place. For the Imam is not identifying himself with God, but with the knowledge of God — and not with his own knowledge of God, for that would still be a relative knowledge belonging to himself as a relative being. Only knowledge itself, the knowledge God has of Himself, and not the knowledge a human being has of God, can be fully identified with its object. It is only at this transcendent level that the distinctions between knowledge, knower, and known dissolve, just as at that level the distinction between knowledge and being ceases to operate. The knower is thus the known, once the knowledge in question is that consciousness which the divine Reality has of itself; and individual consciousness gains access to this knowledge only on the condition that every trace of individual, creaturely selfhood is utterly extinguished. To 'remember' God, one must have 'forgotten' oneself. And to 'be' the knowledge of God is, by a kind of metaphysical reverberation, to 'be' everything that God is, for the consciousness that is wholly one with the Absolute is nothing other than 'the radiance of the divine Majesty'. Insofar as this consciousness is one with 'the gnosis of the mysteries' — and these mysteries (asrar) are all rooted in the ultimate mystery (al-sirr), the one and only Essence of God — it is of necessity one with all the divine attributes. In this sense the Imam can proclaim: 'I am the First and the Last, the Manifest and the Hidden.'

The same holds for the claim to be 'the Face of God' (wajh Allah). It becomes intelligible only once one grasps that the Imam describes himself as the 'mirror' of God. A mirror loses its identity as a mirror when attention is fixed entirely on the object reflected within it, so that the mirror's own surface becomes, as it were, invisible and only the object is seen. The reverse is also true: when the mirror itself is the object of attention, the face reflected within it can no longer be seen. The mirror is thus identified with what it reflects — which in a certain sense is 'in' the mirror — without the mirror ceasing to be a mirror, and without the Face ceasing to be absolutely independent of it. The 'veil' here is the mirror itself. Attending to it as a mirror prevents one from seeing the object it reflects; but once attention rests wholly on the image the mirror contains, the mirror ceases to be a veil and instead reveals the object itself. Within this symbolism, forgetting the mirror answers to the effacement of the self, and fixing on the image within it answers to the remembrance of God.

Yet this opens onto a further paradox: the deepest effacement of individuality goes hand in hand with the most perfect expression of humanity. The 'friends of God', who know themselves to be nothing before the Absolute, are precisely the ones who most faithfully reflect the divine Names and qualities, which belong not to them but to God. This is why the great saints and sages are also exemplars of virtue, whose true foundations are the 'most beautiful Names' and attributes of God. Those divine attributes shine through them most clearly because the mirrors of their hearts have been polished by the remembrance of God, and the most stubborn stains — those left by egotism — have been removed. The creativity of the saint is raised to its highest pitch precisely because the divine qualities reflected by the heart are no longer impeded, on the inward side, by the individualistic coagulations generated by egotism, nor constrained, on the outward side, by the cosmic coagulations of this lower world. For such saints are those whose 'hearts are in the Gardens [of Paradise] while their bodies are at work.' Their hearts, bound and attached to that divine realm of the abodes and stations of paradise, reaching all the way to the divine presence, are already 'within' that transcendent realm; they already live more in that spiritual world than in this earthly one. And so, though they labor in this world with their bodies, their outward actions veil their inward transcendence of the higher realities: they act, but they are not bound by the fruits of their action.

Such souls have not merely understood this principle; they have been seized by it, existentially, at the very root of their being. It is the principle voiced in the Quranic verse:

> وَمَا هَذِهِ ٱلْحَيَوٰةُ ٱلدُّنْيَآ إِلَّا لَهْوٌۭ وَلَعِبٌۭ ۚ وَإِنَّ ٱلدَّارَ ٱلْآخِرَةَ لَهِىَ ٱلْحَيَوَانُ ۚ لَوْ كَانُوا۟ يَعْلَمُونَ

"And the life of this world is naught but sport and play; and truly, the abode of the Hereafter, that is true life" (Al-'Ankabut: 64).

Their instincts are therefore always attuned to that higher reality. When Ali (ع) was struck by the poisoned sword of Ibn Muljam, the cry that broke from him was not one of pain but of triumph: "I have triumphed, by the Lord of the Kaba!" At the very moment of death there was in him nothing but the remembrance of God, and the Imam passed away, it is said, with his tongue still repeating the words la ilaha illaLlah. His death was thus consummated exactly as his life had been lived, in keeping with the wisdom of the Prophet, who, when asked which of all acts is the most meritorious, replied: "That you die while your tongue is moistened with the invocation of God."

All the rites and practices of the chivalric orders of Islam were traced, without exception, back to the Prophet's son-in-law, Ali (ع) ibn Abi Talib, who was thereby established as the supreme source of the virtues of futuwwa, as Muhammad Jafar Mahjub observes in his "Chivalry and Early Persian Sufism." S. H. Nasr writes in a similar vein that Ali (ع), having received the "truth and power" of futuwwa from the Prophet, "became the supreme source of futuwwa in Islam for both Sunnis and Shiites... The personality of Ali, at once sage and knight, contemplative and protector of laborers and craftsmen, has continued to dominate through the centuries over the horizon of futuwwa as it has over much of Sufism." Nasr also draws attention to the organic relationship between futuwwa and the guilds — the asnaf, singular sinf — of craftsmen and artists established throughout the length and breadth of the Muslim world.

It is often said that the Naqshabandi tariqa is the one exception to this rule, tracing its line of descent from the Prophet through the first caliph, Abu Bakr, rather than through Ali (ع). But this affiliation with Abu Bakr belongs to only one of its three principal silsilas. 'Abd al-Majid b. Muhammad al-Khani, himself a Naqshabandi-Khalidi shaykh, calls the first chain (al-silsilat al-ula) the "golden chain," which begins with Ali (ع) and proceeds through the Shii Imams down to Ali (ع) b. Musa, then to Maruf al-Karkhi and the other masters. The second chain (al-silsilatul-thaniya) likewise begins with Ali (ع), proceeding through Hasan al-Basri. Only the third chain, called al-silsilat al-siddiqiyya, proceeds from the Prophet to Abu Bakr (al-siddiq).

<!-- editorial:begin -->
> **Editorial note (tradition-grounded).** 
> The chapter turns repeatedly on "limit" and "limitation" — the individuating boundary whose
> negation opens onto unconditional, supra-individual being. A word on the term may help a modern
> reader: in the wider tradition the Arabic root behind such "limits" means to set an edge or
> boundary, whether a physical limit or a sharpened blade, and from it come hudud, the fixed
> limits God has set, and even hadid, iron, the sharp and hard metal. To bound a thing is to
> define it; so the "limitation" that makes an individual an individual is, at root, its very
> edge. Read against this, the chapter's "negation of negation" effaces not the self's reality but
> that defining boundary — the edge marking off one existent from the sole reality present within
> all things.
<!-- editorial:end -->

## 14. The First Sermon of the Nahj al-Balagha

With that golden chain in view, the sermon that opens نھج البلاغہ takes on its full weight. Maulana Ali (ع) delivered it as a خطبۃ proclaiming the oneness of God — a oneness that does not belong to the category of number. Its method is paradox: the loftier the truth to be revealed, the deeper the paradox shaped to reveal it. By denying that human words and concepts can reach God, the sermon affirms the Reality that no word and no concept can bound. This is the manner of these discourses. The eye cannot see Him, yet the mind cannot deny Him; He is farther than the flights of imagination can reach, yet nearer than the jugular vein. Nowhere is this plainer than in the opening lines.

Praise be to God, الحَمْدُ للهِ الَّذَي لاَ يَبْلُغُ مِدْحَتَهُ القَائِلُونَ — whose praise no speaker can fully deliver. Here the very office of speech, which is to convey a concept, is turned to show that God is so lofty and sublime that words can deliver nothing of Him. Whose graces no one who counts can number, وَلاِ يُحْصِي نَعْمَاءَهُ العَادُّونَ — for counting suits what can be enumerated, and not His bounties. Whose rightful due no striver can render, ولاَ يُؤَدِّي حَقَّهُ الُمجْتَهِدُونَ — for however hard they strive, they can never render Him the praise and gratitude He is owed. He whom far-reaching aspirations cannot reach, الَّذِي لاَ يُدْركُهُ بُعْدُ الهِمَمِ, and whom the profoundest intuitions cannot fathom, وَلاَ يَنَالُهُ غَوْصُ الفِطَنِ. He whose attribute has no fixed limit, no existing description, no appointed time, no extended term, الَّذِي لَيْسَ لِصِفَتِهِ حَدٌّ مَحْدُودٌ، وَلاَ نَعْتٌ مَوْجُودٌ، وَلا وَقْتٌ مَعْدُودٌ. His attribute cannot be confined within a definition, and yet it is affirmed as real — described in the very words of revelation, though no description can capture Him. Through His power He originated all created things; through His mercy He set the comforting winds loose; and He made His swaying earth firm with pillars of rock.

Foremost in religion is the knowledge of Him. The perfection of this knowledge is to believe in Him; the perfection of this belief is to affirm His oneness; and the perfection of this affirmation is to purify one's devotion to Him. This is the perfection of sincerity, أخلاص: to make one's devotion, and one's very conception of God, pure. The perfection of this purification is to strip Him of all attributes, نفی الصفۃ, since every attribute testifies that it is other than the thing it describes, and everything so described testifies that it is other than the attribute. So whoever ascribes an attribute to God, glorified be He, has joined Him to something else; whoever joins Him has made Him twofold; whoever makes Him twofold has fragmented Him; and whoever fragments Him is ignorant of Him. Whoever points to Him confines Him; whoever confines Him counts Him; whoever asks 'in what?' encloses Him; and whoever asks 'upon what?' isolates Him.

The attributes by which He is named in revelation — that He is the All-Compassionate, رحمن, the All-Merciful, رحیم, the Almighty, عزیز — are affirmed as real and yet cannot be defined, bounded, or described. He is merciful, but not with any mercy the mind can picture; powerful, but not like any storm or flood or force yet known. Reason itself sets limits — it draws a boundary, a حد, around whatever it grasps — and by breaking that boundary the sermon opens an awareness beyond the reach of speculative thought, so that in denying the limits of human words it affirms the Absolute that has no limit. This is the very movement of the testimony of faith: لا الہ, 'no deity,' which is the negation, نفی, and الا اللہ, 'except God,' which is the affirmation, اثبات. Each attribute is negated only so far as the limited mind conceives it, and is thereby affirmed as nothing but God Himself: La Rahmana illaLlah, there is no All-Compassionate but God; La Raheema illaLlah, there is no All-Merciful but God. Thus is God glorified above all that the mind would attribute to Him: سُبْحَنَ ٱللَّهِ عَمَّا يَصِفُونَ, 'Glorified is God above all that they attribute to Him!' (Surat al-Muminun, 23:91).

Being, but not by any becoming; existing, but not out of prior non-existence; with every thing, but not by association; other than every thing, but not by separation; acting, but not through movements and instruments; seeing, even when there was nothing of His creation to be seen; alone, even when there was none whose closeness might be sought or whose absence might be missed.

He brought creation forth, originating it from the very first, without pondering any plan or drawing on any past experience, without setting anything in motion or straining Himself in any troubled effort. He gave every thing its span of time and harmonized their differences; He set within them their natural dispositions and impressed their forms upon them; He knew them before they came to be, encompassed their limits and their ends, and knew their makeup and their contours.

Then He, glorified be He, split open the atmospheres, cleaving apart the directions and the highest layers of the air; then He made water flow through them, a current of surging waves that swelled and swirled in overflowing abundance. He mounted it upon a tempestuous wind, a thunderous gale, and set that wind to hold the water back, so that it mastered the violence of the water's flow and kept it within its bounds; the air lay open beneath the wind, while the water above it ran on in abundance.

Then He raised up another wind and made it barren, fixed its course, strengthened its rush, and set it far from its source. He commanded it to stir the swelling waters and to rouse the waves of the sea. The wind churned up the waters as one churns milk in a milkbag, driving upon them in the open air the same violence, turning their front back upon their rear and their stillness into motion, until their torrents surged and their swelling waves threw up foam. Then He lifted that foam into a cleft, open air and a wide expanse, and from it formed the seven heavens: He made the lowest of them a suspended wave and the highest a guarded shelter and a lofty ceiling, with no pillars to hold them up and no rivet to fasten them. Then He adorned the heavens with the ornament of the stars and the brightness of the firmament, and set a shining lamp — the sun — and a glowing moon to follow their course in a revolving sphere, beneath a roof in motion and a firmament in flux. Then He parted the highest heavens and filled them with angels of differing natures: some prostrate and never bowing; others bowing and never standing upright; some drawn up in ranks and never breaking from one another; others glorifying Him and never wearying, whom no sleepiness of the eye overtakes, no wandering of the mind, no weariness of the body, no heedlessness of forgetfulness. Among them are the keepers of His Revelation, the bearers of His Words to His Messengers, and those who come and go with His Decree and His Command. Among them are the guardians of His servants and the wardens of the gates of His Gardens. And among them are those whose feet stand firm on the lowest worlds while their necks rise past the highest heaven, whose sides reach beyond all bounds and whose shoulders match the pillars of the Throne; their eyes are lowered before it, and they wrap themselves beneath it in their wings. Between them and all others stands a barrier: veils of Glory and screens of Might. They do not imagine their Lord through any fashioned form, nor ascribe to Him the attributes of created things; they do not confine Him within places, nor point to Him through likenesses.

Then He, glorified be He, gathered a quantity of dust — some of it hard ground and some soft, some sweet and some salt — and poured water over it until it was pure, then worked moisture into it until it held together. From this He fashioned a form with curves and joints, limbs and parts; He pressed it firm until it held fast, and hardened it until it became sounding clay, and He assigned it a measured span and a known end. Then He breathed into it of His spirit, and it rose up a human being — endowed with mental powers to reflect with, thoughts by which to govern himself, limbs to put to use, instruments at his disposal, and a knowledge by which to tell true from false, one taste and scent from another, one color and kind from another; a constitution compounded of differing hues, matching forms, opposed contraries, and distinct mixtures of heat and cold, moisture and dryness.

Then God, glorified be He, called upon the angels to fulfill what He had charged them with and to comply with His injunction, by prostrating in submission before Adam and humbling themselves before him. So He said, "Prostrate to Adam," and they prostrated — all save Iblis.
